#10600. I bought the knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands, and it’s crazy how good they are [Gallery]

I love swapping out the bands on my Pixel Watch 5 and Fitbit Air, but Google’s official options are not cheap. It doesn’t take long to find a plethora of knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands around the web and, as it turns out, they’re actually really good.

Over the past few generations, I’ve collected a ton of Pixel Watch bands and, at first, that was almost entirely first-party bands. For the first couple of years of Pixel Watch, third-party bands were either non-existent or of terrible quality, but they’ve gotten a lot better.

Last year, I showcased some knock-off Active Sport bands that, for $8, very effectively filled the gap for me while Google’s lineup was lacking some colors I wanted.

Since then, I’ve tried others. The most recent batch is a 6-pack of Pixel Watch bands that are copies of Google’s standard “Active” band. They’re made from silicone, not the fluorelastomer from past generations, nor the “Hydrogenated Nitrile Butadiene Rubber (HNBR)” used on Pixel Watch 5. But, in action, it gets the job done.

The bands sit somewhere between Google’s small and large sizes and slot into the Pixel Watch 5’s mechanism perfectly. If you handed this to me and said it was an official band from Google, I’d probably believe you.

To get this level of quality at such a low price is wild, all while getting colors that, again, Google doesn’t make. I found this while just wanting a purple band, but the price tag pushed me to just buy a whole pack, and the striking deep red included sneakily became one of my favorites.

Pixel Watch isn’t alone here – third-party knock-off bands for Fitbit Air are very good, as my colleage Damien Wilde has pointed out repeatedly.

I originally bought the Stephen Curry edition Fitbit Air and, long story short, I don’t have that official band anymore. But the $9 (currently even cheaper!) knock-off I bought on Amazon is quite literally identical in quality, feel, and even the details. The only thing it’s missing is the Stephen Curry badge (which, frankly, I don’t miss anyway), but it still has that little piece of added material where the wordmark would have been.

The same goes for the matching band I picked up for my Pixel Watch 5.

Google doesn’t sell this band on its own, which is annoying, but the knock-off I bought is basically identical to what I felt during our hands-on with the official one back in August, though this one doesn’t have the space for the Stephen Curry badge the real one does. The lugs connecting to the watch are also different and not nearly as stylish, but I’m absolutely not complaining here.

Is it possible that Google’s supply chain is producing these near-identical versions? Given the quality at play, I really wouldn’t be surprised! That said, a lot of these are simple copies. It’s not all that hard to spin up generic versions of products like these.

Most third party cheap Fitbit Air bands I've tried range from "decent" to "very good".

Most third party cheap and expensive Pixel Watch bands I've tried range from "very, very bad" to "almost decent".

The major issue with PW 3rd party bands isn't the band itself. It's the connecting lugs.

Not how they look, but how they're manufactured. High tolerances, rattling, too lose or too tight connections.

And that applies also to certified "made for Google" bands (i.e. the Spigen ones).

I've given up. I'm going to buy the official Pixel Watch leather band and take the official lugs out of it, so I can use with any 22mm strap.

I’ll still happily pick up some of Google’s official bands because it’s hard to beat some of those color options but, for the most part, it’s getting hard to go wrong with the knock-off Pixel Watch bands, and even more so with the Fitbit Air. The simpler tracker gets even better third-party options seeing as Google made the mounting mechanism free for everyone.

Whatever the case, third-party / knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands are in a really good place right now, and that’s refreshing after a few years of using this lineup every day.

#10598. ‘You’ve Got Mail’ is getting a remake. There’s just one problem with that: Everything!

There are plenty of reasons to not remake a movie. Maybe it’s a stone-cold classic that seemingly can’t be improved upon. Sometimes it’s a foreign film that worked well enough already without photoshopping American faces into the lead roles. Other times, it’s an older movie with a recognizable title that nobody actually cares about anymore or one made so recently that remaking it serves no discernible purpose. These are all legitimate critiques, yet studios enamored with intellectual property frequently ignore them—with varying artistic and box office success.

You’ve Got Mail is a different situation altogether. 

While it’s perhaps impossible to replicate the star wattage, irresistible charm, and effortless chemistry of 1998 Tom Hanks and Meg Ryan, or the whip-smart comedic precision of filmmaker Nora Ephron (RIP), that isn’t even the main strike against a remake. Never mind that You’ve Got Mail reunited this trio five years after their funny, tearjerking smash, Sleepless in Seattle, giving it the rom-com equivalent of a Marvel movie’s magnitude. What makes You’ve Got Mail uniquely un-remake-able is that technology, social norms, and economics have changed so much in the last 28 years that everything about this movie now feels like a relic of the distant past.

Gen Z moviegoers—currently, the most active and coveted demographic—have likely never even heard the AOL notification that lent You’ve Got Mail its name. (It finally faded away for good last year when AOL discontinued its dial-up internet service, but the audio file had fallen out of cultural relevance sometime in the early-aughts.) If the words “You’ve got mail” now mean anything to young people, it’s likely in the context of “That old movie my parents like.”

An extremely 1998 film

Title aside, not much is known about the new Warner Bros. project. It will be directed by Jason Orley, the creative visionary behind the Pete Davidson-led Peacock series Bupkis, but plot details have yet to be announced. Possibly because those details threaten to turn the new film into something bearing scant resemblance to the original.

You’ve Got Mail, itself a remake of 1940’s The Shop Around the Corner (starring Jimmy Stewart and Margaret Sullavan), is centered around Kathleen Kelly (Ryan), an idealistic Upper West Side independent bookstore owner, and Joe Fox (Hanks), the cynical scion of a Barnes & Noble-like bookstore dynasty. The pair meet anonymously in an AOL chatroom, those early precursors to social media, and strike up a tastefully flirty friendship that drives them to consider leaving their current romantic partners (Greg Kinnear and Parker Posey, respectively). The dynamics of their quasi-courtship change, however, when Fox realizes his unnamed potential paramour is actually the woman his new store on the Upper West Side is about to put out of business. It’s a Montagues-and-Capulets-caliber obstacle that forestalls the inevitable happy ending.

The plot of this warm blanket of a movie hinges on two intertwined ideas: that strangers can connect over the internet and not turn out to be creeps, and that enormous chain bookstores are the mortal enemy of independents. Both ideas now seem quaintly, adorably naive.

Dating? On the internet??

In 1998, the internet was a mysterious novelty. Only 41% of adults had even logged on at all. While early adopters swore it would eventually change everything, most Americans were still wrapping their heads around the concept of email. Now, of course, the internet is the infrastructure modern society is built upon, and the filter through which many people experience the world. About 97% of adults younger than 50 are now connected to it at all times through smartphones, a technology that was nearly a decade away when You’ve Got Mail was made.

In retrospect, it’s a miracle that a person of Nora Ephron’s age—she was 56 at the time—perfectly depicted the moment when meeting strangers on the internet first broached mainstream acceptability. Prior to then, meeting people online was considered sordid, shameful business. Ephron both grounded her film in that perception and circumvented it by having her two leads fall ass-backward into internet romance.

There was something interesting and even sort of sweet about the idea of potential soulmates accidentally finding each other within the randomness of a non-sexual Web 1.0 chatroom. It now sounds like the kind of meet-cute one of those elderly couples in the Ephron-penned When Harry Met Sally might share if that film came out today.

What could possibly be an organic way for two attached people in 2026, though, to accidentally strike up a relationship online that retains the innocent curiosity of the 1998 internet? 

There’s nothing interesting anymore about meeting someone online. A chance encounter with a stranger on the r/Pringles subreddit could potentially lead to a hookup, which is something everyone has known or at least intuited for a very long time. Internet dating is now well past mainstream status. It’s something so pervasive as to no longer require any hand-wringing or soul-searching—you either do it or don’t do it, and nobody cares much either way. According to a 2019 study from Stanford University, over 39% of heterosexual couples now meet online, making the internet the most popular destination for sparking a new relationship.

But even if the filmmakers found a plausible scenario in which the two leads chastely meet online, they won’t face the dilemma that occupies the first half of the original movie—whether to physically meet someone who digitally swept you off your feet. After multiple decades of normalized internet dating, that question no longer carries any tension. Without it, the remake will just be about whether two people in unfulfilling relationships should turn their emotional infidelity into something more—essentially the plot of any generic romantic comedy.

Borders: Now there’s a name I haven’t heard in a long time

Although You’ve Got Mail correctly portrays the two leads’ ongoing anonymity as a red flag, the mutual identity firewall makes sense given the pair’s hesitation to go any further. But the idea that both parties in a more modern online flirtation would emotionally invest several months in someone who insists on keeping their identity a secret is ludicrous.

Even harder to pull off in a modern update than keeping their identities secret is who the two main characters actually are offline.

In 1998, an indie bookseller going up against a big-box bookstore would have been a genuine David-and-Goliath conflict. Barnes & Noble and Borders represented an existential threat to any local shop who struggled to keep up with the bottomless inventory, discounted pricing, and piping hot coffee those chains had in spades. Little did either side know they’d soon be united together in a battle for brick-and-mortar bookstore survival against Amazon, which launched just three years before You’ve Got Mail.

Now, all bookstores are fighting for their lives, with Barnes & Noble cribbing notes from indie bookshops to stay relevant in the BookTok era. (Borders did not survive Amazon’s ascendance, having reached its final chapter back in 2011.)

Affinity for independent booksellers was no match for the convenience and savings of a Barnes & Noble back in 1998, which is why Kelly eventually loses her shop in the film. That’s no longer necessarily the case. Anyone setting foot in a bookshop now has generally made an intentional choice to not buy a book from Amazon for cheap, and to instead experience human curation and customer service while supporting a local business. Meanwhile, Barnes & Noble is now more likely to buy a struggling independent bookshop than put one out of business.

The only way the major conflict in You’ve Got Mail would work in a modern update is if the plot moved out of the book world entirely, which is what will likely happen. But current options for a David-and-Goliath-esque business conflict that poignantly capture a changing world, mid-transformation, aren’t exactly inspiring. Maybe it will involve a celebrity podcaster at a major network launching a new show with the exact same idea as a well-established indie podcast—a concept I’m already mad about just having thought of. Maybe it will be the creator of a hot new AI chatbot financially ruining the founder of a once-great search engine. It might even be something even dumber. Unfortunately, the resulting movie could accidentally provide some incisive metacommentary about the moment “enshittification” came for Hollywood. 

Whatever happens, any remake of You’ve Got Mail that’s even vaguely faithful to the source material will have a title that no longer has any meaning, a premise that no longer matters, and a conflict that no longer exists. 

The only way to fix these problems would be to change so much about the original that what’s mainly being remade is the definition of what can reasonably be considered a remake. At that point, why not just start from scratch? The resounding box office lesson of 2026 is that audiences are tired of being treated like seals who reliably clap at the dangled fish of familiar IP. Original hits like Obsession, You Can See Everything, and The Drama inspired as much conversation this year as they did box office returns. 

Unrecognizable remakes of beloved classics, on the other hand, seem more like the modern descendants of another relic of the late 1990s: the straight-to-video sequel.

#10597. Big Google Antigravity update brings Remote Agents, Voice Mode, and redesign

Google is rolling out a big update to Antigravity this week that features a new design, Voice Mode, and Remote Agents.

Version 2.29 of Google’s agentic development platform for Mac, Windows, and Linux starts with a redesigned conversation page that moves the prompt box to the bottom of the screen, while there’s now a greeting at the top, like the Gemini app. The app also now has a default Google Sans font and Onyx theme.

Additionally: “Single-repo projects combine branch and Git worktree selection into one menu, and the agent picker now lives inside the model menu.”

Next to the existing microphone, there’s a new Voice Mode that’s powered by Gemini Live. This lets you “talk to Antigravity and hand requests to the agent by voice.” Google offers various options for Antigravity’s voice, while you can customize speed from 0.75x to 2x.

We want Antigravity to be working for you all the time. We’ve been rolling out remote agents this week. Your tasks run in independent cloud virtual machines. #10595. Galaxy SmartTag 3 hits Samsung online store as single tracker or four-pack

Samsung’s latest generation of item trackers for Galaxy devices is available online in two colors, starting at $29.

Samsung announced the Galaxy SmartTag 3 last month, though it’s only now available for purchase online. The new tracker bears a different appearance, with a much more uniform and compact shape compared to the larger profile in previous designs. It’s a little more portable, too, even though it ditches the built-in loop.

For one reason or another, the Galaxy SmartTag 3 works with the Galaxy Find network but not Google’s larger Find My network. That means the SmartTag 3 will be exclusive to Galaxy devices and iPhones, since Samsung’s iOS app will support basic connectivity with the device.

The new Galaxy-branded tracker brings a few important features into play. These include routine activation, leave-behind alerts, and location history. The tags are rated for 550 days of battery power and are rated IP67 water and dust resistant.

Samsung added two colorways to its website – white and black. Both are sold as an individual Galaxy SmartTag 3 for $29, but you can grab a bundle of four – two of each color – for $99.

#10594. Jensen Huang brought a lot more to Nvidia than a cult of personality. Why his downfall hits so hard

By now, you have probably heard: Nvidia’s cofounder Jensen Huang resigned Thursday after he confirmed having an affair. The woman at the center of it all, Alice Huang, is alleged to be a Chinese spy, in what sounds like a page ripped from a James Bond thriller. Reports now suggest that Huang also had at least two other affairs with employees at Nvidia.

The scandal is rife with intrigue and all-too-real consequences—including national security concerns and a major hit to the financial markets, as Nvidia’s share price continues to fall.

NVIDIA Corporation (NVDA) was down about 5% in afternoon trading on Thursday at the time of this writing, after suffering double digit losses over the past few days since the scandal broke.

Altogether, the developments shine a spotlight on Huang’s unique geopolitical value, as he—and his AI chips—sat at the center of the U.S. AI arms race with China. Nvidia controls about 90% of the advanced chips market used for AI, per The New York Times.

His departure is a big loss for Nvidia—one that goes beyond his outsized personality, which made him one of tech’s biggest media darlings.

It also goes beyond his close proximity to President Donald Trump, who earlier this week defended and praised Huang for doing a “fantastic job.” (Reports allege that Trump knew about Huang’s affair: According to The Washington Post, Trump was briefed by Secretary of State Marco Rubio about the situation on May 12 on Air Force One, and The New York Times reported Trump was briefed about Jensen Huang and Alice Huang’s ‘close relationship’ as early as October 2025. Trump, for his part, denied hearing about the situation in May.)

“Sadly, greatness doesn’t guarantee goodness. It’s disappointing,” venture capitalist Sarah Guo wrote on X. “The Economist says ‘no one is irreplaceable.’ They are very wrong.”

Huang’s value comes not only from the fact that he built and co-founded Nvidia—and remains the company’s largest individual shareholder—but also from his close personal and business ties and connections in Asia, with, among others, semiconductor maker TSMC and its founder Morris Chang, which has contributed to a massive supply chain pipeline for Nvidia.

Another issue: Huang’s departure from Nvidia comes at a critical political moment, on the heels of the Democratic party’s so-called “blue tsunami” sweep into Congress in last week’s midterm elections, which ushers in a new era for Silicon Valley—and could likely bring an abrupt end to its cozy relationship with Washington’s Republicans.

“Congress must demand answers from Mr. Huang and others at Nvidia about how they may have put U.S. national security at risk,” Democratic leader Senator Chuck Schumer posted on X Thursday. “The Trump administration must also answer for what it did once it learned about Mr. Huang’s relationship with a suspected Chinese intelligence asset. Were government officials told not to investigate because of Trump’s relationship with Mr. Huang? What did Trump know when he personally invited Mr. Huang aboard Air Force One?”

#10591. How the effects of Jensen Huang’s ouster is spiraling out through the economy

NVIDIA and Jensen Huang aren’t the only ones in the crosshairs. 

Jensen Huang has had a bad week. It all began when news surfaced that he’d been having an affair with an alleged spy for the Chinese government. In response, NVIDIA’s corporate board began an investigation, and the FBI launched a probe, too. Then, late last night, news broke that Huang had at least one other affair, with a company executive who reported directly to him. By Thursday morning, Huang had resigned, and the chip company had appointed a new interim CEO. Reuters then reported a second affair with a different employee.

Obviously, this is bad news for NVIDIA. Stocks are down, and Democrats, now preparing to take back Congress, appear to be ramping up plans to investigate the company and any possible risks to US national security. But this is also bad for a range of other entities that have a stake in NVIDIA’s success.

Here are a few entities that stand to feel some of the blowback. 

The White House

The White House, fresh off a devastating loss in the midterm Congressional elections, stands to face questions about why it held off on taking action after it learned about Jensen Huang’s affair with an alleged spy. The Trump administration already had a poor reputation for security and intelligence practices, but seemingly sleeping on this knowledge is all but certain to raise the prospect of further probes. 

Trump officials, notably, have cleared deals allowing NVIDIA to sell advanced computer chips to the US government, and even leveraged its influence over the company’s chips in geopolitical negotiations. 

Current and prospective NVIDIA customers

The US AI supply chain runs on NVIDIA. OpenAI, Anthropic, and a slew of other large language model developers rely on the company’s hardware. Meta, Microsoft, and Oracle are also major customers. This is, in large part, because GPUs—or graphics processing units—are particularly adept at the kinds of computations needed to develop large reasoning models. 

Now, with so much scrutiny on NVIDIA, there’s a risk those companies—and any deals in progress—might face headwinds as the still-scrambling board figures out what to do next. The Semiconductor Industry Association, a major trade group that advocates for chip companies, declined to comment. Hugging Face, an AI developer that agreed to an acquisition by NVIDIA earlier this year, did not respond to a request for comment. 

Huang, notably, doesn’t seem to have had a succession plan, and reportedly had 60 direct reports instead. Colette Kress, the company’s chief financial officer, was appointed interim CEO on Thursday morning. 

The scandal and disarray, of course, isn’t just bad for American companies that need NVIDIA’s chips. Given the extent to which Jensen Huang advocated for loosening chip sale limits to China, any customers with suspected links to the Chinese government may also see far more scrutiny on NVIDIA deals than before. 

U.S. federal agencies

The U.S. government is a major supporter of NVIDIA. The US government has also signed plenty of deals to use Nvidia on its own systems, including classified networks. The latest scandal is not only embarrassing for the US government, but raises concerns about the extent to which government officials were aware of the affair, and how they might have influenced the company’s dealings with federal agencies it works with. One former federal chief information told Fast Company that the scandal raises suspicions that the US security clearance system, which is supposed to engage in continuous monitoring, isn’t as up to snuff as it should be. 

Everyone else 

We really are that dependent on NVIDIA. If we learned anything from the chip shortage we experienced during the pandemic, the semiconductor market is remarkably concentrated, and disruptions with just one company can quickly ripple through the economy. In 2020, it was companies like TSMC, a Taiwan-based contract manufacturer that supplies chips for a host of devices. And the stock market—including pensions funds and 401ks—are deeply exposed to Nvidia stock.

“It is striking that NVIDIA, a $4 trillion company central to both the AI economy and US equity markets, appears to have no publicly identified successor to Jensen Huang,” Ann Lewis, a technology executive and former US government tech official, told Fast Company Wednesday. ‘In cybersecurity, we call this a single point of failure. A company of this scale should not depend so completely on one person’s relationships and judgment.”

Even open-source isn't safe from Nintendo's legal team

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nintendo has set its famously litigious sights on the open-source firmware that powers hardware modchips on Nintendo Switch. Earlier this week, GitHub posted a DCMA request from Nintendo of America confirming that an entire network of 144 total repositories, including the parent repository, was taken down. Mod chip firmware for hardware including SX Core, SX Lite, Picofly, and HWFLY was targeted.

Early original Nintendo Switch consoles shipped with an unpatchable hardware exploit found in the device's recovery mode, which has since been patched in later units such as the Switch Lite and Switch OLED (the modchip hack doesn't work on Switch 2). As such, running custom software on patched Switches requires hardware modification, soldering a microchip like a Picofly, HWFLY, or SX Core to your Switch's motherboard. These chips require firmware, specifically code that can perform the voltage glitching required during boot to hack the Switch.

Rather than playing whack-a-mole with vendors of off-the-shelf microcontrollers, Nintendo has instead issued a DCMA takedown notice to the entire family of GitHub repos hosting this firmware. "The reported repositories offer, link to, or otherwise provide access to mod chip firmware including but not limited to SX Core, SX Lite, Picofly, or HWFLY," the DCMA request from Nintendo reads. "The firmware allows Nintendo Switch modchips to illegally circumvent Nintendo’s TPMs in order to run illegal copies of Nintendo Switch games. Specifically, during operation, the mod chip firmware at the reported repositories circumvents the technological protection measures put in place on the Nintendo Switch console by bypassing the boot sequence.

Nintendo went on to state that because these repositories are designed "primarily" to unlawfully circumvent technological measures controlling access to work protected under the DCMA, distributing mod chip firmware "constitutes unlawful trafficking in technology that is 'primarily designed or produced for the purpose of circumventing a technological measure that effectively controls access' to copyrighted works." In support of this argument, Nintendo cited criminal proceedings brought by the US Department of Justice against Team-Xecuter, a maker and seller of SX Pro, SX Core, and SX Lite modchips.

While these modchips can be (and are) used for game piracy, they unlock a host of features including running homebrew and custom applications, emulation, alternative operating systems, and more. They also enable game modding, custom themes, and even Switch overclocking. Naturally, Nintendo has chosen to focus on their utility in accessing pirated games and bypassing DRM.

Because much of this hardware and firmware is already out in the wild, the move won't have any impact on existing Switch owners who have a modchip installed. However, this will prevent firmware updates to existing modchips, as well as firmware flashing and reflashing. Naturally, it will also prevent future potential hardware modders from hacking their Switch in the same way.

Nintendo is famous for its fervent prosecution of copyright law, but developments in AI are turning this into a drastically more complicated battle. Nintendo's Zelda remake was dumped online on the same day it launched on Switch 2, while earlier this year one developer used AI to bring the original Nintendo 64 version to iOS. In July, Mario Kart Wii was recompiled for PC in 4K with over 200 tracks.

#10588. Two more Garmin smartwatches are down to record-low prices

The Garmin Fenix E and Garmin Venu 3S are for two different types of people, but both are available at a surprisingly low price thanks to these limited-time Garmin deals.

Garmin watches are often expensive, which is why I always recommend them on sale. For example, the Garmin Fenix E, usually $799, is now down to $399. That’s the all-time low price for this model and probably the best Garmin deal I’ve seen so far this fall.

The Garmin Fenix E is essentially a Garmin Epix Gen 2 with a newer OS. It sports multi-GNSS navigation plus a 3-axis compass, gyroscope, and barometric altimeter that should cover basically all your tracking needs. It’s got pulse ox monitoring, respiration tracking, advanced sleep monitoring, real-time stamina tracking, and a training readiness score (so you know what days you should be taking it easy).

Despite all the features, this one will last you two weeks per charge and can utilize GPS for up to 42 hours on each charge. The stainless steel bezel looks great, and the 47mm fiber-reinforced polymer case is built to last. This is one of those watches that, quite frankly, makes no sense at full cost. At half the price, though? Yeah, that looks much better.

If you’re looking for something a little more elegant-looking, the Garmin Venu 3S is the right choice. At $299, this Garmin deal makes it $250 less than the Garmin Venu 4. You might think buying the newest one is automatically better, but that’s not necessarily the case between these two models.

For example, the Venu 4 is thicker and heavier because it uses an all-metal casing, has one less button on the side, lacks the permanent hour markers that are on the Venu 3S’s bezel, and has worse battery life by 1-2 days, depending on your usage. The Venu 4 has more features and a newer OS, but I personally like the OS design on the 3S better (and I’m apparently not alone on that front).

The Venu 4 also has new sleep guidance and coaching features, training load and several tools that used to be for Garmin Forerunner watches, plus lifestyle logging, but I’m not sure I’d want to pay $250 to get those. Either way, you’ve got a great option now that the Venu 3S is at such a fantastic price.

#10586. Trump’s suggestion for the next Nvidia CEO is another tech titan he’s already close with

With Jensen Huang officially out as president and CEO of Nvidia, rumors are swirling over who will take his place at the helm of one of the world’s biggest tech companies. Could it be interim CEO Colette Kress, who’s spent more than a decade as Nvidia’s executive vice president and CFO? Or maybe the company’s cofounder Chris Malachowsky, now a senior vice president of engineering and operations?

President Donald Trump has a different idea. His left-field pick: former Apple CEO Tim Cook. In a post on Truth Social, Trump announced his preference for Huang’s successor.

“NOW THAT Nvidia CEO JENSEN HUANG, who has done a fantastic job, has resigned, there is only one man to take his place – TIM COOK of Apple,” Trump wrote. “A Legend replaces a Legend!”

Beyond Trump’s dubious claim that Huang did “a fantastic job” as CEO—having an affair with an alleged Chinese spy might disqualify him from that distinction—Cook is far from an obvious pick to step into the role.

It was just months ago in September that Cook stepped away from being Apple’s CEO and became its executive chairman. In a company meeting announcing the decision, Cook said his stepdown had to come at a time when “business had to be doing great,” when the “product road map to be incredible,” and when his replacement, former senior vice president of hardware engineering John Ternus, was “ready for the role.” With all those conditions met, “now was the time,” Cook said.

Why, then, would Trump want Cook to take on another CEO mantle? Notably, the two have gotten close during Trump’s second term as president.

Trump and Cook, sitting in a tree

Trump and Cook weren’t always buddies. During Trump’s first term as president, he infamously referred to Cook as “Tim Apple,” seemingly forgetting his name.

For his part, Cook insists that he’s not politically aligned with Trump, instead abstaining from politics entirely.

“What I do is I interact on policy, not politics. I’m not a political person on either side. I’m not political,” Cook said in an interview for Good Morning America earlier this year. “I’m kind of straight down the middle. And I focus on policy, so I’m very pleased that the president and the administration is accessible to talk about policy,” he added.

That purported focus on policy aligns with Cook’s intentions for his role as Apple’s executive chairman, which include overseeing the company’s global relations. “This is an area where we’ve built relationships over multiple years and a decade-plus, and I think I can help with that,” Cook said when announcing his stepdown.

View on Threads

But for most, the jokes are encouraging Microsoft to take its time fixing the outage, with users not exactly in a rush to get back to work.

Another added, “we can just keep saying outlook is down when they try to get it back up. we don’t have to keep doing this. we can all be free.”

#10583. Discord is about to get way, way faster on your Android phone

A new Discord client built in Rust is looking pretty shiny and clean, even on low-end Android phones.

I love Discord, but if there’s one thing the app isn’t known for is it’s speed. That’s particularly true on phones that don’t have much RAM, but an upcoming new Discord client is changing the game for all users, regardless of the platform.

Discord CTO and co-founder Stanislav Vishnevskiy posted on X about several new Discord updates that are getting users excited. In the video, embedded below, you can see Discord running on two very old, lower-end phones (looks like a Samsung A-series to me). The current client, on the left, takes a full 3 seconds to launch, and then another 2 seconds to fully load. The new client, on the right, launches in under 1 second and is finished loading everything before the 2-second mark.

A release date has not been announced for the new client, although it is presumably launching soon.

We didn't forget about mobile, especially Android. Inspired by React Native, it drives the OS-native view layer through the same Rust codebase as the new desktop app. I think it might be the most responsive app on my phone now? https://t.co/uf5EwUtQLh pic.twitter.com/F8QCedZcJj

Stanislav says the new client uses a lot less RAM, which goes in line with the new PC client he also posted about this week. Now that Discord is cleaning up its client and the iPhone Duo is out, I’m personally hoping for a version that works properly with foldable phones. The Discord app has been finicky on foldable phones ever since they merged the Android app with the iOS one a few years ago. One can certainly hope.

#10581. How to Get a YouTube API Key and Use the Data API (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You need a YouTube API key the moment your app has to read anything from YouTube: a video's length, its view count, a channel's latest uploads. Getting one takes about five minutes. Using it well takes a little longer, because YouTube changed how its quota works in June 2026, and plenty of guides still describe the old rules.

In this guide we'll get a key, lock it down and test it. Then we'll use it from Node.js for three real jobs: adding up the length of a playlist, listing a channel's videos without spending a search, and estimating what a video earned. Along the way we'll cover what the YouTube API costs (nothing), how the 2026 quota works, and the errors you're most likely to hit.

A YouTube API key identifies your project to the YouTube Data API v3. With just a key, you can read public data:

A key isn't enough for anything that touches someone's account. Uploading videos, editing playlists or reading private data needs OAuth 2.0, where the user signs in and grants your app access.

You'll need a Google account. The YouTube Data API is free, so there's nothing to pay for.

The restrictions matter more than they look. A key is just a string in a URL, and anyone who copies an unrestricted one can spend your daily quota for you. With an API restriction, the key is useless for other Google APIs. With an application restriction, it only works from your server or your site. Changes to restrictions can take up to five minutes to apply, so don't panic if a request fails straight after you save.

Before writing any code, check the key with a single request. This one asks for the first video ever uploaded to YouTube, and uses the fields parameter to trim the response down to four values:

That request cost 1 unit of your daily quota, which brings us to the question everyone asks.

Yes. There's no charge per request and no paid plan. Instead you get a daily quota, and since June 1, 2026 it's split into separate buckets:

Google's quota calculator lists the cost of every method. Quotas reset at midnight Pacific Time. Every request costs at least 1 unit, including requests that fail, so a retry loop around a broken call can use up a day's quota on its own.

If you've read older guides, search is the big change. A search used to cost 100 units out of the same 10,000, so 100 searches emptied your whole day. Uploads were worse still, at about 1,600 units each until December 2025. Now both have their own buckets, and the 10,000 units are left for everything else.

To give you a feel for what 10,000 units buys, here's what each example in this guide costs:

You can't pay for more. To go above the defaults, fill in the YouTube API Services Audit and Quota Extension Form, which is linked from Google's page on quota and compliance audits. YouTube first audits your project to check it follows the YouTube API Services Terms of Service, then someone from the API team gets back to you. Google doesn't give a timeline, so apply well before you need the extra quota.

Most projects never need to. The rest of this guide is about getting a lot done with very few units.

The examples need Node.js 20.6 or later, for the built-in fetch and the --env-file flag. There are no packages to install. Make a folder with a .env file in it:

Every example imports one small helper. It builds the URL, adds your key and turns API errors into readable messages:

Run any example with node --env-file=.env followed by the file name.

YouTube doesn't tell you how long a playlist is. To work it out, you need two methods:

Durations come back in ISO 8601 format. PT15M33S is 15 minutes 33 seconds, PT1H2M3S adds an hour, and a stream longer than a day looks like P1DT2H3M4S. The toSeconds() function below handles all three.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Full Courses in One Video playlist:

That's 184 hours of courses for 4 units of quota. Private and deleted videos still show up in a playlist's items, but videos.list doesn't return them, which is why the script prints "52 of 52 videos available". On a playlist with removed videos, the two numbers will differ.

This is the core of the free YouTube playlist length calculator I built on createthat.ai. The live version adds a target time box that works out the speed you need to finish by a deadline, and it accepts channel, video and Shorts links as well as playlists.

The obvious way to find a channel's videos is to search for them. Don't: search is now capped at 100 calls a day, and it isn't built to list everything a channel has posted. Every channel has an uploads playlist instead, and you can reach it in two cheap calls:

Two units, and none of your 100 daily searches. To get every upload rather than the newest five, page through the playlist with the same pageToken loop as Example 1.

videos.list with part=statistics returns a video's view, like and comment counts. Dislike counts have been private since December 2021 and favoriteCount is always 0, so don't build anything on those. The script also copes with a missing like or comment count, which you'll see on some videos.

This time the fields parameter trims the response to the values we use. That makes the response smaller, but it doesn't change the cost: the call is 1 unit either way.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Learn Python full course, one of the most-watched videos in that playlist:

Treat the last line as a rough range, not an answer. The Data API doesn't share anyone's revenue, so any estimate is views multiplied by an RPM: what a creator keeps per 1,000 views. RPM moves a lot with the topic and where the viewers live, and the estimate assumes the video shows ads, which not every channel does.

And sometimes you don't need an API at all. When I built a font tester, I needed a list of Google Fonts. The Google Fonts Developer API would have meant a key and a call on every visit, for a list that barely changes. Instead I generated a file of the 500 most popular fonts once, from Google's public font metadata, and the page loads it like any other file. No key, no quota and nothing to fail.

When a call fails, the response's error.message and error.details[0].reason tell you why. These are the ones you'll see most:

For the invalid key error, the full response looks like this:

Yes. Creating a key and calling the YouTube Data API costs nothing. You're limited by the daily quota instead, and there's no paid tier: more quota comes through an audit.

In the Google Cloud console, select your project and go to APIs & Services > Credentials. Your keys are listed under API keys, with a Show key option next to each one.

It works, but anyone can read the key from your page. If you do it, restrict the key to your website and to the YouTube Data API v3. A small server route that calls the API for you is safer, and it gives you somewhere to cache results.

By default, 100 searches, 100 uploads and 10,000 units for everything else. Most reads cost 1 unit and videos.list takes 50 IDs per call, so that's enough for the details of up to 500,000 videos a day.

Getting a YouTube API key is the easy part: one project, one API to enable, one key and two restrictions. The 2026 quota is generous if you work with it:

All three examples together cost 7 units, out of 10,000. From here, try commentThreads.list to pull a video's comments 100 at a time, or extend Example 2 to page through a channel's entire upload history and add it all up with the code from Example 1.

#10579. A major change to the U.S. mortgage market could be coming. Here’s why homeowners should care

As President Donald Trump enters the lame-duck era of his presidency, that raises questions about which of his various agenda items will actually get accomplished.

One of his long-standing goals has been to end the government conservatorship that mortgage giants Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac have been subject to since the financial crisis. After failing to make much headway during his first term in office, Trump revived those intentions for his second term—but once again hasn’t made it much of a priority.

This past May, Trump posted on Truth Social that he was “working on taking these amazing companies public,” while adding that the U.S. government would retain its mortgage guarantees and oversight role for both government-sponsored entities. But only a matter of days later, when asked about an initial public offering for Fannie and Freddie, Trump said: “It’s not a rush.”

Meanwhile, Trump has also seen an opportunity with the current government control of Fannie and Freddie to use them as tools to address affordability. On the eve of the midterm elections, he announced at a rally in Kansas that the entities would invest $4.3 billion in home loans in the state next year, which he promised would help 15,000 people achieve their American dream.

Removing Fannie and Freddie from conservatorship, experts have cautioned, could push up mortgage rates. And because Democrats regained control of Congress by campaigning on affordability, that may make them reluctant to entertain an end to this long-standing conservatorship now, when so many Americans are grappling with an affordability crisis. 

Further complicating matters is how, exactly, the Trump administration could go about ending the conservatorship—whether by an executive order or with support from Congress. Legal and political analysts are divided on whether that could happen or if Trump would need support from Congress, as Realtor.com reported.  

Frank Cassidy, the former Federal Housing Commissioner in Trump’s second term, told the outlet that Trump can privatize Fannie and Freddie without Republican control of Congress, but noted that how they were structured post-conservatorship would matter. 

“It hasn’t been a priority yet,” Cassidy told Realtor.com. “It would need to be worked on very quickly because it will take some time.” 

A PUSH FROM WALL STREET

One person who’d very much like privatizing Fannie and Freddie to be a priority is hedge funder Bill Ackman. He says that removing these entities from conservatorship would be “helpful” for improving housing affordability because it would remove any political uncertainty from the mortgage market, particularly if Democrats were to use Fannie and Freddie in the future to “achieve political objectives.”

Of course, he has an incentive at stake: His firm, Pershing Square, is invested in both Fannie and Freddie. Though the companies were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010, they have traded in the over-the-counter market ever since.

Still, Ackman argues that Americans stand to benefit from ending the conservatorship. “This is a major point of economic policy where the president can have a hundreds of billion-dollar win for the American people,” he told CNBC, adding that it could be a “windfall” to the tune of $500 billion.

WEIGHING THE CONSEQUENCES

Such optimism for a widespread benefit to Americans counters what Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent expressed on a 2Way podcast appearance last month. 

While he said that the Trump administration will tackle the ownership structure of Fannie and Freddie in either 2027 or 2028, he said the “north star” guiding a decision will be whether ending the conservatorship can be accomplished without meaningfully increasing the spread between mortgage-backed securities and Treasury yields.

“It’s unclear why homeowners or mortgage holders would benefit from a change in the arrangement of the shareholder structure,” Bessent said.

What people on both sides of the political aisle can agree on is that a rush to end an 18-year arrangement would be most problematic. Still, the clock is ticking on Trump’s time in office to accomplish this goal, with less than 800 days remaining. 

And one person who could stand in the way is Representative Maxine Waters, a Democrat from California, who is running to chair the House Financial Services Committee after serving on it for more than a decade. 

As Realtor.com reported, Waters has historically opposed any efforts to rapidly end the conservatorship without comprehensive congressional oversight. “To address the affordable housing crisis, Congress must ensure that Fannie and Freddie meet their mission to provide liquidity, sustainability, and affordability to all housing markets in the U.S., and at all times,” Waters said at a hearing in April.

The King 95 3D is a solid, premium-class mid-tower with three tempered-glass panels for panoramic views inside. The seven included Link fans on the Pro version deliver enough airflow and RGB bling, but can get noisy at full speed. For $119 or $199 for the Pro, it’s an excellent update from the original King 95.

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

PC case, cooling, and PSU maker Montech is celebrating its 10th anniversary this year and has released a new flagship ATX chassis, the King 95 3D and 3D Pro. Like the previous King 95, the 3D version keeps the signature 15-degree curved-glass panoramic front panel and adds a top glass panel, giving you a third window and a unique aesthetic for viewing your build. An integrated, contoured ARGB strip follows the chassis's smooth lines, making it look every bit the king of the castle.

Inside, the large mid-tower chassis supports multiple radiators (though only one 360mm), large graphics cards (plus integrated adjustable GPU support), CPU air coolers, and spots for 2.5- and 3.5-inch drives. However, it doesn't support E-ATX motherboards. The King 95 3D supports rear-connect motherboards and has ample room for cable routing and management. The front aluminum panel IO runs vertically, as with previous King cases, and includes a fast USB 3.2 Gen2x2 (20 Gbps) Type-C port. Overall, it has everything you want in a premium chassis. The uncommon three-panel view into your PC is a great way to show off your rig.

The King 95 3D is available for an inexpensive (at least for curved glass) $119.90 if you want to select your own fans. The 3D Pro includes seven pre-installed LINK Fan INF 120 fans for builders who want a more coordinated look out of the box, plus it includes the Lumi hub (a dedicated fan controller), for $199.90. We have the 3D Pro version in our lab and ran the case through thermal and noise testing in its out-of-the-box configuration to show how it performs compared to others. Will it make our best PC cases list? It has a chance. But before we get into the details, here are the case's specifications from Montech.

21 x 21 x 18.8 x 11.8-inches (534 x 480 x 300mm)

Top - 3x120Front - N/ABottom - 3x120mmSide: - 2x120mmRear - 2x120mm

(5) Link Fan INF 120 (Reverse blades, side/bottom)(2) Link Fan INF 120 (rear)

King 95 3D - $119.90 (w/o fans)King 95 3D Pro - $199.90 (w/7 pre-installed fans)

From the outside, the King 95 3D Pro looks the part of its royal name. That signature curve and three panes of glass offer a panoramic view inside your case like few others, letting you show off your PC from even more angles. It also features a frosted, continuous LED strip running along the tiered top, right side, and bottom, adding some tasteful bling to the chassis. If you get the Pro version with the seven fans, it’s an RGB light show inside which could be polarizing. I do wish there were an option for preinstalled non-RGB fans, since the seven included ones and the infinity mirror styling may not be for everyone. Still, the curved front corner and clean lines really cement the King 95 3D as one of the better-looking panoramic cases on the market.

Up front, the removable curved glass corner with a smoked tint sets it apart from most others. On the right, the front IO panel runs vertically, with the power button on top and the LED on/off button below. It has three USB ports: one 3.2 Gen2x2 (20 Gbps) Type-C and two USB 3.2 Gen1 (5 Gbps) Type-A ports. Finally, it has a 3.5mm audio jack. That continuous 118-LED strip is visible from the front, running along the top, down the right edge, and along the bottom edge. I’m not a fan of a lot of RGB, but this implementation is tasteful. You can control this lighting through the motherboard software or Montech's Lumi software, which lets you fully customize the fans and RGB strip.

The main window panel also uses a smoke-colored tint, with Montech branding in the upper-right corner, and it's secured to the frame by two captive thumbscrews on the back. To remove it, loosen the thumbscrews, slide the panel back, pull from the top, then lift it out of the slots. Easy. I have to say, the biggest problem with all this glass is keeping it pristine and fingerprint-free.The other (metal) side panel, behind the motherboard, slots into the frame and is secured the same way. It has filtered venting along the top and side for the fans/radiator locations, plus another area for airflow near the power supply and drive mounting locations. In the middle is a gold placard with Montech King 95 3D 10th anniversary badging. Note: The dust filters on this panel aren't removable, so you’ll need to remove the whole panel to clean things up.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

At the top, we get a better look at the third windowed panel, letting observers peer into the case from a different angle rather than straight on or from the front. We can also see the tiered top and part of the long RGB strip. Taking this panel off isn’t as simple as the rest. Yes, it’s also secured by two captive thumbscrews on the back, but to remove it, you’ll need to open the main window panel to expose a retaining screw, remove it, then loosen the back thumbscrews and slide it to the rear. After you remove the top glass, you can remove the upper and side retaining screws to take it out. When building, I didn’t find this necessary, but it’s good to know you can take it apart if needed.

Looking at the case from behind, one of the first things I noticed was the vertical PSU mounting location on the left. This space supports up to 220mm power supplies, which is all but the largest on the market. On the right, we see space for two 120mm fans, the motherboard I/O, and seven horizontal expansion slots. Unfortunately, Montech says the King 95 3D does not support vertical GPUs.

Underneath the case, there isn’t a lot going on. We get a better look at the four short rubber-bottom ‘feet’ and the removable dust filter. Montech put on its thinking cap and designed the dust filter to slide out from the front, so chances are you won’t have to move your heavy case to clean it.

Inside, the case uses a dual-chamber design, with the power supply and all wires hidden behind the motherboard tray. At the bottom, we see three Link Fan INF 120s (reverse blade) along with the mirror finish. The magnetically linked, daisy-chainable fans run up to 2,300 RPM (2,200 RPM on the reverse blade), pushing between 65-67 CFM with a maximum rating of 32dBA (29 dBA on the standard blade). A single Link Fan INF 120 costs $19.90, while a 3-pack (with Lumi Hub) is $64.90. If you do the math for seven fans and the Lumi Hub, it’s cheaper to buy them with the case rather than separately.

When powered on, the mirrored sides become an infinity panel, and with all seven matching, it creates a consistent view across the build. The bottom fan bracket is adjustable, with three positions (8 degrees). We left it down during testing, but with it up, you can focus more air towards the GPU and show off whatever fans you put in the space.

The motherboard tray has plenty of cutouts to access the back of the motherboard for easy installation/removal of air coolers or AIO/custom water-cooling blocks. The case also supports rear-connect ecosystems, including Asus BTF, Gigabyte Stealth, and MSI Project Zero boards. Grommets surround the motherboard tray and are in ideal locations, but like most, they pull out easily when you try to route cables, especially those with larger connectors. I wish all case vendors would secure these better, since they're a pain to reinstall, especially when the case is full of cables.Up top, we can see the huge gap above the motherboard tray for more fans and radiators. The case cooled just fine with the seven included fans and our air cooler, though GPU temps ran a bit warmer in our noise-normalized testing. That top gap is begging to be filled with fans, even if only for aesthetics. If you plan to use an AIO cooler, this isn’t a problem, since the radiator/fans will fill the space.

From the front, you can see the two Link INF 120mm fans and, at the bottom, the tool-less, adjustable GPU support strut. The back has space to mount two 120mm fans or one 120mm radiator. You can also see the seven horizontal expansion slots held in by thumbnail screws.

Inside, the top of the case above the motherboard space fits up to three 120mm fans or a 360mm radiator. The space is offset back from the motherboard tray, which is a boon for cable management. Normally, there’s barely any room to route the EPS cables, but here there's plenty. Just note that if your build uses air cooling, you will need to fill that area with fans to make it look good. Otherwise, it’s just a blank open space above the motherboard.

On the rear side of the chassis, we get a good look at the space behind the motherboard tray and the fan baffles. There’s plenty of room to route cables, and Montech has multiple pre-installed Velcro straps for cable routing. Even with the power supply installed, there’s plenty of room underneath it to stash excess cables.

At the bottom, we see a simple ARGB hub that consolidates the three different ARGB cables from the fans into one header to control all of them. Montech also includes a magnetic Lumi fan/RGB hub (which supports up to 13 Link Fans total) with both the Pro and non-Pro variants. Oddly, it comes loose/uninstalled on the Pro, but installed on the non-Pro. The box in the lower-right holds one 3.5-inch HDD and a single 2.5-inch drive. The second 2.5-inch drive mounts in the other location at the bottom left, next to the side fans.

We do like the look of the King 95 3D Pro case in black, and I bet it looks great in white with an all-white build too. The curved glass and 3-panel panoramic interior views are great for anyone who’d like to show off their now-ridiculously expensive parts. The included Link Fan INF 120 fans work well and run relatively quietly. If there’s an aesthetic drawback, it's that I’m not a huge fan of all the RGB and infinity mirrors on the pre-installed fans. It’s a lot, and it makes me yearn for an option with preinstalled fans and no RGB. Of course, you can turn them off in the software, or buy the non-Pro and choose whichever fans fit your build.

Current page: Features and Specifications

#10576. CEOs still think they can get away with affairs after #MeToo

Nearly a decade after the #MeToo movement burst into public consciousness, the upheaval at Nvidia may be a lesson in how little CEOs seem to have learned. 

Nvidia’s embattled CEO Jensen Huang has resigned, following a New York Times report on Wednesday that revealed he had indulged in another extramarital relationship—this time involving a subordinate, the company’s vice president of health care and one of his 60 direct reports. The affair was a blatant violation of Nvidia’s code of conduct, which notes that employees should steer clear of situations that could create a conflict of interest, including “personal relationships.” The company also purports to “promptly investigate” reports of misconduct.

While this dalliance has fewer national security implications than his entanglement with an alleged Chinese spy, it does suggest that CEOs like Huang don’t expect to face consequences for getting involved with employees. 

Huang is in good company: Many employers have similar policies that forbid romantic relationships between managers and their direct reports, but that hasn’t stopped executives from crossing the line anyway. (Who could forget the viral video of the Astronomer CEO caught canoodling with his chief people officer at a Coldplay concert?)

In recent years, however, a number of executives have been forced to step down after pursuing inappropriate relationships, in addition to CEOs like Leslie Moonves being fired over sexual harassment. 

Back in 2019, former McDonald’s CEO Steve Easterbrook was ousted over a relationship with a subordinate, then later sued by the company for concealing other relationships with employees and misleading investors. (He was eventually forced to pay back his $105 million severance package.) There was Alan Shaw, the former CEO of the railroad Norfolk Southern, who was let go in 2024 over his relationship with the company’s chief legal officer. Last year, the former Nestlé SA CEO Laurent Freixe was given the boot after an internal investigation confirmed a relationship with a subordinate (which Freixe had initially denied). Kohl’s CEO Ashley Buchanan was also recently forced out when the board learned that he had given millions of dollars in business to a vendor who he was dating without disclosing the relationship.

The fact that CEOs continue to pursue their subordinates, regardless of the consequences, may seem like a sign that the #MeToo movement is in the rear view. Perhaps some executives truly believe they are irreplaceable, or that there is now limited public pressure, years after #MeToo took hold. 

But Amy Nicole Baker, a professor at the University of New Haven who studies workplace romance, argues that not much has shifted after #MeToo, both in terms of CEO behavior and how companies tend to handle these situations. 

“The stock price goes down, at least in the short term,” she says, citing her research on how stock prices are impacted by sexual harassment allegations or affairs involving the CEO. “And usually CEOs are removed and replaced.”

Boards tend to have limited tolerance for this kind of behavior, which can be a sign of poor judgment. Indiscretions like an affair or inappropriate relationship can set off alarm bells or lead them to wonder what else a CEO is hiding. “Boards look at CEO behavior as a signal for what could be going on in the internal management of the company,” Baker says.  

Still, it appears CEOs don’t necessarily feel constrained by those expectations, or the rules they impose on their employees—even when their own peers have been in the line of fire. 

“People show less restraint when they are in power, and they’re also more likely to perceive someone else sending them sexual signals when they’re actually not,” Baker says. “So the research on power can tell us a little bit about why this keeps happening.” 

People in positions of power tend to be more impulsive and comfortable with risk—something they are often rewarded for in a business context. Very often, company culture can also reinforce that behavior, if it is more informal and encourages people to push boundaries. “If you’re willing to break a rule in one way, it’s probably more acceptable to tacitly break a rule in a different way,” she says. 

The intense scrutiny of Huang’s misdeeds might discourage some CEOs from engaging in relationships with subordinates or other inappropriate behavior—but likely not for long. 

“In terms of the CEOs and their propensity to get involved in risky workplace relationships, that doesn’t seem to have changed,” Baker says. “More people are willing to say, ‘Yes, this happened to me. Yes, this is an issue. Yes, this is a problem.’ There’s certainly more recognition and awareness. But when it comes to people at this highest echelon, I’m not seeing a change in behavior.”

#10573. Ray-Ban Meta Audio review: Great specs, shame about the price [U]

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses attempt to curb the controversy by removing the cameras, and while they’re still an excellent product, the price makes them harder to recommend.

This has been a bit of a rollercoaster year for Meta wearables. Meta’s VR division shrank considerably earlier this year, then later announced a massively redesigned follow-up that’ll launch next year. And while Meta has debuted dozens of new smart glasses styles throughout the year that have sold quite well, the company can’t seem to get away from controversy. That’s where Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses come in.

Now, that doesn’t mean Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses aren’t worthwhile. They feature a much wider prescription range (-12 to +6) than the majority of Meta smart glasses models, which often cap out at -6 to +6. The battery life is also significantly better thanks to the lack of a camera and other improvements in the hardware. And, of course, if you want a pair of AI glasses but don’t want the camera, these are the only options from Meta.

If I were to choose between the two available models, I’d pick the Clubmaster style every time. Not only do they look better overall and feature the thinnest frames of any Meta smart glasses, but they also have substantially better nosepads that are adjustable and can be easily replaced.

The Burbank model looks just fine, but the nosepads aren’t the same, and the glasses feel more rigid overall (which I find less comfortable). Both models come in lots of colors with many different lens options, including clear, tinted, polarized, Transitions, and prescription.

Both models feature a touchpad on the outside of the right temple arm, as well as a small, circular button up top to activate Meta AI. You can alternatively use the “Hey, Meta” keywords to activate Meta AI. The button and touchpad can be disabled if you like. Only the touchpad has an alternative function, and that alternative function only controls music playback via Spotify or Amazon Music. Other Meta glasses offer far more options.

Both styles are the lightest Meta glasses available. Most Meta smart glasses with cameras weigh in around the 54g mark. Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses weigh just 44g. They’re not as light as Even Realities G2, RayNeo iO, or MemoMind One, but 44g is still considered pretty light for smart glasses.

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses reuse the case from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 2, which looks more like a traditional Ray-Ban case rather than the foldable one that ships with Meta Ray-Ban Display or the 2026 Meta Camera Glasses. I don’t have an issue with Ray-Ban Meta Audio’s case at all, but I really enjoy the foldable one and wish it were included.

Audio playback on these is great, and while the speakers are identical to all other Meta glasses products, using them as spatial surround sound “headphones” while watching a movie with surround sound is a fantastic experience. They also have spatial audio recording and truly excellent noise cancellation thanks to the 6-mic setup.

Unfortunately, Ray-Ban Audio Glasses don’t support the new hearing aid function. To me, that’s a massive missed opportunity since these are audio-only glasses. When I asked Meta about it, they only said it wasn’t available on Ray-Ban Meta Audio for now, so this problem could be fixed in an upcoming update.

And that’s actually where Meta’s glasses might shine brightest: software updates. The company has been exemplary with update cadence on all of its products, and its smart glasses constantly get new, innovative features that surprise and delight. While I’m a little underwhelmed with the number of features available on these glasses today, this product will likely look quite different a year from now, a fact that’s backed up by years of Meta significantly improving product feature sets over time.

If you buy these glasses today, know that you’re mainly investing in them for the future. They’re fine enough for today’s usage, especially if you’re interested in a hands-free way to use Meta AI products like Muse or just want an open-ear way to listen to music and take phone calls, but Meta’s penchant for continually adding new features is, undoubtedly, going to be the highlight for everyone who ever buys them.

#10567. Top 6 AI Image Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer who once spent an afternoon searching stock libraries can now describe the picture they want and have four versions in under a minute. AI image generators have moved from curiosity to everyday tool for blog headers, product mockups, ad concepts, game art and slide decks. The quality gap between tools has narrowed, but the differences that remain matter a lot: how well each follows a detailed prompt, whether it can render readable text, how it handles edits, and what you are allowed to do with the result.

This guide compares six of the best-known options, what each does well and where each falls short. Models, plans and limits change often, so check each vendor's own pages before you commit to a workflow.

OpenArt’s AI image generator puts several image models behind one interface. Its generator page lists models including GPT Image 2.5, Grok Imagine, Qwen Image 3, Nano Banana 2 and Seedream 5.0 Pro, and lets you create images from text prompts or reference photos. It also lists tools for camera angle control, upscaling, collages and a Character Builder for keeping characters consistent across scenes, with 4K export.

Best for: Creators who want to compare several models on the same prompt without juggling separate subscriptions and accounts.

Limitations: The page offers a free start but does not state credit amounts or plan details, and it does not address commercial use, so check the pricing and licensing pages before using results for client work. Output quality depends on which model you pick.

OpenAI's image generation in ChatGPT works inside the chat interface, which makes iteration natural: generate an image, then ask for changes in plain language. OpenAI highlights accurate text rendering, the ability to follow prompts with many distinct objects, and consistent characters across multi-turn edits. You can also upload reference images and have the model transform them. It handles both photorealistic and stylized looks.

Best for: Marketers and writers who want to describe, refine and re-describe an image without learning a new tool.

Limitations: OpenAI notes that tall images such as posters can be cropped too tightly, and that dense small text, multilingual text and precise graphs can still go wrong. Images can take up to a minute to render. All generated images carry C2PA metadata identifying their source.

Gemini's image generation runs on Google's Nano Banana models. Google highlights character consistency, text rendering in many languages, combining several photos, precise editing and 2K resolution. A free tier covers image generation and editing, while the most advanced model requires a Google AI plan. You start from the "Create images" option in the Gemini app, then prompt or upload a picture to edit.

Best for: People who need to keep the same subject across several images, or who want to edit existing photos quickly.

Limitations: Usage limits apply, and the strongest model is behind a paid plan. Images include an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible AI label, which is good for transparency but may not suit every use.

Midjourney is an independent research lab known for images with a strong, painterly look. Its official site points users to a sign-up flow and to a Discord community and help center for support. Many creators choose it for concept art, mood boards and illustration where style matters more than literal accuracy.

Best for: Artists, art directors and game designers who want rich, stylized results and enjoy exploring variations.

Limitations: I could not confirm current pricing, trial terms or feature details from Midjourney's public pages, so check them yourself before subscribing. The workflow can feel less like a normal app than the others on this list, and it is not the easiest route for precise, text-heavy graphics.

Adobe Firefly is built for professionals who already work in Creative Cloud. Adobe states that its Firefly models are commercially safe, trained on licensed Adobe Stock and public domain content, and outputs carry Content Credentials that show AI involvement. It offers text-to-image, image-to-image, sketch-to-image and style transfer, plus editing tools such as background removal, object removal, upscaling and generative expand. It connects with Photoshop, Illustrator, Lightroom and Adobe Express, and also lets you use partner models from other companies.

Best for: Designers and brand teams who need clear licensing and want AI inside the apps they already use.

Limitations: Plans work on a credit system, so heavy use adds up. Adobe lists a free tier with limited daily generations and paid plans starting at about $9.99 a month, but confirm current terms on its site.

Ideogram built its reputation on a problem that trips up many generators: readable text. Its current models are presented as producing clean typography in different layouts, with attention to prompt fidelity and reliable editing. It also offers an API with access to a catalog of models, and has released open weights for one earlier model for local use and customization under its license.

Best for: Posters, social graphics, logo concepts and anything where words are part of the image.

Limitations: The pages I checked did not describe a free plan, so look at current plans before relying on it. Like every generator, it still needs a human to proofread the text it produces.

Start with the job. If you want to compare several models side by side, try OpenArt. If you want to talk your way to a finished image, try ChatGPT or Gemini. If you need images that will sit inside a branded design workflow, Firefly is the safer fit. If you are chasing a distinctive look, Midjourney deserves a trial. If text must be perfect, test Ideogram.

Run the same prompt in two or three tools before paying. Differences in composition, hands, faces and text show up quickly, and they matter more than any feature list.

Reviews only go so far, because your own use case is what counts. Pick one realistic job, for example a 16:9 header image for a blog post about web performance, and run the same prompt through two or three tools. Write the prompt once and paste it unchanged, so the comparison is fair:

Then score each result on five questions: Did it follow every instruction? Are there odd details in hands, edges or objects? Is any text spelled correctly? Does it still look good when cropped to a square? Could you license it for your project? Ten minutes of this teaches more than any feature list, and it shows how much editing each tool needs before you can publish.

Most readers of this guide will put these images on web pages, where a few habits matter more than the generator you choose.

There is no single best AI image generator. The right pick depends on whether you value conversation, style, licensing, control or typography. Test two or three on a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final choice. Whatever you generate, review it for errors and label AI-made images clearly where your audience would expect it.

Are AI-generated images free to use commercially? It depends on the provider and your plan. Read the licensing terms for the tool you use, and consider vendors that publish how their models were trained if you work for clients.

Which AI image generator is best for text in images? Ideogram and ChatGPT both highlight text rendering, and Gemini lists it as a feature. Test each with your own wording and proofread the result.

Do AI images have watermarks? Some do. OpenAI adds C2PA metadata, Google adds an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible label, and Adobe attaches Content Credentials.

Can I use more than one image model without separate accounts? Yes. Platforms such as OpenArt put several models in one interface, which makes side-by-side testing easier. Check the licensing terms for each model you use.

Should I add alt text to AI-generated images? Yes, when the image carries meaning. Describe what it shows or why it is there. Purely decorative images can use an empty alt attribute, following the W3C guidance.

#10566. Google’s original Nest Doorbell is broken for many right now

An issue with Google’s original Nest Doorbell has left live view of the video feed broken for many owners, and there’s no word on a fix just yet.

Users are reporting widely in the past few days that Live View video feeds from Nest Doorbells are broken in the Google Home app. The issue is affecting specifically the first-gen Nest Doorbell, which is best known as “Nest Hello,” but Google has been referring to it as “Nest Doorbell (wired, 1st gen).”

The issue has popped up in several Reddit threads in recent weeks, but seems to have spread much more widely in the past few days based on nearly a dozen Google support threads we found.

Some users also report that some first-gen Nest cameras are also not working properly, but we couldn’t find nearly as many reports of this.

Notably, Google did roll out an update to older Nest hardware at the end of October.

I'm so glad to see this article because I've been complaining and complaining to the void about this for weeks now. Google is taking NO responsibility to fix what they obviously broke. Internet searches say that I'm supposed to just upgrade the power supply but that makes no sense.

Interestingly, affected users say that Nest Doorbell notifications on Nest Hub displays are still working properly. Some have deduced that Google’s switch to WebRTC may be the cause of the problem, but it’s hard to say for certain seeing as workarounds based on that theory don’t reliably work.

Outside of generic troubleshooting messages, Google has yet to say anything about a fix, or even confirm there’s an issue. But, based on the number of reports, it’s very obvious there’s a problem on Google’s end.

We’ll update this post as more details come to light but, for now, sound off in the comments below if you’re running into this issue.
