New firmware might prevent more bricked cards, but ASRock isn't saying for sure

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

ASRock has rolled out a new firmware update for its Radeon RX 7000-series graphics cards. The latest VBIOS version 1.0.6 appears to be a follow-up to the previous 1.0.5 release. According to community reports, that older version was bricking some of the company's Radeon RX 7900 XT and RX 7900 XTX graphics cards. The new update has now quietly made its way to the ASRock official website, although the company has not published a changelog or provided any details on what the new update addresses.

Last month, AMD released a VBIOS patch for Radeon RX 7000-series graphics cards to address potential security vulnerabilities. While the company did not disclose any technical details about the vulnerabilities, it assured gamers that there had been no reports of them being exploited. According to a statement shared with ComputerBase, the company provided the following rationale for the update:

"AMD is releasing a firmware update as part of our usual approach to address potential security vulnerabilities. These corrections are already made available before the public announcement to give customers time to update their firmware. As a best practice, we recommend that users update the firmware of their graphics cards as soon as they are provided by the respective GPU manufacturer. There are no reports of exploiting these security vulnerabilities in practice."

ASRock subsequently listed the 1.0.5 VBIOS update for 10 graphics card models, spanning its Radeon RX 7600 XT, RX 7700 XT, RX 7800 XT, RX 7900 XT, and RX 7900 XTX families. However, several Radeon RX 7900 XT and RX 7900 XTX owners reported that their systems failed to boot after installing the firmware. Some lucky users were able to use external EEPROM programmers to recover their cards. Although ASRock has since removed the 1.0.5 update from its website, it has yet to provide clarity on what caused the failures or confirm whether the newly released VBIOS addresses the issue.

There are also unanswered questions around how ASRock plans to help customers who have already been affected. According to reports shared with VideoCardz, a user in Japan received a confirmation from ASRock that the failed update would be covered under warranty. However, another owner in Europe was reportedly directed to seek warranty service despite having an out-of-warranty graphics card. This essentially leaves affected owners uncertain of whether they will receive free repairs or replacements.

Additionally, the lack of a changelog or an official statement, along with the new firmware version, makes it difficult for users to determine whether installing the replacement firmware is safe. We can only hope that ASRock provides some clarity around what version 1.06

#10561. Which MCM Has the Best Revshare? A Developer’s Guide to Evaluating Ad Partners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An ad partner’s revenue share looks easy to compare: keeping 90% sounds better than keeping 80%. But the percentage does not tell a developer what will happen when the integration reaches production. Extra JavaScript, unstable ad slots, opaque reporting, and difficult rollbacks can change the value of an apparently attractive deal.

For teams maintaining an advertising-funded website, choosing a Multiple Customer Management (MCM) partner is partly an engineering decision. The integration affects page templates, consent handling, third-party requests, account access, and monitoring. Those changes deserve the same scrutiny as a new analytics SDK or payment integration.

This guide explains how to compare revenue shares on a consistent basis, review integration requirements, and test an ad setup without sacrificing usability. The goal is a repeatable evaluation process, rather than a universal winner.

Google Ad Manager’s MCM system lets a parent publisher manage a child publisher’s account or delegated inventory. The commercial agreement defines the partner’s services and compensation. Before comparing offers, ask which revenue streams the quoted percentage covers and whether the reporting figure is before or after that fee.

Use three separate lines in your evaluation spreadsheet:

Revenue base: record the amount to which the partner’s percentage applies. Advertiser spending, exchange-reported revenue, and publisher earnings are different figures. Do not treat them as interchangeable.

Partner compensation: identify the percentage, fixed charges, and any optional services. Ask whether the same terms apply to every demand source and ad format.

Adjustments: distinguish estimated revenue from finalized earnings, including invalid-traffic deductions and other corrections. Document when adjustments appear and which party bears them.

A common mistake is applying the partner fee twice because a dashboard already displays net revenue. Label each exported metric before using it in a comparison.

The two delegation models have different implications for access, reporting, and payments. Google’s documentation is the starting point for checking the proposed setup.

Manage Account gives the parent edit access within the child’s account while the child retains access and permissions. Payment facilitation can allocate eligible earnings from the child’s primary Ad Exchange account, but it does not automatically cover every third-party revenue stream. Confirm the invitation’s payment percentage and the separate commercial agreement.

Manage Inventory lets the parent manage delegated inventory in its own account, without access to the child account. Reporting for that inventory sits in the parent account. Google pays the parent for the relevant inventory, and the parent pays the child according to their agreement. Ask how you will obtain reports and reconcile payments.

Before implementation, document who can change tags, ad units, consent settings, and demand configuration. Agree on access for debugging and exporting historical data. A developer should be able to trace a production request to its configuration owner.

Use this table as a request-for-information checklist. It does not rank providers or assert that any named service supports a particular implementation. Obtain current terms and technical documentation for the product you are considering.

Apply the same standards to every proposal. The following examples show what a developer should ask during evaluation; each question is relevant across providers.

MonetizeMore offers publisher monetization products, including PubGuru and Traffic Cop. When evaluating its proposal, connect the commercial discussion to concrete integration questions: which scripts will load, who owns the configuration, and how will reported earnings be reconciled?

Request a sample report showing the revenue base, partner fee, and deductions as separate fields. Check whether the proposed plan includes every service shown in the sales demonstration.

Ask for a written ownership map covering Ad Manager access, tag deployment, configuration changes, and incident response. Identify who can restore the previous setup if delivery fails.

During a trial, compare representative templates and devices. Record script requests and main-thread activity alongside net revenue, using the same measurement definitions for the existing setup.

Decision criterion: choose on measured results and documented responsibilities, rather than assuming that a quoted split predicts final earnings.

For any proposal involving a header-bidding wrapper, including one from Setupad, ask how the wrapper fits into your existing page lifecycle.

Review bidder configuration, auction timeouts, and when the ad server receives targeting information. A missing bid and an unfilled ad slot are different problems.

Check whether your team can inspect the active configuration and reproduce an issue on a test page. Agree on who approves changes to bidders or timeout values.

Measure the complete sequence: consent readiness, auction start, bid responses, ad request, and rendering. Avoid attributing every delay to the same component.

Decision criterion: the implementation should be observable enough to explain failures, with commercial terms verified separately.

When assessing Publisher Collective or another managed partner, establish how the proposed setup could be tested on a limited, agreed scope.

Ask whether rollout can be restricted to selected templates or an eligible traffic cohort. Do not assume partial testing is available until the partner confirms it.

Record device, geography, page type, and consent state so differences in traffic mix do not masquerade as a revenue improvement.

Keep a dated deployment log connecting each configuration version with the reporting period.

Decision criterion: a measured comparison should separate implementation changes from changes in audience or demand.

A proposal from AdPushup or another optimization provider may involve changes to ad placement. Review the actual proposed changes with both design and engineering.

Check spacing, responsive behavior, keyboard navigation, and whether sticky placements obscure content or controls.

Reserve space for expected ad dimensions and test what happens when no ad is returned.

Compare layout stability before and after the change.

Decision criterion: additional earnings should be assessed alongside usability and performance, with explicit acceptance criteria.

For an Ezoic proposal, as for any managed platform, start with the exact integration method offered to your site.

List required changes to scripts, plugins, caching, DNS, or delivery infrastructure, if any. Do not assume that every deployment uses the same method.

Test interactions with your consent manager, content security policy, caching rules, and existing analytics.

Document how to remove the integration and restore the prior configuration.

Decision criterion: the operational cost should be visible before the first production rollout.

When reviewing a Monumetric proposal, establish which implementation work belongs to your team and which belongs to the provider.

Ask who creates ad slots, updates templates, and validates responsive sizes.

Request a support escalation process with the diagnostic information required for an incident: URL, timestamp, device, consent state, and affected slot.

Confirm onboarding costs and payment terms in writing.

Decision criterion: routine changes and production failures should have a clear owner.

Chrome DevTools helps trace third-party requests in the Network panel and investigate long tasks in the Performance panel. Capture a baseline before deployment, then repeat the same navigation and interaction sequence after deployment. Use comparable device and network settings.

Google Publisher Console helps investigate Google Publisher Tag ad slots and delivery issues. For a Prebid-based setup, use Prebid’s debugging tools to inspect the auction path. These tools answer different questions: whether the auction behaved as expected and whether the ad server delivered the requested slot.

PageSpeed Insights provides performance diagnostics; distinguish its lab results from available field data. Add web-vitals to your own monitoring when you need measurements associated with a deployment or cohort. Track LCP, INP, and CLS alongside revenue instead of treating a single Lighthouse score as the complete user experience.

Reserve responsive space for ad slots before content arrives, and investigate shifts when empty slots collapse. SitePoint’s guide to web performance optimization explains related techniques for loading JavaScript, reducing layout work, and managing long tasks.

With the same traffic volume, revenue definition, and deduction rate, the break-even increase in pre-partner revenue is: (current publisher share ÷ new publisher share) − 1. Express shares consistently as fractions or percentages. The table below shows the required lift; it is not a forecast these thresholds only describe the arithmetic. Different ad density, audience mix, deduction rates, or reporting bases can invalidate the comparison. Normalize to the same denominator, such as revenue per thousand pageviews, and compare matched periods or properly assigned concurrent cohorts.

Assume two hypothetical setups each receive one million pageviews. Here, RPM means revenue per thousand pageviews before the partner fee. For this simplified example, deductions reduce that revenue before the publisher share is applied.

Partner A: $10 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.90 after deductions × 0.90 publisher share = $8,100 net revenue.

Partner B: $12 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.98 after deductions × 0.80 publisher share = $9,408 net revenue.

The difference is $1,308 for the period despite Partner B’s lower share. These are illustrative inputs, not provider performance claims. Faster payment improves cash-flow timing; it does not, by itself, increase the revenue earned.

Use Playwright with mocked responses or test creatives in a controlled environment to check slow loading, empty slots, blocked scripts, and navigation between routes. Verify that the page remains usable when the ad system fails. Automated tests should not click live advertisements or generate artificial monetized traffic.

Separate unit tests for configuration logic, integration tests for component boundaries, and browser tests for user-visible behavior. SitePoint’s explanation of unit, functional, and integration testing provides a useful foundation. Define rollback criteria before launch, keep the control configuration available, and review finalized revenue alongside performance data before expanding the deployment.

There is no universal answer from the percentage alone. Compare current written offers using the same revenue base, then evaluate finalized net earnings, performance, access, and implementation effort.

Not necessarily. Reports may differ in timezone, currency, transaction coverage, fee treatment, and finalization date. Reconcile those definitions before treating a discrepancy as an integration bug.

Ask how renewals, volume tiers, optional services, and rate changes are handled. Record the applicable terms with the configuration and reporting period used for your evaluation.

No. If deductions and the revenue base are otherwise comparable, an 80% share needs 12.5% more pre-partner revenue to match a 90% share. Measure whether that lift exists rather than assuming it.

Do not treat any protection claim as a guarantee. Ask how suspected invalid traffic is handled, what evidence is available, and how later adjustments are reflected in your reports and payments.

Choose the setup that produces verifiable net value while meeting the site’s performance and operational requirements. A good evaluation leaves your team with reproducible measurements, understandable reports, and a practical rollback plan—not just a favorable percentage.

#10559. Google Messages homepage redesign: Pinned chat avatars, bottom search, corner Gemini

In addition to the new app icon, Google Messages for Android is testing a big homepage redesign that has three core components.

Update 11/7: Instead of pinned chats just appearing at the top of the list, Google Messages is trying a redesign that significantly elevates them above all conversations.

This redesign takes advantage of the existing background layer that’s currently just home to the logo/wordmark, search, and account menu.

You’ll see large contact photos appear above all chats, which pushes the list of other conversations down the page. Like iMessage on iOS, this layout has the downside of not showing any history or last sent information.

Meanwhile, Google is testing another change where search becomes a centered floating action button (FAB) alongside new chat. When you’re scrolling down the page, you’ll see “Search” in a pill-shaped button, with the other button minimized.

Hate the idea of just the pinned "bobble head" look. I like being able to view message tidbits. Plus, it's directly copying Apple (enough said). I have to use an iPhone at my job. I hate the pinned bubble conversation look and more importantly it's function.

Otherwise, you’ll see a circular magnifying glass icon and “Start chat” in the pill. The animation that accompanies this change looks nice and feels alive, while boosting one-handed usability.

Google has replaced the Gemini FAB with a button in the top-right corner next to your profile image. This matches the design leveraged by Workspace apps on desktop and mobile.

These updates are currently in the beta channel and not yet widely rolled out to all testers. Some users have all aspects of the redesign, but it appears that Google is rolling out the various components in a piecemeal fashion.

#10557. Android tablets are back, but are they for you?

Android tablets have always been a bit of a touchy subject, but it’s abundantly clear that the form factor is making a comeback. But, as someone who’s a fan of the smaller sizes, are you actually using the gargantuan Android tablets that every company seems obsessed with? In other words – are you the customer for the Android tablets these brands are selling?

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

For a while there, Android tablets had hit what felt like a dead end. Bigger smartphones and cooling of the tablet market as a whole, along with the general sentiment that Apple was doing a way better job with the iPad anyway, left the category in a place that felt stagnant – and that’s the nice way of putting it.

But, over the past few years, Android tablets have had a renaissance.

New form factors, big releases from a variety of brands, and flagship-tier devices left and right. Despite Android’s push into foldables and still-huge displays on nearly every smartphone, tablets are very much back. Prior to RAMageddon’s worst impacts, tablet sales were growing for nearly everyone, and Android brands were seeing a larger slice of the pie.

Anyway, this is on my mind right now because I’ve had a lot of Android tablets arrive in my office in the past few weeks, capping off a lot of tablet announcements in the past few months. I just finished up with Redmagic Astra 2, a tablet I think fits the description of “Android’s iPad Mini OLED,” and I’m in the process of moving into tablets from Lenovo and Amazon. Our Jordan Floyd has also been testing out the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra behind the scenes. And if there’s one trend amongst recent tablet releases, it’s that big tablets seem to be the primary focus among a lot of brands.

Take Amazon’s new Google Play-equipped Alexa Tablet lineup, for example.

The Alexa Tablet 12 is the crown jewel of the lineup, packing the most powerful specs and a premium anti-glare display. It’s the only one that gets a “Pro” moniker, while the 11-inch and 8-inch models are noticeable steps down. Unboxing the Alexa Tablet 8 this week, for example, I was immediately reminded this is not a premium device – the screen makes that clear without any hesitation whatsoever. It was a case of whiplash after setting up the Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 with its 4K display.

It’s a weird one for me. I’ve never been a big fan of these super-sized Android tablets (or iPads, for that matter). I can get behind a 10-inch or 11-inch model, but I find myself more at home on the smaller slates. They’re fantastic for travel as an alternative to draining my phone’s battery, while I find myself just reaching for a laptop instead if I want a bigger screen. A big tablet, regardless of specs, can rarely replace a proper laptop for me, so lugging both around just sounds like more trouble than it’s worth.

But, clearly, people are buying these bigger slates. Samsung appears to be seeing enough success with its mammoth Galaxy Tab S Ultra lineup to keep the 14.6-inch tablet around each year while the smaller sizes are now on an every-other-year cycle. Lenovo’s lineup is virtually all underpowered when the screen size goes down, aside from the Legion Tab that’s now $900.

Are you buying these bigger tablets? What are you using them for?

I imagine that most folks opting for a bigger tablet are using these in the place of a laptop. Thinner and lighter, longer battery life, etc. And in cases like the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro and its $550~ price tag, I get the appeal. Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 takes that down a notch for me at $799 – you’re getting into laptop territory there – but it still is a compelling-enough alternative if it fits your needs. The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra at $1,400 plus a $200 keyboard, yeah I don’t get that one at all.

Let’s discuss! I want to know if you’re using an Android tablet, what you’re using it for, and what size you actually prefer.

Ladies and gents, we’ve finally got a new flagship in the US. Motorola Signature 27 is launching on November 19 and looks excellent, though its flashy spec sheet won’t come cheap at $1,599.

Finally, something good came out of Googlebook

Googlebook is a disaster, but there’s finally one good thing coming out of it in Henry Cavill’s new “Mustache Protocol” game which, supposedly, was built on Googlebook. I’ll bite my tongue there, but the accompanying video brought a needed bit of joy into my week.

I use an 8" Samsung. Not the best in specs, but fine for browsing and reading books. I had a Nexus 10 years ago and gave it to my sister-in-law as I rarely needed something that big. My wife also uses an 8" Samsung, mostly for reading.

“This is simply not correct at all,” Softbank argued after Elon Musk claimed it had defaulted on a raft of unrequired data center turbines.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

By early 2026, Japanese tech investment outfit SoftBank had reserved more power generation capacity than the entire state of Connecticut after cornering a huge portion of the global gas turbine production capacity. Now, the same company has chosen to take a $3.7 billion hit to its earnings after canceling reservations for the highly sought-after power generation hardware. The WSJ reports that SoftBank had reserved more than 13 gigawatts of power-producing turbines, citing unnamed "people familiar with the orders." That's the equivalent of more than six Hoover Dams' worth of capacity, with SoftBank founder and chief executive Masayoshi Son believing that it would be vital to the company's earning potential moving forward. Notably, this development surfaced after SoftBank's CFO fired back at Elon Musk's recent revelation that the company had defaulted on turbine blade contracts, saying, "there is no such default."

The gas turbines were to power AI data centers, and SoftBank is set to build a 9.2-gigawatt power plant in Ohio, with construction scheduled for early 2027. It'll cost $33 billion to build and will provide power to a neighboring eight-gigawatt data center. That data center will be leased to OpenAI, albeit only after Nvidia entered discussions about guaranteeing around $250 billion of financing for the project.

But SoftBank got ahead of itself. After initially planning to use the turbines for future gas-powered plants, the company has now chosen to change direction. Orders for turbines, engines that convert natural gas into the electricity used by data centers, must be placed years in advance of them being required. The company had bet big, placing huge orders with the expectation that demand would greatly outstrip supply. That would leave SoftBank in an enviable position, holding huge orders for the vital components required as part of a data center rollout boosted by AI demand. But those reservations weren't cheap — turbine manufacturers regularly charge customers tens of millions of dollars per gigawatt.

It's now canceled its remaining reservations with GE Vernova, its turbine supplier, while also unwinding contracts for orders that were too far gone. SoftBank told investors on a recent earnings call that the orders had been placed “to secure power generation equipment in anticipation of shortages,” but it now appears it anticipated a little too much.

Crucially, SoftBank is also keen for everyone to know that this move was voluntary and that it did not default on its orders. That's contrary to what xAI CEO Elon Musk claimed in a post on his X social network. SoftBank Chief Financial Officer Yoshimitsu Goto was quick to counter Musk's claims. “This is simply not correct at all,” Goto reportedly said. “There is no such default.”

#10552. Meta’s Horizon+ looks to become Meta VR+ and include 3D movies

Meta Horizon+ is rumored to be changing its name to Meta VR+ and may start including movies in addition to games with the subscription price.

Meta’s quest (ahem) to distance itself from the Horizon era is in full swing, and the latest rumor says it’s time for the company’s Game Pass-like subscription to change its brand name. VR leaker Nori posted a screenshot on X showing a handful of 3D movies coming to the Meta VR Plus service. Considering there is no such Meta VR Plus service currently, the obvious conclusion is that Meta Horizon+ is being renamed once again.

Meta Horizon+ currently includes over 100 games with its $7.99 per month subscription and is similar to Microsoft’s Xbox Game Pass service in many ways. Unironically, Meta Horizon+ also includes the basic Xbox Game Pass tier with over 50 Xbox games playable via the Game Pass cloud. Meta offers a 3-month trial with every new Meta Quest purchase.

The new Meta VR+ service is purported to include 3D movies as well, although we don’t yet know if this will be included in the regular $7.99 per month rate, or if Meta will be introducing additional content tiers, as services like Netflix, Disney+, Xbox Game Pass, and others do. Based on the screenshot, these will be big-name movies and are likely positioned to enhance the value of the upcoming $1,300 Meta VR Glasses, although all the same content will work on existing Meta Quest 3 units.

Meta has been slowly distancing itself from the “Horizon” name ever since the beginning of this year, when its VR division got a big reset. In a February blog post, Samantha Ryan, the VP of Content at Meta’s Reality Labs, spoke about the changes to the company’s strategy, including the loss of most in-house VR gaming studios. Since then, several prominent VR developers from Meta have gone on to create independent studios, including the super-popular Supernatural exercise app.

Meta then decoupled the unpopular Horizon Metaverse from its headsets, moving it to mobile-only, and has been removing the “Horizon” name from just about everything since then. Meta Horizon+ looks to be the latest big change, as the company moves toward “Meta VR” branding, which is clearer and separates its VR division from mobile and smart glasses efforts.

#10548. Top 6 AI Video Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A year ago, most AI video clips lasted a few seconds, had no sound and drifted off-model by the final frame. Today several tools produce 1080p or even 4K clips with synchronized audio, camera control and reference-based consistency. The market also moves quickly: OpenAI announced it was shutting down Sora in March 2026, so the list of tools worth using has changed.

This guide compares six AI video generators that developers, designers and marketers can use today. For each one you will see what it does well, where it falls short and who it suits. Plans, credits and model names change often, so confirm details on each vendor’s site before you commit.

OpenArt’s AI video generator puts a range of video models behind one interface. Its page lists models such as Veo 3.1, Kling, Seedance, Wan, Hailuo and PixVerse, so you can run the same prompt on different engines and compare the results without managing separate accounts.

Beyond text-to-video and image-to-video, it offers start and end frame control, video-to-video restyling, motion transfer from a reference clip, a character builder for consistency, lip sync and conversational editing for tasks such as swapping a background or relighting a scene. Resolution goes up to 4K on many models, with 1080p as the default, and clips are usually up to 15 seconds, with longer limits on a few models.

Strengths: model variety, a single credit pool, editing tools alongside generation and a shared library for your clips.

Limits: credit use varies by model, so it is worth testing short clips first. Commercial-use rights depend on your plan and on the model you pick, so check the pricing and terms pages.

Best for: creators and small teams that want to compare models quickly instead of committing to one.

Veo is Google DeepMind’s video model. Veo 3.1 generates sound effects, ambient noise and dialogue together with the video, supports image-to-video, first and last frame transitions, reference images for characters or objects, and outputs 1080p and 4K. You can reach it through the Gemini app, Google Flow and the Gemini API.

Strengths: audio generated with the picture, strong prompt adherence and an API that fits into developer workflows.

Limits: base clips are short, around 8 seconds, so longer scenes rely on scene extension. Dialogue quality in short segments is still being refined, and access and quotas depend on your Google plan.

Best for: developers and marketers who want audio and video from one prompt, or who plan to automate generation through an API.

Runway has been a staple of AI filmmaking since its early Gen models. Its pricing page lists Gen-4.5 and Aleph alongside partner models, and the paid plans include all video and image models, 4K upscaling and parallel generations. Higher tiers add features such as voice clones, brand kits and HDR or ProRes output.

Strengths: polished editing tools, video-to-video workflows and a professional output pipeline that suits post-production teams.

Limits: credits are consumed quickly on premium models, and on the lower paid plans they do not roll over from month to month.

Best for: video editors and agencies that need control over shots and want AI to fit into an existing editing process.

Kling from Kuaishou has built a reputation for natural motion and expressive characters. The current release, Kling 4.0, sits alongside tools such as Motion Control, a canvas workspace, an agent and MCP or CLI batch generation for teams that want to automate production.

Strengths: realistic movement, motion transfer and batch tools for producing many short clips.

Limits: the main site does not spell out all clip-length and resolution limits for each model, so check the model page you plan to use. Pricing and free credits vary by region and plan.

Best for: social video, product shots and character-driven clips where motion quality is the priority.

Luma’s Dream Machine is built on the Ray model family. Its current page lists Ray3.2 and Ray3.14, along with a “Modify video” tool that restyles footage, extends duration and reframes aspect ratios. It also lists camera-motion controls and high-quality EXR export for professional pipelines.

Strengths: reshooting or restyling existing footage, camera-motion control and pipeline-friendly export.

Limits: paid plans start at around $30 per month (or less when billed yearly), and heavy use pushes you into the higher tiers.

Best for: filmmakers and designers who want to modify and extend footage rather than only generate new clips.

Adobe Firefly offers text-to-video and image-to-video with Adobe’s own Firefly Video Model. It also provides partner models, including Google Veo 3.1, Luma Ray3, Kling and Runway Gen-4.5, so you can compare outputs from inside one product. An Assembler and AI video editor let you trim, reorder, extend and add voiceovers, music and sound effects.

Adobe states that the Firefly Video Model is trained on licensed content such as Adobe Stock and public domain material, and describes its output as safe for commercial use. Partner models may carry different terms, so read the details for whichever model you choose.

Strengths: a clear licensing story for the native model, integration with Adobe’s creative apps and an included editor.

Limits: most clips run up to 8 seconds, with some models reaching 15. Credits are allocated monthly, and premium partner models can use them faster.

Best for: brand and in-house teams that care about provenance and already work in Adobe tools.

The table summarizes what each vendor’s own pages say at the time of writing. “Entry paid plan” figures are US prices billed annually where the vendor offers that option.

Credit counts are hard to compare, so convert them to clips. Runway’s pricing page lists Gen-4.5 at 60 credits per 5-second clip, which means the 625 monthly credits on the Standard plan cover roughly ten clips. Adobe says its Standard plan’s 2,000 credits equal up to 40 five-second videos, and OpenArt’s Starter plan lists about 50 videos for 4,000 credits. Premium models cost more: the same Runway page lists Aleph 2.0 at 140 credits per 5 seconds.

Plan for waste. If you keep one usable clip out of every three attempts, your real cost per finished shot is about three times the sticker price. Test with short, low-resolution drafts, then render the winner at full quality.

Because every tool outputs short clips, the real work is assembly. A typical workflow is: write a shot list, generate two or three takes per shot, pick the best, normalize them to one format, join them and add captions and audio. FFmpeg handles the joining step from the command line:

Matching resolution, frame rate and codec before joining avoids glitches at the cut points. For character consistency across shots, reuse the same reference image in every prompt and keep descriptions of the subject identical.

If the clip ends up on your own site, treat it like any other media. Provide a poster image, load metadata only until the visitor presses play, and include captions so deaf and hard-of-hearing users, and anyone watching without sound, can follow along. The W3C’s guidance on captions and subtitles explains what to include, and the MDN video element reference covers the attributes below.

Also write a text description or transcript near the video. Search engines and screen reader users cannot see the footage, so the surrounding text is what explains it.

Short, structured prompts beat long, vague ones. Describe the elements in the order a director would:

Change one element at a time between takes so you can tell which change improved the result.

Marketing demos show the best of a tool, so run your own test. Use the same prompt, duration and aspect ratio on each tool, generate a few takes and score them against a short checklist. Keep notes on how many credits each usable clip costs.

Run the same test again with an image-to-video input if your workflow starts from product photos or illustrations. That often exposes differences in consistency that text prompts hide.

Which AI video generator is best? There is no single winner. Veo is strong for audio, Runway for editing, Kling for motion, Luma for modifying footage and Firefly for licensing clarity. A multi-model tool such as OpenArt helps you compare them.

Can AI video generators make long videos? Not in one pass. Most models create short clips, so longer videos are built by extending or stitching clips together.

Is Sora still available? OpenAI announced in March 2026 that it was shutting down the Sora app and API, so it is not a safe choice for new projects.

Can I use AI videos commercially? Often, but it depends on the plan and the underlying model. Read the current terms before publishing.

How do I keep a character consistent across clips? Reuse the same reference image, keep the subject description identical in every prompt, and use character or reference tools where the platform offers them.

Should I add captions to AI-generated video? Yes. Captions help accessibility and viewers watching without sound, and they give search engines text to index.

Do I need a powerful computer? No. These services run in the cloud, so a browser and a stable connection are enough.

The best AI video generator depends on what you make. Start with a short test prompt, compare a few tools, and keep the one that gives you usable clips at a sensible credit cost. If you are not sure where to begin, a multi-model option such as OpenArt lets you try several engines before you settle on one.

#10546. SpaceX develops its own ‘Kessel’ rack-scale system featuring 72 Nvidia Rubin GPUs for data centers in space, report claims

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

SpaceX/xAI is developing a custom version of Nvidia's Vera Rubin NVL72 rack-scale AI system that replaces conventional NVLink cable assemblies with a massive 84-layer printed circuit board (PCB), in part for use in orbital data centers, reports SemiAnalysis. The system is codenamed Kessel and retains 36 Vera CPUs, 72 Rubin GPUs, and nine NVLink switch trays, but introduces a different solid-state interconnect architecture. While SpaceX/xAI will continue to buy Nvidia hardware directly from the company, Elon Musk's company will source other components independently, which could give it greater control over its hardware supply chain.

The Kessel system retains the fundamental configuration of Nvidia's Vera Rubin NVL72 platform, which comprises 72 GPU packages distributed across 18 compute trays and nine NVLink switch trays. However, instead of Nvidia's conventional arrangement with internal NVLink cable cartridges, SpaceX plans to position compute and switch trays back-to-back around a passive PCB midplane. The architecture resembles a smaller version of Nvidia's Kyber design with 576 AI GPUs (albeit in 144 packages), which also relies on a PCB-based interconnect rather than conventional cable assemblies, SemiAnalysis notes. Interestingly, the design is aimed at both terrestrial and orbital deployments, according to the analysts.

Arguably the most distinctive feature of Kessel is its 84-layer PCB midplane, which uses ultra-low-loss M10 copper-clad laminates (CCLs). By contrast, Nvidia's Kyber architecture relied on a backplane based on a considerably more complex 156-layer PCB featuring M9 CCLs. The reduction in layer count by nearly half could substantially improve manufacturability and production yields, but the more advanced copper-clad laminate will almost certainly increase material costs. In any case, an 84-layer PCB is a device that is exceptionally hard to build.

The move to a PCB midplane could simplify system assembly by eliminating numerous high-speed cable connections, but it would not necessarily reduce manufacturing costs. An 84-layer PCB that relies on ultra-low-loss M10 laminate is an exceptionally expensive component that requires sophisticated manufacturing processes and precise quality control. To put an 84-layer PCB number into context, modern high-end server motherboards use 15 – 20 layers, whereas server development boards use more than 20 layers to virtually eliminate any electrical issues with the board itself. Furthermore, manufacturing yields of an 84-layer PCB could become a major obstacle, as defects in internal layers may render an entire board unusable. Consequently, the economic viability of Kessel will depend on whether savings from cable elimination and independent component procurement can offset the cost of its complex midplane.

Perhaps more importantly, Kessel represents a departure from Nvidia's conventional system-level procurement model. Instead of purchasing Nvidia's assembled L6 compute boards or even L10 trays that Nvidia plans to sell with its Vera Rubin generation, SpaceX reportedly plans to acquire AI accelerators, CPUs, switches, and networking components directly from Nvidia and procure other components from third-party suppliers (one can wonder how easy it will be for SpaceX/xAI to procure memory amid today's shortages). This would enable SpaceX to select its own PCB manufacturers, component vendors, and manufacturing partners, optimize its hardware for its infrastructure (which will be important for orbital deployments), and perhaps reduce intermediary costs. Meanwhile, the company would assume greater responsibility for system design, validation, manufacturing quality, and supply-chain management, which is something that most hyperscalers have been doing in recent years.

For Nvidia, the implications are less straightforward. Although direct sales of CPU and AI GPU packages could reduce the company's revenue from complete compute-board assemblies, the arrangement could also encourage other large customers to develop their own Nvidia-based systems and eventually deploy more Nvidia-based machines, something that may be important in a world where custom silicon developed by hyperscalers is rapidly gaining traction. However, to make this happen, Kessel must be a smashing success that will offer clear advantages over systems designed by Nvidia.

#10540. The indie game community is in disarray over AI and Big Walk

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Big Walk’s developers said that they used AI ‘sparingly’ to help work through ‘difficult technical challenges.’

Big Walk’s developers said that they used AI ‘sparingly’ to help work through ‘difficult technical challenges.’

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Some Independent Games Festival (IGF) jurors published a statement yesterday saying they would not vote for games that use generative AI in “any capacity.” Soon after, the developer of indie hit Big Walk has published a statement saying it “sparingly” used AI in the “final stretch” of the game’s development to “help work through difficult technical challenges and bugs.”

According to the jurors’ statement, a copy of which was posted by Austin Walker, while this year’s IGF entries have been “incredible,” there has been “a significant increase” in the number of games “developed with generative AI tools,” leading to “fairly intense discussion and disagreement” with no “universal consensus.” The statement includes a list of beliefs, including that generative AI has “negative effects,” that AI-generated code is “not an exception to these harms,” and that use of AI tools is “not a forgone conclusion in our industry.”

IGF jurors that signed include Telling Lies and Immortality creator Sam Barlow and Celeste composer Lena Raine. The jurors say that the statement doesn’t speak for IGF as an organization or for all IGF jury members, noting that some voiced concern about being able to safely sign “without professional blowback.”

One contentious title was apparently Big Walk, the co-op adventure game from Untitled Goose Game developer House House. In a statement to Aftermath and posted on its website, House House said that “In some cases, snippets from LLM responses were copied as a starting point to be modified into game code—we’d estimate that well under 0.1% of Big Walk’s code had anything to do with this usage, and generative AI was not used in any other context when making the game.”

House House said its “primary use case” of LLMs was “to research, via chatbots, issues we encountered with third-party code,” including fixing an issue with shadows by modifying LLM-suggested code. House House noted it turned to the “LLM chatbot” after researching the problem “extensively” and even speaking to an engineer at Unity.

The studio said it disclosed its AI usage as part of its IGF submission, though it has not disclosed AI usage on Big Walk’s Steam page because “the platform does not request disclosure of code or chatbot usage.”

“This is not an endorsement of, or argument for, the use of AI tools, however minimal,” House House said. “We have some concern that voluntary disclosures like this might contribute to the normalisation of this technology, or its treatment as ‘inevitable’. We support the work of those who are trying to limit the harm generative AI is doing to videogames and our world, and apologise that Big Walk’s LLM usage may not meet their standards.”

House House didn’t have an AI policy in place when it developed Big Walk, which it described as a “failure,” and said it will “work to have a more comprehensive policy going forwards.”

Barlow said after we published this post that Big Walk was not the “impetus” for the IGF’s discussion, but it became the “tricky edge case — well what about this minimal use of AI, what about when your favorite game, or team uses it?” Barlow added that “This is not a call to boycott a certain game, or to economically hurt a team — this was about a single award’s show of many and what that award should mean to reflect our ‘indie’ alternative to mainstream and larger scale game development.” Walker also shared a long blog post that’s worth reading about his position on AI and the IGF awards.

Update, November 10th: Added posts from Barlow and Walker.

#10537. 7 E-commerce UX Tips for Selling Car Accessories and Home & Kitchen Products Online

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Here is a question a product page should answer in about five seconds: “Will this magnetic phone mount hold my phone with the case still on?” When the answer is hard to find, shoppers tend to open another tab instead of contacting support.

Stores that sell both car accessories and home & kitchen products face an extra challenge: two very different catalogs share one site. A car accessories buyer cares about fit, mounting and compatibility. A home & kitchen buyer cares about size, capacity and how easy the product is to clean. A single generic product page frustrates both.

This guide covers seven practical e-commerce UX tips that designers and developers can apply right away, with code snippets and a checklist at the end.

A car mount and a vegetable peeler should not share the same spec table. Keep a single reusable product page component and change only the fields based on category. The layout stays consistent, while each shopper sees the details they need.

In code, this can be a config object that your template reads from:

Showing “Not specified” instead of hiding a missing field is a small habit with a real benefit: it makes gaps in your product data visible to your own team.

Shoppers look for deal-breaker information near the price and the purchase button: fit, size and compatibility. Do not bury it in an “Additional information” tab halfway down the page.

A short comparison table also works better than three paragraphs of marketing copy, because shoppers scan rather than read. Baymard Institute’s product page research consistently finds that unclear or hard-to-find specifications are a common reason shoppers hesitate.

A responsive layout is the starting point, not the finish line. People compare car accessories in a parking lot and browse kitchen gadgets while standing in the kitchen, often with one thumb.

A phone mount on a white background says nothing about how it attaches to a dashboard. Show it in a real car, from the driver’s point of view. For home & kitchen products, place the item next to something familiar, like a coffee mug, so people can judge its size.

Good alt text matters for accessibility and for image search. Compare “phone mount” with “Magnetic phone mount fixed to a car dashboard, holding a phone in landscape mode”. The W3C alt text decision tree is a useful guide for deciding what to describe.

Keep images fast, too. Responsive sources with srcset and sizes, explicit dimensions and lazy loading help prevent layout shift and slow pages:

One caveat: do not lazy-load the main product image at the top of the page. It is usually the Largest Contentful Paint element, so web.dev advises loading it eagerly.

Shoppers often search for a problem rather than a product. Few people type “seat gap filler” the first time; they type “stuff falling between car seats”. Your search, synonyms and category names should handle both.

Mixed catalogs show why this matters. Stores that cover both vehicle and household products, such as VNSPORT.org with its separate product collections, keep the two audiences apart so that someone looking for a dashboard mount never has to scroll past kitchen tools. The principle applies to any store with more than one audience: group by the shopper’s intent, not by your supplier list.

Decorative “100% secure” badges do little. What builds trust is information shoppers can check for themselves:

At checkout, show the full cost, including shipping and taxes, before payment, let people fix mistakes easily and offer guest checkout. In Baymard’s checkout usability research, unexpected extra costs and forced account creation are among the top reasons shoppers abandon a cart.

If your support inbox gets the same question every week, the product page has a gap. Answer it on the page, close to where the decision happens, instead of on a generic help page three clicks away.

You can mark up those answers with FAQPage structured data:

A reality check: since 2023, Google shows FAQ rich results only for well-known government and health websites. Do not add FAQs just to chase a snippet. Add them because they answer real questions and help shoppers decide.

What are the most important e-commerce UX tips for a car accessories store? Show compatibility and mounting details next to the price, use in-car photos, and make sure the mobile layout works with one hand.

How should product pages differ for car accessories and home & kitchen products? Use the same layout but different spec fields. Car accessories need fit, mounting and power details. Home & kitchen products need dimensions, capacity, materials and cleaning instructions.

How big should buttons be on a mobile product page? WCAG 2.2 sets a minimum target size of 24 × 24 CSS pixels. For primary actions such as “Add to cart”, around 44 pixels is more comfortable.

Do FAQ sections still help SEO? Mostly indirectly. FAQ rich results are now limited, but clear on-page answers can match long-tail searches, reduce returns and keep shoppers on the page.

How can I reduce cart abandonment? Show total costs early, offer guest checkout, display delivery times and return terms before payment, and keep the checkout form short.

Good e-commerce UX is not about clever animations. It is about answering the shopper’s question before they have to ask it. Clear specs, honest photos, thumb-friendly controls and real FAQs turn an ordinary product page into one that helps people buy, whether they are shopping for car accessories or home & kitchen essentials.

"Logi Game Day" is available to everyone from full-time workers to interns, and they can play something else if GTA 6 isn't their style.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As GTA 6, the most anticipated game of the year, perhaps the decade, nears its November 19 release date, one company has offered its employees the chance to take the day off to play it. Amid the expectation that gamers around the globe will call in sick to play the game instead of heading into the office, PC peripheral and hardware outfit Logitech is getting ahead of the game by giving 7,000 employees the chance to take a day off to play it. Or, if they prefer, they can play any other game instead, the company confirmed in a press release detailing the move.

The new initiative is called "Logi Game Day," and it isn't only available to the top brass. Interns and full-time employees alike can take a day off from now until this time next year, at a time when it's been estimated that the GTA 6 launch could see the U.S. economy take a $1 billion hit in terms of productivity. The game, which is a console exclusive ahead of a claimed early 2027 PC release, is already available for preorder, with pre-loading also up and running. It's a good job, too, with a 212GB PS5 download set to be a real downer for eager gamers with slow internet. The ongoing price hikes also mean that upgrading your PS5 storage for GTA 6 won't be a fun time, either.

As for Logitech, the company believes that it's time for people to move away from the belief that playing games is a waste of time, hence its new initiative. It makes sense for a company that makes gaming accessories to say that, of course, but CEO Hanneke Faber argues that the company sees "how engagement in one sphere supports performance in the other.” The hope will be that, by allowing employees to play games, a productivity boost will follow.

Whether that comes to fruition remains to be seen. But there's less doubt about GTA 6's popularity a little more than a week out from its release. If you've yet to get your order in, you can also grab Grand Theft Auto VI for $79.99 now. You'll get a box with a code that you'll need to redeem on your PS5 to be able to download and play the game.

The game's release brings an end to years of rumors and leaks, not to mention legal battles. Take-Two hit Microsoft with a subpoena in relation to a massive leak earlier this year in an attempt to find the perpetrator. Rockstar Games addressed the leaks in a post on the X social network shortly after the subpoena was made public, saying that it "would be an understatement to say that having videos of Grand Theft Auto VI gameplay leak in this way has been heartbreaking for our team."

#10531. Samsung bets that you’ll either buy a Galaxy S27 Pro or Ultra next year

Samsung is teeing up to kick off 2027 with an all-new lineup of flagship smartphones, and despite rising prices at your local Best Buy, the brand is still hedging that you’re going to pony up for the most premium model available.

As reported by ETNews, Samsung is currently allotting over 70 percent of its total production capabilities to just the Galaxy S27 Ultra and the new Galaxy S27 “Pro” model expected to launch alongside it. That leaves the plain Galaxy S27 and the larger S27+ on the outside looking in. In comparison to this year’s launch, the Galaxy S26 Ultra made up about 50 percent of this series’ production volume, for a grand total of 18 million units.

Historical performance implies Samsung is probably correct here. While big displays are typically what consumers are drawn to, it does appear that the S27 Pro won’t be all that much smaller than the S27+. Considering the Pro should slot in right around the typical price point as the Plus model, it’s not hard to imagine plenty of would-be S27+ buyers moving to the Pro.

For what it’s worth, Samsung betting big on high-end devices usually pays off. We’re on year two of the Fold series seeing big successes — first with the redesigned Fold 7 in 2025, then with the reshaped Fold 8 this year — and the Galaxy S26 Ultra managed to become the best-selling Android phone in Q2 of this year in a list typically populated by iPhones and cheap Galaxy A-series models. Even with prices constantly on the rise, the people yearn for flagship smartphones. You just have to wonder where that ceiling actually is.

#10525. Microsoft’s DLSS competitor is out for the Xbox Ally X and works in handheld mode

With the official launch of Auto SR, your Xbox Ally X can use Microsoft’s upscaling technology undocked and with games running at 1080p.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft is now rolling out Automatic Super Resolution (Auto SR), an upscaling technology that competes with Nvidia’s DLSS, for the Xbox Ally X, Xbox Ally X20, and select Windows Copilot Plus PCs, the company announced today. Auto SR is also getting some new features alongside this official release.

In April, Microsoft started testing Auto SR on the Xbox Ally X, but you could only use it while docked. The full release of Auto SR announced today now works while you’re playing handheld and running on battery, and it also supports games running at 1080p. Microsoft does note that playing in handheld and at 1080p might lead to performance that “may not be as optimal as other configurations,” but says you’ll see visual improvements “especially in older games.”

Previously, Auto SR was also limited to framerates of “around” 60 to 75fps, but support for a new Auto SR Performance mode will allow you to hit framerates of up to 120fps. The Performance mode, and new compatibility with Auto HDR, will be available through Windows updates.

#10523. Top 6 AI YouTube Transcript Generators: Features, Accuracy, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A one-hour YouTube talk contains roughly nine thousand words, and finding the one point you need can take longer than watching the whole thing. A transcript solves that. With text in hand you can search for a phrase, quote an expert accurately, turn a webinar into a blog post, create subtitles or feed a summary tool.

Plenty of tools now promise instant YouTube transcripts, but they work in different ways. Some read the captions YouTube already has. Others listen to the audio themselves and produce a fresh transcript. That difference affects accuracy, speed and cost more than any feature list does. This guide compares six options, explains how each works and points out where each one falls short. Plans and limits change often, so confirm current details on each vendor's site.

Caption extraction. The tool pulls the captions attached to a video, either written by the creator or generated automatically by YouTube. It is fast and usually free, but it is only as good as those captions. YouTube notes that its automatic captions come from machine learning, so quality may vary, and accents, background noise and overlapping speakers can cause errors. Captions may also be missing entirely for some videos.

Fresh speech recognition. The tool takes the audio and runs its own speech-to-text model. This can be more accurate on difficult recordings and works when no captions exist, but it takes longer and often costs money.

Lynote is built for exactly this job. Paste a YouTube link, search by keyword or add a playlist, and it returns a timestamped transcript with an AI summary. Batch mode accepts up to 10 videos at once. The tool lists transcription in more than 100 languages, translation into 25, auto-chapters, one-click copy and TXT download, with SRT export also mentioned. The page says transcripts are free, with no sign-up required.

Best for: Students, researchers and marketers who want a quick, readable transcript or summary from a public video without installing anything.

Limitations: It extracts YouTube captions, so videos without captions cannot be processed. If the original captions are poor, the transcript will be too. For critical accuracy, review the text or use a service with human verification.

Before you install anything, check what YouTube already gives you. Many videos show a transcript option in the description area, and creators can review and edit automatic captions in YouTube Studio under Subtitles. YouTube offers automatic captions in dozens of languages for long-form videos and Shorts.

Best for: Quick lookups on videos that already have decent captions.

Limitations: Captions may not generate for videos with poor sound, long silences, overlapping speakers, mixed languages or unsupported languages. Export options are minimal, and there is no summary or cleanup.

Notta transcribes uploaded audio and video files and also accepts YouTube links. It lists support for 58 languages, real-time and bilingual transcription, and AI Notes that produce summaries and action items. A free plan includes 120 transcription minutes per month at the time of writing.

Best for: Professionals who handle a mix of recordings, meetings and YouTube videos in one place.

Limitations: The free minutes disappear quickly if you process long videos, and the strongest features sit behind paid plans.

Descript is an audio and video editor built around text. It transcribes recordings automatically, and deleting a word in the transcript removes the matching footage. You can import video, audio or a Zoom recording, and there is a free tier that does not require a credit card.

Best for: Creators who want to clean up their own videos and podcasts, not just read a transcript.

Limitations: It is an editor first, so it is more than you need for a quick YouTube lookup. It works with files you own, so you need the source media rather than a public link.

Rev offers both AI and human transcription. It advertises AI transcripts at 96 percent or better accuracy in minutes, and human transcripts at 99 percent or better, guaranteed within 12 hours. AI transcription covers 37 or more languages, while human transcription is English only. Pay-as-you-go rates start at $0.25 per minute for AI, and human transcription is listed at $1.99 per minute.

Best for: Legal, research and media work where mistakes are costly and a human check is worth paying for.

Limitations: The cost climbs for long videos, and the human option is slower than instant AI tools. Those accuracy figures are the vendor's own claims, so test your own audio.

Whisper is OpenAI's open-source speech recognition model, released under the MIT license. It handles multilingual transcription, translation into English and language identification. You install it with pip and need ffmpeg, and it comes in several model sizes from tiny to large, so you can trade speed against accuracy and hardware demands.

Best for: Developers building their own pipeline and teams with sensitive audio they prefer to keep on their own machines.

Limitations: You supply the audio, which means downloading it yourself and respecting the video owner's rights and YouTube's terms. Larger models need a capable GPU, and there is no built-in interface for non-technical users.

Start with the source. If the video already has good captions, a link-based tool is the fastest route. If the audio is difficult or the stakes are high, pick a tool that runs its own recognition or offers human review. If you are processing hundreds of videos or handling private recordings, look at Whisper or a paid platform with bulk features.

Whatever you choose, treat the transcript as a first draft. Check names, numbers and technical terms against the video before you publish or quote anything, and credit the original creator.

There is no single best transcript generator. Caption-based tools are quick and free, recognition-based tools are better when captions fall short, and human-verified services make sense when accuracy matters most. Try two or three on a video you know well, compare the results and keep a human in the loop for anything you publish.

Can I get a transcript if a video has no captions? Caption-extraction tools cannot. You need a tool that runs its own speech recognition on the audio, such as the options above that transcribe files.

Are AI transcripts accurate enough to publish? Often they are close, but not perfect. Accents, noise and technical terms cause mistakes, so review before publishing.

Is it legal to transcribe someone else's YouTube video? Transcribing for personal research or accessibility is common, but republishing the text may infringe copyright. Check the video's license and YouTube's terms, and get permission when in doubt.

What is the difference between a transcript and subtitles? A transcript is the full text, while subtitles are timed text shown on screen. Many tools can export both, often as SRT files.

#10522. Hunter is killing Google Home, Alexa controls for its smart ceiling fans

Hunter, a major brand for ceiling fans in the US, is pulling the plug on its Google Home and Amazon Alexa bridge for controlling “HunterSMART” models in just a few weeks.

Effective January 1, 2027, Hunter will pull support for the cloud-reliant “HunterSMART” connection used for Google Home and Amazon Alexa controls. Hunter’s app and the included remote will continue to work, but connections to smart home ecosystems will not.

Beginning January 1, 2027, the legacy cloud-to-cloud connections used for app linking between the HunterSMART™ mobile apps and Google Home and/or Amazon Alexa will no longer be supported.

After this date, Google home and Alexa functionality that relies on this legacy cloud connection will no longer work for HunterSMART fans, including voice commands, scenes, and automations.

This bridge is going away in favor of a Matter connection, with Hunter having been upgrading select models to be Matter-compatible over the past year. But this is only available to “eligible” models, not everyone who was already using Hunter’s existing bridge. A list of compatible models is available on Hunter’s website, with firmware updates rolling out via the HunterSMART app.

Some models, such as the one I reviewed a few years ago, require updated hardware. Besides that, the one I have can’t even be re-connected to Wi-Fi to check for an update as Hunter’s Android app consistently crashes during the setup process.

Once the fan has been updated, it won’t be available via the existing link and will instead require re-pairing via your smart home app of choice, whether that’s Google Home or another.

…Hunter is transitioning eligible HunterSMART™ products to Matter, an industry standard designed to support broader smart-home compatibility.

If your fan is eligible for Matter-compatibility, you will be able to continue using all smart functionality within the Google Home and/or Alexa ecosystems after completing the applicable Matter migration & setup.

Once your HunterSMART fan is Matter-enabled, connecting your fan to Google and Alexa will work differently than it does today. Instead of linking your HunterSMART account to Google Home or Alexa, you will need to setup your Matter-enabled fan directly into these compatible ecosystems using the Matter setup available via the Google Home and/or Alexa apps. Matter-enabled devices do not use the legacy cloud linking service for smart controls that is being discontinued.

How can I migrate my HunterSMART fan to Matter?

Depending on your fan model and hardware configuration, a firmware update or updated hardware components may be required for Matter compatibility. Available Matter upgrade options and instructions will vary by product. You can visit our HunterSMART™ Matter resource page for the latest information about Matter compatibility, available upgrade options, and next steps.

#10521. OpenAI and Georgia county leaders hit with Mafia-style RICO racketeering lawsuit over $30 billion AI data center

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Residents of Effingham County, Georgia, just filed a RICO lawsuit against their county commissioners, county leaders, and OpenAI and its affiliates, alleging that they committed various offenses in a conspiracy to get OpenAI’s “Project Camellia” approved. According to the Effingham Herald, the plaintiffs — Dr. Michelle Finch-Mincey, Lehman Mincey Jr., and Raymond Carver — are seeking civil damages, saying that the project has harmed their property values and even pointing out that purchase offers for nearby residential properties have dropped or been withdrawn since the data center was announced.

The lawsuit names all Effingham County Commissioners — Chairperson Damon Rahn, Forrest Floyd, Roger Burdette, Jamie DeLoach, Elizabeth Helmly, and Phil Kieffer — as defendants, alongside County Manager Tim Callanan, Effingham County Industrial Development Authority (IDA) CEO Brandt Herndon, the IDA itself, OpenAI, Octans GA LLC, the entity operating the data center on behalf of OpenAI, and Savannah Industrial Development LLC, the agency involved in the sale of IDA property to Octans and OpenAI.

It’s alleged that the defendants committed fraud and concealment through non-disclosure agreements and misleading public statements so that residents remain unaware of the nature of the projects for as long as possible, and that there was a civil conspiracy “to advance Project Camellia through confidential negotiations and closed-door decisions.” They also claimed that the parties engaged in improper land transactions, zoning violations, and civil racketeering.w

Project Camellia is one of OpenAI’s biggest data center projects planned for construction in Effingham County. The site is expected to cost somewhere between $20 billion and $30 billion, to be built across five phases, with a planned compute capacity of 3.2 gigawatts. The company also promised that it will not affect the community’s utility costs, as it will pay for the full cost of all its required infrastructure upgrades, and that it will have minimal water use due to its closed-loop water system. Despite those promises, it seems that some people are opposing the project because of how the county had allegedly tried to secure the land, tax incentives, and zoning approvals without informing the residents until key decisions were finalized and contracts had been signed.

The RICO Act — or Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act — was originally conceived as a way to counter organized crime, particularly Mafia leadership, who often used low-level members to commit individual crimes and do the dirty work for them. However, Georgia’s RICO Act is less stringent than the federal version, which is why it’s been used multiple times for cases that seemingly had nothing to do with the Mafia. For example, it was in the 2023 election racketeering case against Donald Trump and 18 others in the attempt to overturn the 2020 U.S. presidential election results in the state, and it was also used in Young Thug’s trial after the rapper was accused of running YSL Records as a criminal street gang.

Critics say that Georgia’s version is way too encompassing, though, and that it was being used to squeeze defendants involved in more mundane or common felonies. “When you have a hammer, everything looks like a nail,” Georgia State University professor of criminal justice and criminology Volkan Topalli said in Atlanta Magazine.

The actions of both state officials, OpenAI, and its affiliates are not new, though. There have already been multiple complaints of data center developers and local governments using non-disclosure agreements (NDAs) while they negotiate terms for possible data center projects. Developers say they use this tool to avoid “running afoul of insider trading rules,” but people have pointed out that West Feliciana Parish in Louisiana was able to close a data center deal without resorting to one. An AWS data center in Gilroy, California, is also facing scrutiny after residents only discovered the nature of the project when construction began after a five-year permitting saga that relied on zoning rules that were set 45 years ago.

#10519. Googlebook apps built by Googlers add macOS-like status bar shortcuts, keyboard commands

Googlebooks are a bit starved of desktop-class features at launch, but one of Google’s pitches around this platform is how apps can be built for it, and the company’s own employees are showing off what that looks like with some clever apps that just so happen to add features that feel a lot like they’re replicating macOS staples.

In a Reddit thread, three Googlebook apps built by Googlers through Antigravity are shown off, and they’re pretty cool, regardless of how you might feel about the platform itself.

“BentoBox” is a utility that gives you control over the status bar on Googlebook, enabling you to fill the status bar with shortcuts, mini-apps, and more. It’s full of glancable information such as weather conditions, battery charging speeds, and more, but also includes useful tools such as being able to access a clipboard history, set a timer, and more. The app requires permissions including accessibility overlays, but it works like a charm.

I had no idea the status bar on Googlebook could even be used like this, and it really makes that space a lot more useful. Of course, you have to draw a comparison here to macOS, which also uses this space for app shortcuts and tools, though BentoBox offers a different type of customization.

BentoBox is available for download via Google Play for free.

The other Mac-like app here is “Booklight,” a launcher that’s basically Apple’s Spotlight, just for Googlebook. It has a transparent design and lets you search “apps, settings, and the web.”

Technically, Googlebook already can do this in its built-in search bar (in the app drawer), but Booklight has more customization and functionality, including being able to write out shorthand commands all in one sentence with the app then completing the command in the appropriate apps, as well as having a clearer UI for experiences such as typing in quick math equations. It’s also good at finding symbols (so you don’t have to remember the U+ codes that carried over from ChromeOS). Not one I found myself wanting to use, but the potential is there.

Both BentoBox and Booklight were developed by Alexander Kuscher, VP over Laptops & Tablets at Google.

The other app highlighted in Google’s thread was Wayfinder, a file manager app described as a “desktop-grade file manager.” The big bonus here is network file access for NAS boxes and other uses, but the app also just has a more advanced UX compared to Googlebook’s built-in Files app.

Googlebook’s app gap is a real problem at launch, but these apps do show there’s a lot of potential in the platform, it’s just a matter of getting developers on board – which itself will require Google and its partners to actually sell some of these laptops… classic chicken-and-egg situation.

#10517. Sony’s latest Pulse wireless headset packs planar magnetic drivers at a good price

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A comfortable, minimalist option that supports concurrent low-latency audio and Bluetooth.

A comfortable, minimalist option that supports concurrent low-latency audio and Bluetooth.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It’s tempting to seek out the do-everything wireless gaming headset with noise cancellation, phenomenal sound quality, multiplatform connectivity, swappable batteries — you name it. But if you just want the basics done well, Sony’s new $99.99 PlayStation Pulse headset is worth considering. It has very good sound and just enough extras, even if you use more gadgets than just your PlayStation 5.

Inside the Pulse’s nondescript ear cup shells are custom planar magnetic drivers, which deliver clean, detailed sound whether you’re gaming, listening to music, or both. They aren’t bass-heavy or too shrill; their default equalizer is pleasing and punchy, though you can also tweak it in the PS5’s interface, like other Pulse headsets. They’re not Sony’s first audio gaming product to feature this type of high-end driver (the first was in 2023), but they are the most affordable at $99.

The Pulse comes with either a white or black headband, with a soft-touch underside and a decent amount of give near the top of your head where it rests its weight. Thankfully, the headset is lightweight and offers size adjustments that go beyond what my large head requires for comfort. These are some of the only headphones I’ve used with ear cups that can rotate 180 degrees in either direction.

Functionally, the Pulse stands out as a semi-affordable wireless headset that can stream audio from PlayStation Link (Sony’s low-latency, lossless wireless connection) and Bluetooth simultaneously. It includes a USB-C Link dongle that lets you pair it quickly with whatever you plug it into, whether it’s a PS5, PC, or Nintendo Switch 2.

The headset’s ear cup controls include a dedicated Bluetooth button that, beyond pairing with another gadget, lets you pause or resume Bluetooth content (it can also answer or hang up calls). You can also control Bluetooth audio volume using the integrated volume rocker, but only when a device isn’t connected via PlayStation Link. In simultaneous audio mode, the rocker adjusts audio solely for the device connected via the Link dongle.

If you have Sony’s PlayStation Portal, the Pulse can connect directly to the handheld without the Link dongle, just as Sony’s Pulse Elite and Explore can. It’s so convenient, and I hope Sony pushes for more devices that integrate the Link wireless technology in the future.

The Pulse isn’t the only headset that Sony has in the works. Its Pulse Edge wireless headset doesn’t have an exact launch date or price, but the company touts that it packs in twice as many magnets in its drivers (eight versus four), active noise cancellation, and a detachable boom mic that will likely provide better-sounding voice audio than the built-in mic in the standard Pulse. It will also include game / chat audio controls, a 3.5mm jack, and a carrying case.

While the $99 Pulse is comparatively light on features, Sony’s focus on providing standout audio quality and comfort makes it worth seeking out if you have a PS5 and are on a strict budget for your next wireless headset.
