#10515. Fitbit Sense & Versa losing 3rd-party watch faces this month, existing downloads remain

Google announced today that Fitbit Sense and Versa smartwatches will soon lose the ability to “install or update third-party apps and clocks.”

This change to the Fitbit App Gallery applies to the Versa, Versa 2, Versa 3, Versa 4, Sense, and Sense 2.

Existing “third-party apps or clock faces installed on your Fitbit device” will remain available, but you can no longer download new ones (or update them) starting November 28. As such, “if you choose to delete an app or clock face, or factory reset your device, you will no longer be able to re-install it.”

Instead, the Fitbit App Gallery will “exclusively feature apps and clock faces created by Fitbit and Google.”

The November 28 deadline also applies to third-party developers. Google will “no longer support uploading new apps and clock faces or updating existing ones.”

Google winding down Fitbit OS for smartwatches is not particularly surprising as the company has been all-in on Wear OS and the Pixel Watch. That said, the Sense 2 and Versa 4 should probably no longer be on sale.

#10514. Ocarina of Time is even better if you use a smartphone app

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Finding Ocarina of Time’s hidden collectibles is a lot easier with the help of Zelda Notes.

Finding Ocarina of Time’s hidden collectibles is a lot easier with the help of Zelda Notes.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I never liked hunting for Golden Skulltulas in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. The game has 100 of them tucked all over Hyrule, and even over my many hours with the N64 classic growing up, I can’t remember ever actually finding all of them, or even wanting to. Jumping into the Ocarina of Time remake, I expected to ignore the Golden Skulltulas once again. But while poking around Nintendo’s Zelda Notes companion app, I discovered that its maps are a huge help to tracking down all of the elusive golden spiders — and pretty much every other collectible in the game — and now I find myself constantly opening up the app to make sure I haven’t missed a secret.

Inside Zelda Notes, which is available in the free Nintendo Switch app (you don’t need a Switch Online subscription to use it), you can peruse a big map of Hyrule and tap on individual areas for a closer look. The app offers just enough direction to nudge me toward an elusive pickup; when zoomed in, the app drops icons on the map showing you the locations of different collectibles, and if you tap on an icon, the app will show a popup noting any items or special requirements you need to fulfill to be able to obtain it. There are also a bunch of filters you can apply, like swapping between the maps for child and adult Link and toggling categories of icons on and off.

For Skulltula hunting, the map has already saved my sanity many times. While exploring, I’ll often come across the telltale Golden Skulltula sound but have no idea how to get to the tricky spider, even with the help of additional chimes from the new Echo Gem Skulltula tracking item. But with a peek at the Zelda Notes app, I can pinpoint the exact location of where the Skulltula is supposed to be and if I need to use a certain item or change the time of day to be able to get to it.

I recently delved into the Shadow Temple, for example, and I couldn’t figure out how to get up to a Skulltula that I saw while coming down an elevator. But when I checked the icon on the Zelda Notes map, it said that I needed the Scarecrow Song to create a hookshot point, which I hadn’t gotten yet. As soon as I finished the dungeon, getting the Scarecrow Song was my first priority.

Zelda Notes has been useful for tracking down Pieces of Heart, too, which give Link more health once you collect enough. While running up and down Zora’s River, I kept seeing two Pieces of Heart on faraway ledges that I couldn’t figure out how to get to. (My memories of my extensive time with Ocarina of Time as a kid also failed me.) But tapping one of the icons on the Zelda Notes map revealed that I needed a Cucco to get to it — and suddenly, I understood why there was one of the chirping white birds near the entrance of the zone.

Yes, it can be annoying to have to open my phone and dig through an app to track down all of the Skulltulas and other objects of curiosity scattered around Hyrule. I play video games to try and get away from my phone, not look at it more. And sure, I could just look up an online guide, but that often means wading through walkthroughs of varying quality, trusting an AI summary, or scrubbing through a YouTube video. But with Zelda Notes, I’m usually in and out relatively quickly, putting down my phone after seeing a clue or an icon that pushes me in the direction of the next golden spider. I’ve already found more than 70 Golden Skulltulas, and to my surprise, I now want to hunt for the full 100.

#10511. Best Static Website Hosting Providers in 2027: Free and Paid Options Compared

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You've finished building your website. The HTML, CSS, and JavaScript files are ready, everything works locally, and now you need somewhere to publish it.

Static website hosting is one of the simplest ways to get a website online. Many providers offer free plans, and you don't need to maintain a traditional web server.

The harder part is choosing the right provider. Some let you upload a single HTML file and share it immediately. Others connect to GitHub, automatically deploy code changes, and support entire development teams.

We've compared eight static hosting providers, including TiinyHost, Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, Vercel, and GitHub Pages. Below, you'll find their free hosting limits, pricing, advantages, and drawbacks, along with recommendations for different types of projects.

Static website hosting is a service that stores and delivers pre-built website files, including HTML, CSS, JavaScript, images, and fonts.

Unlike traditional server-rendered websites, static sites don't need a server to generate each page when someone visits. The HTML is already prepared and can be delivered directly to the visitor's browser.

That doesn't mean static websites can't be interactive. JavaScript can still handle navigation, forms, filtering, and other features. Static sites can also communicate with external APIs or databases.

The main distinction is how pages are generated.

You can write a static website using plain HTML or generate one with frameworks and tools such as Astro, Hugo, Eleventy, or Next.js static export.

Static hosting is also commonly associated with Jamstack, an approach that separates the front end from back-end services.

Lower hosting costs: Many providers let you publish smaller websites for free.

Simpler maintenance: There's no application server or server-side runtime to maintain for the static pages themselves.

Efficient content delivery: Static files can be cached across a content delivery network (CDN), reducing the distance between visitors and website assets.

There are limitations, too. If your website requires user authentication, database operations, or server-side functionality, you'll need additional services or a hosting platform that supports them.

We compared eight providers based on their deployment methods, free plan allowances, pricing, and suitability for different projects.

Free hosting limits: Bandwidth, storage, build allowances, and restrictions.

Deployment process: Whether you can upload files directly or need a Git repository.

Custom domains and HTTPS: Support for publishing under your own domain.

Development features: Automatic deployments, preview environments, and integrations.

Pricing: What happens when a project exceeds its free allowance.

Usage restrictions: Whether free hosting is suitable for commercial projects.

The following table summarizes the main differences between all eight services.

Free plans aren't directly comparable. For example, Vercel restricts its Hobby plan to personal, non-commercial use, while GitHub Pages prohibits using the service as free hosting for an online business or commercial SaaS.

Bandwidth limits also aren't the same as visitor limits. A website serving large images or downloadable files can consume its monthly allowance with relatively few visitors.

Cloudflare Pages is a strong choice for developers who want to host static websites without worrying about a conventional monthly bandwidth cap.

You can connect a GitHub or GitLab repository, and Cloudflare automatically builds and deploys the website when you push changes. Direct uploads are also supported.

A maximum individual file size of 25 MiB.

The combination is useful for blogs, documentation websites, and other projects that primarily serve HTML, CSS, JavaScript, and images.

Cloudflare also provides preview deployments, custom domains, and HTTPS support.

No fixed bandwidth cap for static asset delivery.

Generous free plan for typical static websites.

Monthly build limits can matter for frequently updated projects.

Cloudflare's product range can make the initial setup less straightforward for beginners.

Cloudflare is increasingly emphasizing Workers Static Assets for new projects. Pages remains available, but developers starting a new application should compare both deployment approaches.

For a conventional static website, however, Pages is still a practical option, particularly when traffic could grow beyond the limits of other free plans.

See the official Cloudflare Pages limits for the current allowances.

Netlify combines static website hosting with tools designed to make development and deployment easier.

Connect a Git repository, configure your build settings, and Netlify can publish a new version whenever you push changes.

It also creates deploy previews, allowing you to inspect changes before merging them into your production branch.

This is useful when multiple people work on the same website or when you want to review design changes before publishing.

Netlify supports custom domains, HTTPS, serverless functions, and form handling.

Netlify's free plan uses a credit-based model with 300 credits per month.

Production deployments consume 15 credits each.

Web requests consume 2 credits per 10,000 requests.

Deploy previews and branch deployments consume no deployment credits.

The paid Personal plan starts at $9 per month and includes 1,000 monthly credits.

Convenient Git integration and deploy previews.

Built-in features such as forms and serverless functions.

Suitable for both simple websites and more advanced front-end projects.

Credit-based pricing requires monitoring several types of usage.

A busy site with frequent deployments can consume its free allowance faster than expected.

When comparing Netlify alternatives, Cloudflare Pages is particularly worth considering if your main concern is bandwidth.

For developers choosing between Vercel vs Netlify, the deciding factor is often the framework and workflow. Netlify offers a flexible approach to static site deployment, while Vercel has particularly close integration with Next.js.

Vercel is particularly well suited to Next.js applications, although it also supports React, Vue, Svelte, Astro, and other frameworks.

Like Netlify, Vercel connects to your Git repository and deploys updates automatically.

Every deployment receives its own URL, which helps developers review changes before publishing them to the main website.

Vercel also supports server-side rendering, functions, and other features beyond traditional static site hosting.

There is one important restriction: Vercel Hobby is intended for personal, non-commercial use only.

If you're deploying a business website or another commercial project, you'll need an appropriate paid plan.

Convenient deployment workflow for Next.js.

Supports both static and server-rendered applications.

Paid usage can involve multiple resource-based charges.

If you're building a personal Next.js application, Vercel is an obvious candidate. For a basic HTML website, however, its additional framework features may be unnecessary.

GitHub Pages is a straightforward way to publish a website directly from a GitHub repository.

It works particularly well for developer portfolios, project documentation, open-source websites, and small informational sites.

You can upload HTML, CSS, and JavaScript files to a repository, configure GitHub Pages, and publish them under a github.io subdomain.

GitHub Pages can serve existing static files or build websites using supported workflows, including Jekyll and GitHub Actions.

Published websites have a maximum size of 1 GB.

Bandwidth has a soft limit of 100 GB per month.

Free accounts can publish Pages sites from public repositories.

The bandwidth and build restrictions are soft limits, meaning they aren't necessarily enforced as strict cutoffs.

Simple integration with GitHub repositories.

Well suited to documentation and open-source projects.

Free accounts require a public repository for Pages.

GitHub Pages is not intended for running online businesses, e-commerce websites, or commercial SaaS products.

GitHub Pages is particularly convenient if your code already lives on GitHub and you only need to publish documentation or a portfolio.

For commercial websites, Cloudflare Pages or Netlify may be more appropriate.

The GitHub Pages documentation explains its hosting limits and usage restrictions.

Not every website needs a repository, deployment pipeline, or command-line interface.

If you've created a simple HTML page and want to share it, TiinyHost lets you upload an HTML file or ZIP archive and publish it under a shareable URL.

It can also host PDFs, making it useful for sharing documents and presentations alongside website projects.

The main advantage is the upload process. You don't need to configure a repository or install development tools before publishing.

Sharing an HTML file with someone who doesn't use Git.

The free plan includes one active site, a 3 MB project upload limit, and an allowance of 5,000 monthly visits.

The Tiny plan costs €5 per month when billed monthly. It supports uploads up to 25 MB and includes built-in analytics.

The Solo plan starts at €13 per month when billed monthly and adds features such as custom domains, password protection, and additional projects.

Annual billing is available at lower effective monthly prices.

No Git repository or command line required.

Suitable for previews and smaller projects.

The free plan's 3 MB limit is restrictive for websites with many images or other assets.

Custom domains require the Solo plan or higher.

Upload-based publishing is less convenient than Git automation for frequently updated websites.

For someone who simply needs free HTML hosting to share a small project, TiinyHost is worth considering.

For a larger production website that receives regular updates, Git-based hosting is likely to be more manageable.

Firebase Hosting is Google's hosting service for static websites and web application assets.

It integrates with other Firebase products, including Authentication, Cloud Firestore, and Cloud Functions.

That makes it useful when a website needs static hosting but also relies on Google's back-end services.

Deployment is handled through the Firebase command-line interface.

After configuring your project, you can deploy using:

Firebase distributes website assets through its CDN and supports custom domains with SSL certificates.

The Blaze plan uses pay-as-you-go billing while retaining applicable no-cost allowances.

One detail worth checking is the distinction between Firebase Hosting and Firebase App Hosting. They are separate products with different features and pricing models.

Integrates with Firebase Authentication and Firestore.

Useful for applications already using Google's developer services.

Initial setup is more involved than uploading an HTML file.

The daily transfer allowance may become restrictive for larger websites.

Firebase Hosting makes the most sense when Firebase is already part of your application.

For a standalone static blog or portfolio that doesn't use Firebase services, Cloudflare Pages or GitHub Pages may be simpler.

Amazon S3 and CloudFront offer a different approach to static hosting.

Instead of using a platform that handles the entire deployment process, you combine AWS services to store and deliver your website.

S3 stores the website files, while CloudFront distributes them through a CDN.

This setup provides control over caching, permissions, security, and integration with other AWS services.

It can be suitable for organizations already using AWS or developers who need specific infrastructure configurations.

AWS uses usage-based pricing for S3 storage, requests, and applicable CloudFront usage.

Free allowances may apply depending on your account and the current AWS pricing program, but you should calculate costs based on your expected traffic, storage, and requests.

Unlike simpler hosting services, there isn't one fixed monthly price for every website.

Several services can contribute to the final bill.

Incorrect permissions or origin settings can cause access errors.

One common issue when setting up S3 static website hosting is receiving a 403 Forbidden error because of bucket permissions or CloudFront origin configuration.

AWS recommends using CloudFront Origin Access Control with a regular S3 bucket origin so that the bucket can remain private. This differs from exposing the S3 website endpoint directly, which does not support HTTPS on its own.

AWS S3 and CloudFront are worth considering if you need that level of control. For a small static website, a managed hosting platform usually requires less setup.

Render is a cloud platform that supports static websites alongside web services, databases, background workers, and other application components.

Its static hosting works through a Git repository.

Connect your repository, select the build command and publish directory, and Render automatically deploys your website.

The service includes CDN delivery, custom domains, and managed TLS certificates.

Render is particularly useful when your project includes both a static front end and back-end services that you want to manage through the same platform.

Render's Hobby workspace is free and includes:

500 standard build pipeline minutes per month.

Additional bandwidth is billed at $0.15 per GB under the current pricing structure, so the Hobby workspace should not be treated as unlimited free hosting.

The Pro workspace costs $25 per month, excluding any applicable usage-based compute charges.

Static websites and back-end services on one platform.

The 5 GB included bandwidth allowance is relatively small.

Render makes sense when you're already using it for other parts of an application.

If you only need to host a static HTML website, Cloudflare Pages offers a more generous bandwidth allowance without the additional platform complexity.

Free static website hosting can be sufficient for a personal portfolio, documentation site, prototype, or small informational website.

However, the restrictions vary considerably between providers.

Some hosts measure data transfer, while others use visitor counts or a credit system. Check how each provider calculates usage rather than comparing headline numbers alone.

If your website rebuilds whenever you publish content, deployment limits matter. A blog updated a few times a month has different requirements from an application receiving dozens of deployments every day.

Free hosting doesn't always mean you can run a business website. Vercel's Hobby plan and GitHub Pages have restrictions that may rule out certain commercial projects.

Some services include custom domains on free plans. Others require an upgrade before you can connect one.

A free plan might stop serving additional usage, while another provider may bill for overages.

Before publishing a production website, check the provider's billing behavior and configure spending controls where available.

The right service depends primarily on how you build your website and what you intend to do with it.

For most developers publishing a conventional static website, Cloudflare Pages is a reasonable starting point.

If you're working with Git and want convenient previews and deployment tools, Netlify is another good choice.

For small files, temporary demos, and client previews, a simpler upload-based service may save time.

You should also consider how often the website will change.

Uploading files manually may be fine for a page that rarely changes. For a website with regular content updates, automated deployments from Git will generally be easier to maintain.

Publishing your website is only the first step. If you want search engines to discover and understand your pages, check the technical SEO basics before sharing the URL.

Every important page should have a descriptive HTML title and a relevant meta description.

You can use the Meta Tags Generator to create the necessary markup.

Open Graph tags help control how a page appears when shared on platforms that support them.

Set the title, description, canonical page URL where appropriate, and preview image.

A robots.txt file tells compliant crawlers which paths they may access. It doesn't guarantee indexing or prevent a public page from appearing in search results.

Use the Robots.txt Generator to create your crawler rules.

Include a sitemap with the canonical URLs you want search engines to discover.

Structured data helps search engines interpret eligible content.

Depending on the website, you may want Organization, Article, BreadcrumbList, or other relevant schema types.

The JSON-LD Schema Generator can help generate markup.

Confirm the correct domain loads over HTTPS.

Check canonical tags and redirect behavior.

Make sure important pages don't contain unintended noindex directives.

Test page performance and Core Web Vitals.

Verify the website in Google Search Console.

Static hosting makes serving website files relatively straightforward, but technical SEO still requires configuration and testing.

Cloudflare Pages is a good general-purpose choice because of its free static asset bandwidth and Git integration. Netlify is well suited to teams that need deploy previews, while GitHub Pages works well for portfolios and documentation. The best option depends on your deployment workflow, traffic requirements, and whether the website is commercial.

Yes. TiinyHost, Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, GitHub Pages, Firebase Hosting, Render, and several other services offer free hosting options. Most free plans have restrictions involving bandwidth, deployments, storage, or usage. Check the terms before using a free plan for a business website.

A small static website can cost nothing to host if it stays within a provider's free allowances. Paid plans vary from fixed monthly subscriptions to usage-based pricing. Domain registration, paid back-end services, and additional storage or bandwidth may increase the total cost.

For a standalone HTML file, upload-based hosting is generally the simplest approach. Services such as TiinyHost let you publish a file without configuring Git or a build process. If the project already lives in a GitHub repository, GitHub Pages or Cloudflare Pages may be more convenient.

GitHub Pages is a sensible option for documentation, open-source projects, and personal portfolios. Netlify offers more deployment features, including preview environments, forms, and functions. It also supports commercial projects, making it more appropriate for some business websites that would fall outside GitHub Pages' permitted uses.

Static website hosting doesn't need to be expensive or complicated. The important part is choosing a service that matches your website and deployment process.

Cloudflare Pages is a good starting point for static websites that need generous free bandwidth. Netlify is useful for Git-based development, while GitHub Pages suits personal projects and documentation.

If you're publishing a small HTML file or sharing a prototype, upload-based hosting may be all you need.

#10509. More game developers are starting to use generative AI

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey shows an increase in AI usage among game developers.﻿

The GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey shows an increase in AI usage among game developers.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

More video game developers are using generative AI tools in their jobs compared to last year, according to the GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey. Forty percent of developers surveyed say they use it “as part of their job,” an increase from 36 percent in 2025. Usage was higher with game developers that work on free-to-play games at 58 percent and mobile-only studio workers at 70 percent.

The survey is being published as the game industry continues to reckon with generative AI tools. While many game developers, particularly indie developers, have spoken out strongly against the technology, AI is at the center of indie awards conversations in part because Big Walk developer House House “sparingly” used LLMs to assist with technical challenges in the “final stretch” of development. AI is also increasingly being used to reverse-engineer retro games. And in a post on Thursday, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma seemed to indicate that AI will be central to Xbox’s future, saying that “A small team will be able to build worlds that today demand hundreds of developers and hundreds of millions of dollars.”

Seventy three percent of the survey’s respondents said they used AI for research and brainstorming, while 62 percent used it for data analysis and summarization, and 56 percent for code generation and debugging. Twenty two percent of respondents used generative AI for concept art or visual ideation, and 8 percent used it for production art or final game assets.

The survey also included grim numbers about layoffs, with 34 percent of respondents saying they have been laid off sometime in the past two years.

#10507. Prusa launches new carbon-fiber and carbon-nanotube-filled 3D printing filament for electronics manufacturing

A new PETG ESD filament just entered the market, and it's a better value than the competition.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Prusa released its new Prusament PETG ESD on Tuesday: a carbon-fiber and carbon-nanotube (CNT)- filled, electrostatic-dissipative (ESD) PETG filament meant to protect static-sensitive electronics. While it’s great for enclosures for a Raspberry Pi, ESP32, and the like, Prusa is targeting the filament more toward electronics production than hobbyist use; however, it’s cheaper than the non-ESD Prusament PETG Carbon Fiber at only $56.99.

Prusa isn’t the only one selling ESD filament; 3DXTech and Polymaker were first to the scene, but Prusa is the first to include carbon fiber alongside carbon nanotubes to provide the ESD properties, improving dimensional accuracy and minimizing warping. 3DXTech sells its ESD-PETG at $105; Polymaker’s Fiberon PETG-ESD is $65.98, but the Prusament PETG ESD is only $56.99 and is the only one with carbon fiber reinforcement.

Non-ESD filaments are generally safe for electronics enclosures; many hobbyists use regular PLA, PETG, and ABS without any problems. And while the extra protection can be appreciated, Prusa positions this not for hobbyists, but for electronics production — PCB fixtures and holders for soldering, IC trays for handling and transport, holders for testing multiple boards at once, etc., as Prusa shows on the PETG ESD product page. It saves on manufacturing costs, reducing the need for CNC-machined ESD trays and fixtures, and can be quickly printed on-site with very little lead time. It’s not necessarily for big businesses only either, as indie hardware developers and smaller-scale manufacturers could use this filament to improve their production line without buying expensive ESD filament.

Prusa has not yet published the datasheet containing details about the strength of the filament, but Prusa claims that the filament has increased stiffness and reduced toughness, similar to Prusament PETG Carbon Fiber’s properties. However, the amount of carbon fiber in PETG ESD may be different from PETG Carbon Fiber, as the latter’s purpose is more for strength than for electronics manufacturing.

Prusa is known to use its own 3D printers to print its own components and use its own products for the 3D printers it sells, and on the product page, Prusa says it uses PETG ESD in its own production line to prevent overcoating the edges when powder coating its print sheets. While it isn’t the first or only ESD filament on the market, it’s certainly the cheapest, and when its strength profile is released or tested independently, it may potentially be more worth it than Prusa’s own PETG Carbon Fiber in certain cases.

#10505. 25 years later, Xbox is trying to reinvent itself again

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Xbox has always been about trying something new, so what does Microsoft do next?﻿

Xbox has always been about trying something new, so what does Microsoft do next?﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft has spent the past 25 years trying to shake up the game console market. The original Xbox, which launched on November 15th in 2001, took on an industry dominated by Sony and Nintendo. Twenty-five years later, Xbox finds itself in a similar position. It’s not starting from scratch this time around, but to reach another 25-year milestone, it needs to do something radically different.

The original Xbox was radically different. Born out of a fear of Sony putting a PC in the living room, Microsoft built its first game console around familiar PC technology. A custom Intel Pentium III processor powered the original Xbox, alongside an Nvidia GPU and 64MB of unified RAM. It was different from the fully custom architectures that Sony and Nintendo used at the time, allowing Microsoft to entice developers to its platform with tools that were more familiar to PC game makers.

Microsoft also included two hardware features that were unusual for a console at the time: a built-in hard drive and Ethernet port. The hard drive let game developers cache content without having to rely fully on optical discs, and Xbox players could also use it for game saves without having to purchase a separate memory card. You could even rip CDs to an Xbox and listen to custom soundtracks while playing games.

Xbox wasn’t the first to bring online gaming to consoles, but Microsoft took a different approach. Instead of relying on a built-in modem like Sega’s Dreamcast, Microsoft included an Ethernet port, anticipating a shift toward broadband internet connections. Xbox Live, which launched in November 2002, really nailed the entire online experience. There were friends lists, unique gamertags, voice chat through a bundled headset, and integrated multiplayer services. Microsoft created an entire platform for online gaming, making it easier for developers to support and fundamentally changing how people played games together.

The Xbox 360 took these ideas a step further, with swappable hard drives, digital game downloads, wireless controllers, and backward compatibility. It also turned Microsoft’s game console into an entertainment hub for the living room, complete with streaming video apps. Kinect was perhaps its most radical addition. Launched in 2010, the motion-sensing device used cameras and microphones to let players control games with their bodies and voices. It quickly became the fastest-selling consumer electronics device in early 2011.

But the success of Kinect and Microsoft’s ambition to turn Xbox into a home entertainment device helped lead to its first major strategic misstep with the Xbox One. Microsoft positioned the successor to the Xbox 360 around TV, entertainment, and an always-online future, instead of focusing on the games that had made Xbox successful. Its plans for online DRM and restrictions on used games sparked a huge backlash, while bundling Kinect pushed the console’s price $100 above Sony’s PlayStation 4. Six months after launch, Microsoft was forced to drop Kinect from the Xbox One console bundle to close that price gap.

The Xbox One showed what happens when Microsoft loses sight of what gamers want. The company spent most of that generation playing catch-up to Sony’s impressive PlayStation 4 sales. But its weakened position also forced Microsoft to once again try something radically different with Xbox Game Pass.

Introduced in 2017, Game Pass shook up the very economics of how people play games. The subscription service lets you access hundreds of games instead of buying them individually. Microsoft went on an acquisition spree to power its Game Pass ambitions, buying up smaller studios, Bethesda, and eventually Activision Blizzard.

These days, Microsoft is spinning many Xbox plates. The Xbox Series X and S consoles that launched in 2020 are way behind Sony’s PS5 sales numbers, and Xbox Cloud Gaming hasn’t yet been able to reach the millions more players that Microsoft has always wanted for its ecosystem.

Xbox Cloud Gaming promised to make games accessible on phones and tablets without needing a dedicated console, and combined with Microsoft’s messy project to bring Xbox games to PS5 and Nintendo consoles, the efforts have raised questions about what the Xbox brand actually represents these days. Microsoft hasn’t quite decided whether it wants to be a full third-party publisher, an ecosystem owner, or an unusual combination of both.

Some of Microsoft’s recent radically different Xbox bets haven’t paid off, so it’s now turning its attention to a much smaller Xbox division, with a restructuring that will see overall Xbox headcount reduced by 20 percent by the end of Microsoft’s fiscal year in June 2027. “I believe the future of gaming will look more like its beginning, when a small team with a big idea could change how the world plays,” Xbox CEO Asha Sharma wrote in a message to Xbox employees today.

Microsoft is celebrating 25 years of Xbox today with an event in Seattle for fans, where it’s launching Xbox Playback, a personalized look back at players’ 25-year Xbox history. There will be more fan-focused announcements throughout the day, but I’m not expecting any news on the next Xbox console. The celebration is awkwardly timed, because rumors have suggested that Microsoft is also preparing for more Xbox layoffs in November, during the same month it’s celebrating a milestone.

The reset also comes as Microsoft is preparing for a next-generation Xbox console, codenamed Project Helix, which will play both Xbox games and PC titles. It sounds like an effort to combine Windows and Xbox like never before. The next Xbox could also play every Xbox game ever made, thanks to Microsoft’s impressive bets on backward compatibility over the years.

It’s an interesting full-circle moment. The original Xbox was essentially a PC built for the living room. Project Helix appears to be taking a similar approach, but this time around bringing the flexibility of PC gaming into a console experience. If Microsoft gets it right, it could open Xbox up to a much bigger library of games, and entice people trying to decide between a console or a gaming PC.

The problem for Xbox is getting consumers to care about this next console, especially when its games are available on PC and sometimes on Sony’s and Nintendo’s platforms. “Xbox has the best console ecosystem around, packed with consumer-friendly features like free cloud saves, Game Pass, and my preferred controller,” says Jon Clarke, cofounder and editor-in-chief of DayOne. “None of these things are particularly sexy or flashy. They’re simply features that work, and that can make them difficult to get customers excited about.”

Rebuilding the Xbox brand and launching a new console gives Microsoft an unusual opportunity to make another bold bet on the future, just like it did 25 years ago. But the risks this time around are greater, after more than a decade of trying and failing to catch up to the competition.

“Up against competitors that have firmly established their place in the market, Xbox finds itself in a difficult position,” says Clarke. “Rebuilding the brand will be an expensive endeavor, and the bold ambition of combining the best of console gaming with the openness and flexibility of PC is easier said than done.”

Xbox also needs good games, and after years of studio acquisitions it now has plenty of IP to play with. “Many of those franchises have lost their luster owing to years of mismanagement and potentially a generalized lack of vision,” says Jez Corden, executive editor at Windows Central. “Microsoft from the top will need to be willing to invest here to ensure Xbox’s franchises can not only find new audiences, but simply maintain what they already have.”

Xbox also faces the challenge of RAMageddon’s impact on the hardware industry right now, pushing the prices of components beyond levels that consumers can comfortably afford. “If Xbox is to reclaim any of its former glory, innovating on the costs of the traditional video game ‘console’ will be absolutely paramount, otherwise it’s hard to imagine that it will continue to exist in its current form,” says Corden.

A console projected to cost between $1,200 and $1,800 won’t grow the Xbox ecosystem. It could make more sense to let PC makers build their own Xbox hardware, opening up the platform to a wider range of prices and devices rather than relying on expensive flagship consoles.

Twenty-five years ago, Microsoft built a PC and called it an Xbox. Now it’s preparing to build something that looks increasingly like a PC again, only this time it needs to convince people that it should still be called an Xbox. Microsoft has another opportunity to shake up the console industry, but its biggest challenge might be convincing players that Xbox needs to exist as a console at all.

I’m always keen to hear from readers, so please drop a comment here, or you can reach me at notepad@theverge.com if you want to discuss anything else. If you’ve heard about any of Microsoft’s secret projects, you can reach me via email at notepad@theverge.com or speak to me confidentially on the Signal messaging app, where I’m tomwarren.01. I’m also tomwarren on Telegram, if you’d prefer to chat there.

#10503. Samsung Galaxy S27 series camera specs leak, and they’re disappointing

New leaks reveal the camera specs for Samsung’s upcoming Galaxy S27 series which includes three lenses across the lineup, but not the upgrades you want, nor what Ultra owners might expect.

SammyGuru reports that Samsung is “evaluating” camera setups for the Galaxy S27, S27+, S27 Pro, and S27 Ultra, and it looks like a mixed bag across the board.

The base Galaxy S27 and S27+ are looking very familiar, with identical main camera sensors to last year, and the same ultrawide sensor that’s been in use since the Galaxy S23 series. The telephoto and selfie sensors are looking at new hardware, but without any major spec upgrades.

Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, meanwhile, have a new 200MP main camera on deck, a 50MP ultrawide shooter, and a mere 12MP telephoto. The Ultra might get 5x compared to the Pro’s 3x optical zoom, but with a secondary telephoto lens going missing, existing users of the lineup are sure to be disappointed.

Notably, SammyGuru’s article mentions the Pro as using “the Galaxy S26 Ultra’s 3x sensor,” but shows the 12MP Samsung S5K3LD in that place instead. We’re assuming that’s a typo seeing as the post mentions the Pro as using the Samsung S5KJN3 or the Sony IMX855 earlier on, but regardless, the Pro only gets 3x optical zoom to the Ultra’s 5x.

Some of these specs have been mentioned by other sources such as GalaxyClub and leaker Ice Universe, but this is the most complete picture of the S27’s camera plans we’ve seen to date.

Personally, I’m very “whelmed” on this one. As RAMageddon rages on and Samsung hunts for new ways to balance specs vs price, cameras are a place that’s obviously going to be cut, but competitors are already running laps around Samsung on the hardware front. Cutting lenses and skimping out on upgrades (again) doesn’t help matters.

#10501. Google Antigravity adds ‘Knowledge Work’ interface for non-coding work

Following last week’s big update, Google Antigravity is rolling out a “Knowledge Work” user experience.

Antigravity launched with a coding focus, but Google has seen how people, including employees internally, are using it for “far more than code.”

Alongside software engineering, more and more daily sessions center on deep research, data analysis, drafting documents and presentations, prototyping designs, and automating everyday workflows.

The company shared that “tens of thousands of Googlers outside software engineering (across sales, finance, legal, product, design, research, marketing, and operations) now rely on Antigravity every week alongside engineers to get their work done.”

As such, Google Antigravity now has an optimized Knowledge Work experience that joins the existing Software Engineering interface. The underlying Antigravity agent is unchanged “with full access to frontier models, local files, plugins, skills, rules, custom agents, and MCP servers.”

When Knowledge Work is enabled, you’ll see the following UI changes:

#10499. Samsung’s paid Galaxy AI plan has a giant Gemini problem

Good morning. Today I’m trying desperately not to buy Sony’s new nostalgia-bait headphones, reeling from how emotionally invested I was in Spider-Man: Brand New Day (yes, I know I’m late), trying to figure out what a “private, agentic notebook” is, and slowly coming around to the idea of the New York Times owning Letterboxd.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

The relationship between Samsung and Google has, over the years, waxed and waned depending on their respective interdependence. Samsung is Android’s physical emissary, powering a plurality of all its handsets globally. But Google, and in particular its services, is the glue that holds Samsung’s smartphone experience together.

The back and forth is over a decade old; back in early 2014, Samsung shipped a refreshed magazine-style skin with its Galaxy tablets that eschewed many of the design tenets Google had been pushing its partners to adhere to. Not only was Samsung willfully defying its most important partner, but it made a point of barely mentioning Android during its press conferences.

Around the same time, it transitioned its smartwatches from Android Wear to Tizen and began shipping duplicative apps on its devices (many of which persist to this day). The goal was to distance itself from Google as much as possible.

While we don’t know exactly what happened behind closed doors, we saw the outcome quite clearly: Magazine UX lasted one product cycle, and the two companies signed a 10-year cross-license patent deal, which may have helped Samsung prevent any future lawsuits with Apple. Google then sold Motorola to Lenovo, clearing the way for Samsung to continue building its burgeoning hardware business on the back of Google’s mobile ecosystem.

Since then, the two companies have grown even closer. In 2021, they co-announced a new Wear OS along with an overarching wearables partnership that has since grown to include Android XR as well. And Google is still the default search engine for Samsung devices, despite reported inroads made by Microsoft in 2023 to convince the Korean giant to switch to a ChatGPT-powered version of Bing Search. (Samsung’s ongoing relationship with Microsoft, which bundles its Office suite on most Galaxy devices, could be seen as a point of leverage for negotiations with Google, too.)

This brings us to today, and to AI. Samsung was among the first companies to adopt Google’s full suite of Gemini Intelligence products, and its phones often get new AI features before anyone else. But like a decade ago, when Samsung shipped Bixby to kind-of-sort-of compete with Google Assistant, it still wants to offer its own services alongside Google’s.

So a rumor that Samsung may launch a paid Galaxy AI Premium subscription next year for $5/month piqued my interest for the same reason Magazine UX did 12 years ago. Galaxy AI features today are a blend of first-party and third-party capabilities, but none of them are especially useful, particularly when compared to Google’s own Android equivalents powered by Gemini Intelligence. Now Bar and Now Brief, two heavily promoted features in Samsung’s marketing, are wildly disappointing.

At I/O this past May, Google announced Spark, an agent that goes out and performs actions on your behalf. And while it’s since been somewhat eclipsed by more capable mobile options like Meta’s Muse, it’s obvious that Google is working on a follow-up that will perform functions both on the phone and inside apps.

That’s exactly what Samsung is rumored to be working on with Bixby Action, a spiritual successor to its namesake that, when it debuted, prioritized on-device tasks like turning on Wi-Fi over web-based actions Google Assistant did better.

According to leaks from SamMobile, Galaxy AI Premium will offer agentic upgrades for phone users to “create commands triggers and system routines for daily tasks” or “automate routine daily tasks using Galaxy AI.” Gen Video will reportedly “convert static photos into dynamic video clips,” while a new Daily Briefing likely does what it says on the tin.

Will Samsung users see enough value in this hodgepodge of features paywalled only because they use a surfeit of compute? And what models will power them on the backend?

Right now, Google is in the enviable position of being able to offer its AI at three distinct layers: the model itself, which is invisible to the user; the OS level, which can promulgate Gemini across every Android phone that runs Google services; and the hardware level, where Pixel lets it specialize and optimize.

Samsung, not particularly known for its software prowess, will likely need to step gingerly around Google’s AI shadow while also justifying the cost to shore up its much-needed services revenue. Based on this leaked roadmap, though, I’m not confident it will work out.

Instead, Samsung may want to acquire one of the many up-and-coming AI agent startups whose valuations are rising more quickly than their user bases. While Muse and OpenAI’s Dots have taken up most of the recent conversation, I’ve been using Hark, Town, and Instinct pretty regularly for a month or so and could see them being brought in-house by Samsung and offered either as standalone products or customized for Galaxy devices. As Google is doing with its own AI plans, Samsung could provide Galaxy users a year of free service, after which they would be expected to pay.

Samsung doesn’t have a great history of building software, but many of its acquisitions, from SmartThings to Viv Labs (which became “next-gen” Bixby) to LoopPay (which became Samsung Pay) to Whisk (which became Samsung Food), have proven successful. Given the current urgent state of mobile AI, I’d forgo the hodgepodge and bring in some outside expertise.

Jensen Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy is the only thing tech media can talk about right now, likely because it has implications far beyond just Nvidia’s stock price. The Nvidia CEO, who is one of the world’s richest and most powerful tech executives, is believed to have been targeted by Chinese intelligence to potentially influence American trade policy around AI.

Alex Kantrowitz’s Big Technology piece is a useful starting point for why this matters at a global scale:

Beyond the affair, the real issue is that Huang’s love interest, ex-Megaspeed International executive Alice Huang, is someone the United States government believes passed information to the Chinese government while building data centers with Nvidia’s help in Asia. There’s simply no getting away from this detail. This isn’t just an affair, it’s a corporate and national security issue.

Whether Huang can survive the impending investigation by Nvidia’s board remains to be seen, but the CEO has been a stalwart in the tech space for 33 years, and there is no clear successor to his position should he be forced to resign.

Damien reviewed DroidDeck, which I wrote about last week as part of a broader look at Android’s gaming renaissance.

Over the last few days, things have become even more preposterous: the Ocarina of Time remake, which was released on Thursday, has already been recompiled to run natively on Android (though it runs better on PC), largely with the help of frontier AI models.

All of these AI-powered shenanigans come after Anthropic said it would stop making it possible to recreate copies of games, claiming that “recreating someone else’s specific game using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy.” Good thing there are no other models capable of doing the same thing.

I think when you zoom out and look at production, AI really has the potential to be the biggest creative unlock in modern history. The last time we saw anything close to this was really the, you know, advent of CGI and digital composites in the early ’90s. That technology gave birth to amazing stories like Jurassic Park and Terminator 2, and it’s hard to imagine that AI is not going to have a more significant impact than that. And I personally believe that’s going to be an incredible unlock on creativity.

—David Ellison, Skydance CEO, when asked about how AI could impact the movie-making process

Thanks for reading, and have a great day. I’ll talk to you on Thursday.

#10496. A Practical Introduction to the Twitch Helix API

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Behind almost every Twitch tool you have ever seen, the stat trackers, the moderation bots, the stream overlays, the alert systems, sits one interface: the Twitch Helix API. Twitch is unusually generous with public data, and Helix is the door to it. If you have ever wondered how third-party sites know who is live, what a channel's viewership looks like, or details a Twitch profile page does not even display, this is the machinery.

This article is a practical overview of Helix for developers who have not used it yet: what it offers, how its authentication works, what people build with it, and where the sharp edges are.

Helix is Twitch's current REST API, served from api.twitch.tv/helix. It replaced the old "Kraken" API, which was fully retired in 2022, so any tutorial still showing Kraken endpoints is a sign to keep scrolling. Helix covers users, streams, channels, games and categories, clips, chat, moderation actions, subscriptions, and events, with JSON in and JSON out.

Two things distinguish it from many platform APIs. First, a large share of it works without any user's permission, because the data is public by Twitch's own definition. Second, it is paired with EventSub, a push system that notifies your application when things happen (a stream goes live, a user follows, a message arrives) instead of forcing you to poll. Most serious Twitch tools use both: Helix for lookups, EventSub for live events.

Authentication is where most newcomers stall, mainly because Twitch offers several OAuth flows and tutorials rarely say which one you actually need. The short version:

Every request needs a Client ID, which you get by registering an application in the Twitch developer console (two-factor authentication on your account is required first). Requests carry the Client ID and a bearer token together; forgetting the Client ID header is the classic mystery 401.

App access tokens come from the client credentials flow, a single server-to-server request with your ID and secret. They have no user attached and that is fine, because public reads, user profiles, live streams, top games, clips, need no user context. For most read-only tools, this is the only flow you ever touch.

Tokens expire, so real applications cache them with their reported lifetime and refresh on a 401 rather than assuming a token lives forever.

A tour of the endpoints that power most tools:

Users. Give the users endpoint a username and it returns the account's public record: display name, profile image, description, Affiliate or Partner status, and the account's exact creation timestamp. That last field is interesting because Twitch does not display it anywhere on a profile, yet it is one of the platform's most useful trust signals, moderators use account age to spot throwaway accounts, and streamers use it to filter giveaway entries. The creation date alone powers a small subcategory of tools: free account age checkers, such as Twitch account age checker, are essentially this one endpoint with a clean page around it, taking any public username and returning the creation date, age, avatar, and broadcaster status. It is a good illustration of how far a single well-chosen Helix field can go.

Streams. The stream's endpoint reports who is live right now, with titles, categories, thumbnail URLs, and viewer counts. This is the backbone of every "is my favorite streamer live" notifier and every directory-style site, and polling it over time is how stat trackers chart viewership history.

Games and categories. Top games, category search, and metadata. Combined with streams data, this is how analytics sites rank categories and how tools answer "how crowded is this category right now."

Clips and videos. Clip metadata, view counts, and creation tools. Highlight compilers and clip-sharing bots live here.

Helix enforces a points-based rate limit per Client ID, and every response tells you where you stand through Ratelimit-Limit, Ratelimit-Remaining, and Ratelimit-Reset headers. Well-behaved tools read those headers and back off before hitting zero; badly behaved ones retry 429s in a loop and wonder why things get worse. For lookup-style tools the practical fix is caching, data like account creation dates never changes, so there is no reason to ask twice.

A few more edges worth knowing before they bite:

Usernames change, IDs do not. Store user IDs, not logins, or your saved data will eventually point at the wrong person.

Missing users are not errors. Looking up a nonexistent username returns a successful response with an empty data array, so handle the empty case explicitly.

Batching exists. The users endpoint takes up to 100 logins in one call, which matters the moment your tool checks lists instead of individuals.

Secrets stay server-side. The client secret identifies your application; calling the token endpoint from browser code hands your app's identity to anyone who opens devtools. Public-facing tools put a small server between the browser and Twitch.

The reason Twitch's third-party ecosystem is so large is that the barrier is so low: one public API, generous free access, and a platform where small questions genuinely matter to people. That is why the space is full of narrow tools that each answer one thing well, when was this account created, is this channel live, how fast is this category growing, which clips popped this week. Most of them are a weekend of work on top of one or two endpoints, and some of them grow into real products.

If you want to start, the path is short: register an application at the developer console, request an app access token, and call the users or streams endpoint for something you actually care about. The official documentation at dev.twitch.tv is current and well maintained, which is not something every platform can claim. Pick one piece of data Twitch collects but does not display, put a clean interface on it, and you are following the same recipe as most of the Twitch tools you already use.

Get the freshest news and resources for developers, designers and digital creators in your inbox each week

#10495. Meta issues a high-priority update to prevent some glasses from getting ‘too warm’ when wet [U]

The latest Meta Glasses (not Ray-Ban or Oakley-branded) may temporarily overheat if they get too wet, but a software patch is here to help prevent that.

Folks using Meta Glasses in the Adventurer, Fury, Starfire, Nova, or Capri styles should open the Meta AI app on their smartphones and get their glasses updated as soon as possible. Meta sent out an email to all Glasses owners noting that the company has “an important software update” related to performance when “excessive moisture or sweat” enters the device. Meta specifically says this issue does not affect Ray-Ban or Oakley-branded Meta glasses.

Meta sent out an email to all Meta Glasses owners with the tagline “Action required,” noting that this could be a potentially serious issue:

“We’ve implemented a software update to fix a rare issue that caused a small number of Meta Glasses to get too warm when moisture entered the device. It works similarly to a phone shutting off automatically when it needs to cool down. If people experience this, they can contact Meta Customer Service for a replacement.”

The new software update brings your glasses to version 130, which includes a special software hook that shuts them down if they were to get too warm. Meta told me that the temperature for this threshold is quite low, and that the glasses shut off to prevent any potential issues. The company was very specific about the wording in the statement, carefully using the word “warm” instead of “hot,” so as to avoid the stigma that comes with that term on wearable technology.

While I haven’t seen any specific reports of Meta’s newest glasses models overheating, there are plenty of Reddit threads citing overheating issues on Ray-Ban Meta glasses over the years. Ironically, the one post I found related to sweat-related overheating was from a year ago on Ray-Ban Meta glasses, which this software update does not address.

To update your glasses, follow these steps:

Meta Glasses were launched this summer as a budget-minded alternative to the company’s Ray-Ban and Oakley-branded AI glasses. The glasses start at $299, which is substantially cheaper than the $379 Ray-Ban Meta Gen 2 or the $449 Ray-Ban Meta Gen 3 options. Meta Glasses come in several different styles, including special LISA and Kylie-branded ones, and offer identical functionality to the more expensive Ray-Ban or Oakley options.

#10494. AMD’s Venice-X maxes out family at 5.3 GHz, returning 3D V-Cache to data center after four years

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Venice-X maxes out the family's frequency for the first time.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AMD missed out on deploying Turin-X CPUs in the previous generation, giving its 3D V-Cache tech a four-year hiatus from the data center following the launch of Genoa-X in 2023. In the second half of next year, 3D V-Cache is coming back for HPC with Venice-X, matching the 96 cores and 1,152 MB of L3 cache available on the EPYC 9684X (Genoa-X), but with vastly higher clock speeds, PCIe 6.0, and nearly four times the per-socket memory bandwidth.

After first bringing hybrid bonding to EPYC processors with Milan-X in 2022, AMD followed up a little over a year later with Genoa-X. Turin came and went without a chip sporting stacked SRAM, but now, it’s back with Venice-X. The critical change this time around doesn’t come from big jumps in core count — and consequently cache — as is the case with the standard SP7 lineup. Rather, it’s the 5.3 GHz boost clock speed, another pillar of Venice broadly, that’s particularly beneficial in the HPC workloads Venice-X targets.

As you can see from the top EPYC -X chips from previous generations, boost clocks were significantly lower for the X chips, and higher frequencies were available within the standard chips from each family. Milan scaled up to 4.1 GHz, while Genoa could climb to 4.4 GHz, just not on the EPYC -X chips. Venice-X brings up to the maximum 5.3 GHz boost clock available across the range, unifying a high frequency with a large pool of bonded L3 for HPC workloads.

There are still details missing about Venice-X, including the base frequency, how many SKUs are in the lineup and what their specs are, and the TDP (though the 400 to 600 W range available to other Venice CPUs is likely). The chips appear to use the SP7 socket, however, keeping with previous EPYC -X chips.

The platform comes with 96 PCIe 6.0 lanes, while the smaller SP8 platform targeting enterprises trades memory channels for PCIe lanes. Venice-X comes with the full 16-channel memory setup available on SP7, including support for MRDIMMs at 12,800 MT/s that can drive the quoted 1.6 TB/s of per-socket memory bandwidth.

The critical marriage with Venice-X for HPC workloads, according to AMD, is high frequency with a large pool of stacked cache. AMD currently maintains both 3D V-Cache and high-frequency EPYC chips in its HPC fleet to run its internal EDA workloads. AMD Fellow Phil Steinke says the company runs performance analysis on these workloads to characterize performance, optimize EPYC designs, and push partners to optimize their software.

The biggest consumer on the HPC fleet by far are function verification workloads at over half of AMD's used compute, according to Steinke. These are logic simulations that show up in tools like Cadence Xcelium, and Steinke says the EPYC team is running these workloads “24 x 7 x 365.”

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

An example of one such workload is above, measuring IPC with AMD's uProf monitoring utility. With 3D V-Cache, AMD says this workload completes 40% faster at around double the instructions per clock. AMD didn't clarify which chips it was comparing or what application it was using. However, the comparison is between an EPYC fleet with and without 3D V-Cache.

Steinke says AMD is able to now move toward a single chip with Venice-X, maintaining the cache advantages in workloads like functional verification while driving the EDA workloads more sensitive to frequency. "I mentioned we have a mix of high frequency and 3D V-Cache CPUs in our data center, and that's because through Milan and Genoa, the high frequency parts were always a little higher on the frequency side," Steinke said. "You don't have that dichotomy [with Venice-X]. You can get the best of both worlds in a single CPU, and this is what we're really excited to be picking up for our own use."

AMD hasn't shared any benchmarks for Venice-X yet, but it provided some more specific HPC benchmarks comparing its high-frequency EPYC 9686F to Intel's Xeon 6980P in several HPC workloads, such as computational fluid dynamics. In AMD's testing, the 9686F was 1.6x faster in these benchmarks while running 32 fewer cores.

We had a chance to test the EPYC 9686F along with the dense EPYC 9996, which you can read more about in our Zen 6 EPYC Venice deep dive and benchmarking article. And indeed, the EPYC 9686F looks very impressive in some of the HPC workloads we ran.

We were only able to test a 2P configuration, and as evidenced by the sole Xeon 6980P, CloverLeaf is sensitive to latency. Even then, the 2P EPYC 9686F completed the complex hydrodynamics simulation in about a third of the time as a single Xeon 6980P.

AMD says it's currently using MEXT, its AI-driven flash-as-DRAM technology, to bolster the amount of memory available for its EDA workloads. When it deploys Venice-X, Steinke says the company will continue using MEXT, which you can read more about in our recent MEXT breakdown on Tom's Hardware Premium.

Venice-X CPUs are launching in the second half of 2027, while SP7 and SP8 Venice CPUs are rolling out now. Also coming in the second half of next year is AMD's Verano, which it's pitching as an AI host node that leverages SOCAMM2 LPDDR5X memory.

Indoor cameras can be invaluable for securing your home, but also just in keeping an eye on what’s going on when you’re not home. To that end, Ring has been working on cameras that can be sent around the home remotely, and they’re finally launching early next year.

#10484. Waze rolling out cyclist alerts based on real-time locations from Garmin trackers

Waze is officially rolling out alerts for cyclists along your route that leverages location data from Garmin trackers.

In an effort to “boost driver awareness” of nearby bicycle riders, Waze will start showing real-time alerts for nearby cyclists to drivers in the area. The feature shows both a location on the map ahead as well as a visible alert on screen.

Waze knows the location of a nearby cyclist through a partnership with Garmin, with “LiveTrack” location sharing from the Garmin Edge bike computer or Garmin smartwatches users to share location data with the Google-owned navigation app.

Cyclists need to opt-in to the feature to have their location shown, and the alerts drivers see are temporary and anonymous. When cyclists end their ride, Waze alerts also go away.

This is rolling out starting today to the Waze apps on both Android and iOS.
