#10483. Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

LangSmith is closed-source and restricts self-hosting to enterprise licenses, teams that run their own LLMOps stack and want to keep traces on their own servers need an alternative. To find the best fit, we set a baseline by tracing a small TypeScript agent (equipped with two tools) in LangSmith Cloud. Then, we ran the exact same agent through Langfuse, Arize Phoenix, Opik, and AcruxCore using each platform's recommended TypeScript setup. All tests were run on November 5, 2026, and we pulled every trace back through each tool's API to verify the data.

All four alternatives successfully recreated LangSmith’s trace tree for the test prompt: a single agent span containing three LLM calls and two tool calls. The real differences between them come down to how much boilerplate code you have to write, how spans are typed, whether costs are tracked, and the overall server footprint.

Why Teams Look for a LangSmith Alternative

LangSmith remains the best choice if you already use LangChain and allow cloud hosting—it required just 25 lines of code in our test. The alternatives below are for teams that don't fit that profile.

To test these platforms, we built a simple customer support agent equipped with two tools:

Both tools use static fixture data to ensure every test run is identical. The agent runs a standard loop: it prompts the model, executes any requested tools, and stops once the model provides a final answer.

We tested the agent with three specific prompts to see how the tracing handled different levels of complexity:

For our comparison, we focused entirely on the third question because it generates a robust, multi-step trace.

Under the hood, the agent uses the standard openai package to call the qwen3.8-flash model. We originally picked this model based on its reliable tool-calling behavior during early probing thought it once skipped a tool call later.

We used LangSmith as our baseline. By tracing the agent there first, we created a checklist of every field it captured, and we judged all the alternatives against that exact list.

For setup, we followed the recommended TypeScript approach from each tool's documentation. Phoenix didn't have specific instructions for using its register() helper with OpenAI in TypeScript, so we adapted the pattern from their Anthropic guide.

Here is the agent with no tracing. Each tool section adds one tracing file that imports these files and changes nothing in them.

LangSmith traces the plain openai package with two helpers. wrapOpenAI records each model call, and traceable records your own functions. Without traceable on the run and the tools, wrapOpenAI alone gave three separate root runs and no tool runs.

This file adds 25 lines and removes 3, and installs one package.

LangSmith showed one root run with five children in call order: LLM, get_order_status, LLM, get_refund_policy, LLM. Every field it recorded became a row of the checklist table under "Results". Cost appeared only after a custom price entry. In our error run the failing tool was marked as an error, but the root stayed success: traceable turned the throw into a rejected promise that our agent did not await, so the agent carried on and answered.

Langfuse self-hosts as six containers, including ClickHouse, a column store built for large scans. In our measurement the stack held 2.60–2.64 GiB of memory at idle. Its Docker Compose guide recommends at least 4 CPU cores and 16 GiB RAM, with about 100 GiB of disk.

The TypeScript path is built on OpenTelemetry. You start the OTel Node SDK with LangfuseSpanProcessor, wrap the client with observeOpenAI, and wrap your own functions with observe:

This file adds 31 lines and removes 5, and installs four packages. We pinned the OTel Node SDK to 0.223.0, because @langfuse/otel 6.1.1 does not accept 0.300.0.

Langfuse successfully recreated the same trace tree, neatly tagging the spans as AGENT, GENERATION, and TOOL. It captured all the essentials: tool calls, tool definitions, and the complete message history. However, for the LLM output, it only saved the assistant's message—dropping the finish_reason, response ID, and the provider name.

We ran our deployment in Langfuse v4's events_only mode, which turns off the v1 trace endpoint. Because of this, the API didn't return a centralized trace object or root-level sums for tokens and costs, even though the UI calculated and displayed them perfectly (we didn't test other modes).

Costs showed up exactly as expected once we added a custom price entry. Since Langfuse calculates prices based on the specific usage types you define, and we only set rates for input and output, the separate reasoning tokens went unpriced. Finally, during our error test, the observe wrapper let the error bubble up to the agent, correctly flagging both the tool and the root span as an ERROR.

Phoenix self-hosts as two containers, one Python server and Postgres. In our measurement it held 0.47 GiB at idle. Phoenix is source-available under the Elastic License 2.0, so it is not open source. Authentication is off until you turn it on.

The TypeScript path uses Phoenix's register() helper and the OpenInference instrumentation for the OpenAI package. OpenInference is a set of OpenTelemetry attribute names for LLM spans. traceAgent and traceTool wrap the run and the tools:

This file adds 32 lines and removes 3, and installs three packages.

Phoenix rebuilt the same tree, typed as AGENT, LLM and TOOL. Each LLM span keeps the full OpenAI response object, plus the messages as flattened attributes. The root span stores no token count, but Phoenix computes a sum for the root and the trace when you read it. Cost and those sums are only in the GraphQL API and the UI, and the REST API has no per-trace endpoint.

Cost appeared after a custom price entry under Settings > Models, and Phoenix priced the reasoning tokens by falling back to the output price. In the error run, the tool span and the root were marked ERROR, and the tool span carried an exception event with the stack trace.

Opik self-hosts as eight containers, including ClickHouse, MySQL and ZooKeeper. In our measurement it held 3.69 GiB at idle, the most of the four. The self-hosted UI opened without a login, so every trace was readable from any machine that could reach the port.

The TypeScript path wraps the client with trackOpenAI and your own functions with track. By default trackOpenAI starts a new trace for every model call, so the file passes the run's span through the documented parent option:

This file adds 33 lines and removes 5. It installs opik-openai 2.2.142, and npm adds opik 1.11.14 as its peer, because opik-openai asks for opik ^1.8.61.

Opik successfully recreated the trace tree, and its trace object neatly tallies token counts across all LLM spans. In the logs, tool arguments are bundled into an array, and the LLM output only retains the assistant's message.

We did notice a quirk with the documented OpenAI wrapper (tested on the npm package opik 1.11.14): it tags model calls as general rather than llm, which incorrectly leaves the llm_span_count at zero. (We didn't test newer Opik core versions to see if this has changed). Additionally, because our synchronous tools execute in under a millisecond, they log the exact same start time as the subsequent LLM call, causing the UI to list the LLM call first out of order.

Cost tracking is entirely manual. Opik showed no costs by default, and the documentation doesn't mention a custom price table. We checked the configuration tabs on our 2.2.52 instance (Feedback definitions, AI Providers, Workspace preferences) and found no model pricing settings. To see costs, you have to manually assign a price to each individual span yourself—only the spans we hardcoded this way displayed a cost.

Finally, error reporting is highly detailed. If something fails, Opik attaches an error_info block—complete with the error type, message, and full traceback—directly to the tool span, the root span, and the overall trace.

AcruxCore self-hosts as five containers: Postgres, Redis, a Node API and worker, and nginx. In our measurement it held 0.39 GiB at idle, the least of the four. Its quickstart leaves sign-up open and ships a committed default GATEWAY_ENCRYPTION_KEY, which encrypts stored provider keys; replace it. We tested a local build from 2026-09-17.

The TypeScript path AcruxCore's docs put first is its gateway. The official openai client keeps working: you point its baseURL at ACRUXCORE_BASE_URL + "/gateway" and call a model registered in AcruxCore, which forwards each call to an OpenAI-compatible provider. The gateway records every model call as an llm span. The SDK's step helpers add the rest: traces.step opens the agent span, traces.wrap wraps each tool, and two headers file the gateway's spans under the agent span:

This file adds 30 lines and removes 5, and installs one package. It builds its own client, so it does not use provider.ts. The gateway refused our team API key, so the client uses a gateway virtual key created in the dashboard.

On this path every model call passes through the AcruxCore server, which none of the other four tools need. If you do not want a proxy in the request path, the SDK's register() helper sends OpenTelemetry spans from your process instead. On that path the step helpers do not join the openai package's spans, so you write the agent and tool spans by hand.

AcruxCore rebuilt the same tree, typed as agent, llm and tool, in call order. Each LLM span keeps the full response, the provider and the cached-token count, and the trace carries a token total. The LLM spans do not keep the tool definitions sent to the model, and no reasoning-token count was recorded. The trace took its name from the question, not from the agent span, and each LLM span is named by its start time.

Cost appeared on every LLM span and as a sum on the trace, from the custom price we set on the model in AcruxCore's model list. In the error run, the step helpers marked the tool span and the agent root as error with the error message, with no status code of ours. The API returned no stack trace.

All five tools accept traces over OTLP, the OpenTelemetry wire format. trace-otel.ts sends the same spans with no vendor package: the OTel Node SDK, the OTLP/HTTP protobuf exporter and the OpenInference OpenAI instrumentation. The exporter reads its endpoint and headers from environment variables:

We ran the two-tool question once per backend and changed only environment variables: the endpoint, the headers, and for Phoenix a resource attribute that sets the project name (without it, spans go to a default project). "Accepted" means the exporter reported success, which it does only for an HTTP 2xx, and we read the spans back through each API.

Opik stored the root and tool spans as general although AGENT and TOOL kinds were sent; the cause is Not tested. LangSmith mapped AGENT to its chain type. If you instrument with plain OTel and OpenInference attributes, your raw spans are not tied to one vendor, but span types, cost and trace views still differ by backend.

The first table covers setup: how much server each tool needs, how much code we changed in the agent, and how cost and the plain OTel exporter worked. Phoenix and AcruxCore had the smallest servers, and AcruxCore needed the fewest lines among the self-hosted tools. Opik was the only one without a price table.

The second table checks each trace against what LangSmith recorded for the same run, one field at a time. All five rebuilt the same tree with tokens and durations. They differ in span types, how much of the LLM output they keep, where token sums live, and how much detail they store for the failing tool.

Token totals for the two-tool question ranged from 1,910 to 2,111. Each tool traced a separate run and reasoning length varies, so these numbers do not compare the tools.

All five tools successfully traced our test agent, so the right choice really comes down to your team's specific constraints, infrastructure preferences, and how tracing fits into the rest of your MLOps tooling.

All four alternatives successfully recreated LangSmith’s trace tree. The real deciding factors are boilerplate code, span typing, cost tracking, and server bloat.

LangSmith remains the simplest option for LangChain users who can utilize the cloud. Otherwise, your choice depends on your priorities: Langfuse for community size, Phoenix for a lightweight Postgres footprint, Opik for strict Apache-2.0 compliance with deep error logging, or AcruxCore for combined tracing and tool governance on minimal hardware. Best of all, if you stick to standard OpenTelemetry spans, you aren't locked in—you can try them all and easily pivot later.

Google Drive will soon prompt users to switch to Adobe Acrobat while viewing PDFs, and Gemini will get a few new tricks powered by the company’s creative suite.

The pipeline from PDFs opened in Google Drive and Gmail to Adobe Acrobat is notoriously strained. That goes for any PDF viewer, but as soon as certain tools are necessary, a large number of users rely on Adobe’s products to edit and store documents.

Google announced plans to ease the process. According to a press release today, Google will be adding a “direct pathway” from Google Drive and Gmail in Adobe Acrobat. Acrobat will be suggested to users within the services, which will likely act as a quick transfer sort of feature. It doesn’t seem to indicate users will see any Adobe PDF editing tools within Google Drive. Google Drive has notoriously fallen short in this regard.

The partnership announcement also means Gemini will get access to some of Adobe’s creative tools. Further detail isn’t clear, but that includes Gemini, Gemini Enterprise, and Google AI Mode in Search. It sounds like the integration will allow users to edit or manage PDFs and other files straight from Google’s AI interfaces.

It’s not just PDFs and Google Drive; Adobe’s Photoshop gets a shout, too. Expected sometimes in the future, we may see more powerful image editing powered by Gemini within Adobe Photoshop, as opposed to the current models and limitations within. Gemini Nano Banana models are currently in use, but AI generation and editing could go further with a more focused effort. Google says the push “could enable near-real-time edits,” but there’s no concrete timeline set for when we could see that.

That goes for each of these byproducts of the new partnership. Google Drive and Gmail integration with Adobe Acrobat will likely be the first to roll out, with Gemini features to follow shortly after.

#10479. Gemini 4 Carbon rolling out to Gemini app for AI Ultra, Argon remains

Following the preview at the end of last month, Google is now bringing its latest Gemini 4 Carbon model to the Gemini app.

Officially, Google says “Argon remains the ideal choice for the majority of software engineering and knowledge work, while Carbon is built for tasks that test the limits of a model’s intelligence.” Examples include “complex, multi-step coding and debugging and open-ended scientific research and discovery.”

In the model picker, 4 Argon is for “Code & analysis” and 4 Carbon is for “Most complex work.” Notably, the Gemini app has removed 3.1 Pro with this launch.

To showcase performance on larger, more complex projects, Google Antigravity is previewing /swarm. This turns a “single prompt into a coordinated team of agents you can track in real time.”

Developers can assign Carbon to roles that demand the deepest reasoning, like architectural design, coordination, and auditing, while pairing it with faster, lighter models across parallel isolated workspaces and adversarial verifiers that test every change.

For legal, finance, and enterprise work, Carbon is said to bring “higher-precision knowledge work, artifact generation, and data analysis.” This includes how the model will follow “strict schemas and formatting to generate polished and precise spreadsheets, presentations, and reports.”

Existing benchmarks don’t adequately capture how agents reason natively over dense files with charts, images, blueprints, schematics, and tables, so we partnered with Artificial Analysis to launch the new Multimodal Knowledge Agents (MKA) benchmark. Carbon ranks #1 while using 3X fewer tokens than the next best model across 200 tasks that test a model’s multimodal performance in agentic workflows.

The introductory pricing for Carbon is $2 per million input tokens and $10 per million output tokens until November 30. Afterward, it will cost $4 per 1M input tokens and $20 per 1M output tokens.

#10477. Integrating TorchTPU into TorchTitan and Optimizing Models for TPU

Over the past years, Meta’s TorchTitan has emerged as the PyTorch ecosystem’s premier clean-room reference platform for large-scale generative AI model training. Built entirely on PyTorch-native scaling primitives, TorchTitan provides an unencumbered, highly modular showcase for pre-training flagship dense and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures like Llama 3, Qwen 3, and FLUX.

Historically, developers looking to train PyTorch models on Google Cloud TPUs faced some challenges: either undertake a complete codebase rewrite into JAX, or grapple with legacy torch_xla. That paradigm has officially shifted. With the launch of TorchTPU, Cloud TPUs now feature a native backend (torch.device("tpu")) integrated directly into PyTorch's core interface. Enabling a training script to use TPUs now only requires minimal changes to your device initialization code. Providing device compatibility, however, is only half the battle, developers also need high performance production-grade, distributed training recipes. This is where TorchTitan on TPU comes in. TorchTitan TPU is a fork of Meta’s TorchTitan implementation that is adapted to enable model training on Google Cloud TPUs. The fork is available at https://github.com/google-pytorch/torchtitan/

Rather than requiring engineering teams to invent custom TPU sharding strategies or hand-craft low-level XLA communication passes from scratch, Google and Meta co-developed TPU-native execution passes directly inside TorchTitan. TorchTitan serves as the definitive vehicle for scaling PyTorch on Google Cloud TPUs.

TorchTitan provides official, battle-tested recipes for pre-training large dense models (such as Llama 3 70B/405B and Qwen 3 8B/32B), Mixture-of-Experts (such as Qwen 3 235B MoE), and diffusion transformers (such as FLUX.1 12B).

Beyond initial pre-training, TorchTitan serves as the scalable trainer inside post-training pipelines like VeRL for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) alignment. In these workflows, TorchTitan handles the parameter update step while synchronizing weights with high-throughput serving engines like vLLM-TorchTPU.

Parallelism is also kept separate from the model code: techniques such as fully sharded data parallelism and tensor parallelism are layered onto a parallelism-agnostic model implementation. The exception is expert parallelism: the MoE layer performs explicit communication through a pluggable token dispatcher that each backend can implement in its own way.

TorchTitan builds a model from a configuration tree, and that tree can be rewritten before the model is constructed. This offers a clean way to replace individual blocks of a model with versions that run better on TPU, without touching the model definition or the shared trainer loop.

We use this actively. The TPU recipes replace standard attention with Splash Attention, a Pallas kernel that handles causal, sliding-window and packed-document masks without materializing the attention matrix; replace the final projection and cross-entropy loss with a fused Pallas kernel that never writes the full logits tensor to HBM; and replace MoE expert matmuls with grouped Pallas kernels. Each kernel is a JAX function turned into a PyTorch custom op by TorchTPU's torch.tpu.pallas.jax_op decorator, which is all the glue a new kernel needs. Each replacement is a config flag, so a recipe can turn it on, and a test can compare it against the plain PyTorch path it replaces. Kernels can come from tokamax, the OpenXLA kernel library for TPU and GPU.

FSDP2 is built around the GPU execution model. Its all-gathers and reduce-scatters are issued from module hooks as explicit collective calls, and overlap with compute comes from running them on dedicated CUDA streams synchronized with events. None of that is visible to a compiler: the collectives sit outside the traced region, so on TPU a torch.compile region breaks at every FSDP hook and a whole-step graph cannot be captured at all. TorchTPU provides no-op stream and event objects only so that FSDP2 runs; XLA has no use for them, since it schedules work from data dependencies.

SimpleFSDP takes the compiler's view instead. Parameter sharding is a DTensor parametrization: reading a module's weight performs a redistribute from sharded to replicated, which is the all-gather, and its autograd backward is the reduce-scatter. There are no hooks and no explicit collective calls, so both collectives are ordinary nodes in the traced graph, whether that graph is a per-block torch.compile region or the Graph Trainer's single make_fx step. The XLA compiler schedules the all-gathers ahead of the matmuls that need them and overlaps the reduce-scatters with the backward compute, inside one program. Mixed precision is part of the same mechanism, a policy that sets the gather dtype (bf16 parameters) and the reduce dtype (fp32 or bf16 gradients) on that redistribute. Hybrid sharding for multislice is the same code on a 2-D mesh: shard within a slice over ICI, replicate across slices over DCN.

On FLUX this is what brought TorchTitan to 99% performance parity with JAX MaxDiffusion: the per-layer HLO for a SimpleFSDP block is a single module with the weight all-gathers, matmuls, attention and norms fused together, which is the shape of program XLA was built to optimize.

Many PyTorch trainers either run in eager mode with CUDA graphs to cut launch overhead, or apply torch.compile to isolated regions, typically one transformer block at a time. TorchTPU does not yet support the PyTorch torch.Stream abstraction, which limits the performance of mixing eager-based FSDP2 with layer-wise torch.compile. SimpleFSDP helps by expressing parameter sharding in a way that is compatible with compilation, whether using layer-wise torch.compile or full-graph capture.

TorchTitan's Graph Trainer provides full training-step capture (forward, loss and backward), producing one FX graph ahead of time with PyTorch's make_fx tracer, skipping AOTAutograd partitioning, so the whole computation is visible to XLA). GraphTrainer also runs optimization passes: to implement graph-based activation memory policy that decides what to save, recompute or offload. The train step then executes as a single XLA program.

This is the model XLA was built for, and it makes PyTorch training on TPU conceptually the same as JAX, where the whole train step is one jitted function. Owning the whole step as one graph is also what makes the large-model compilation techniques possible: scan over layers, which runs the decoder layers as a single loop body so that compile time and memory stop growing with depth, and the graph splitter, which cuts the step into separately compiled forward and backward programs with activations offloaded to host memory in between. On Qwen3 235B this path reaches 29%+ MFU.

The optimizer can join the graph as well: While the fused_adam optimizer is very performant, a functional AdamW update can be traced into the train-step (functional_adamw_step) and ensures an iteration has no host-side work between the first forward op and the weight write-back. On Qwen3-8B this path reaches 51-52% MFU.

Every lever below is described in the TorchTitan TPU optimization guide, and nearly all of them are already wired into the recipes. For most models, getting a good MFU on TPU is a sequence of configuration changes on an existing recipe rather than new code. The order matters, because each step changes what the next one measures. A lot of optimizations in TorchTitan are available as ready-to-use building blocks that can be enabled by a flag flip (see illustration below).

4. Tune Compiler, Communication and Profile

To check that TorchTitan runs efficiently across different classes of models, we tuned and scaled several of them on TPU v7x (Ironwood). At scale, Llama 3 405B (large dense model) holds about 57% MFU from 128 to 1,024 chips. Qwen3 235B (large MoE model) reaches 32.42% MFU on a single 1,024-chip slice and 30.51% on 8,192 chips across eight slices. Muse Glimmer 30B (modern dense model) reaches 43.4% MFU on 128 chips (4x4x8). FLUX.1 (diffusion model) reaches 19.4% MFU on 1,024 chips (8x8x16).

The following case studies show how we got there for three of these models, each representing a different class:

FLUX.1 is a 12-billion-parameter diffusion transformer, which makes it a useful check that the playbook above is not specific to LLMs. We trained it at 1024x1024 resolution on a TPU v6e-8 with FSDP in bf16 and a batch of three samples. Out of the box, in eager mode with FSDP2 and full activation checkpointing, a step took 11.49 seconds. Applying the playbook in order brought that down one stage at a time. Deferred-and-fused eager execution cut the number of HLO module invocations by 28% and the step to 9.95 s (15.5% improvement). Replacing FSDP2 with SimpleFSDP reduced the step to 6.52 s (52.6% improvement): the trace was dominated by FSDP2 copying parameter shards into a flat staging buffer before each all-gather and back out into per-parameter tensors afterwards; SimpleFSDP all-gathers each parameter directly, so the staging buffer and both copies are gone (see the SimpleFSDP section above). The splash attention kernel, with sequence packing and segment ids so that no MXU cycles go to padding, took the step to 5.55 s (17.5% improvement), and per-block torch.compile, which hands XLA large sub-graphs instead of fine-grained modules and moves control flow out of the Python interpreter, took it to 3.02 s (83.8% improvement).

The final step came from three small, local rewrites in the model code that the profile pointed at: splitting a fused projection into two separate linear layers so its output no longer had to be sliced apart, simplifying the rotary embedding to plain element-wise math, and unpacking Q, K and V with a view instead of a transpose. None of them changes what the model computes; each removes copies and lets XLA fuse more of the graph. Together they brought the step to 2.78 s (8.6% improvement), 4.1x faster than the starting point and about 99% of the throughput of the JAX-based MaxDiffusion on the same hardware.

We also scaled FLUX from 8 to 1,024 v7x chips, keeping three images per TPU. The best configuration went from 2.53 s per step (20.76% MFU) on 8 chips to 2.71 s (19.4% MFU) on 1,024, which is 93% weak-scaling efficiency. Past one 64-chip cube, pure FSDP slows down due to the model’s compact size. Hybrid sharding via HSDP fixes this: it shards within each 4x4x4 cube and replicates across cubes. We used the topology-aware device mesh, which maps the parallelism optimally to the physical 8x8x16 torus.

Muse Glimmer 30B is a modern dense decoder: 52 layers, grouped-query attention with 32 query and 2 key-value heads, a 202k-token vocabulary with a soft-capped output head, and a layer pattern that mixes sliding-window and global attention, with RoPE skipped on every fourth layer. It is the case that shows how much works out of the box. The model definition, the parallelization and the training loop ran on TPU unchanged; the port came down to two model-specific pieces. Upstream implements the sliding-window pattern with FlexAttention, which has no TPU backend, so the first step was a windowed SDPA inner attention that reproduces the window exactly and gives a correctness baseline, checked against upstream on CPU. The second piece was the fused cross-entropy kernel, which we extended to apply the soft-cap to each vocabulary tile as it computes the loss, so the 202k-wide logits never have to be materialized; without that the LM head alone dominated memory. Everything else, including the Graph Trainer wrapper, was the standard onboarding ladder, with each feature behind a flag and its own test. The resulting recipe trains the 30B on 64 v7x chips (a 4x4x4 slice) at sequence length 16,384 with one sequence per device, Graph Trainer plus SimpleFSDP and fp32 gradient reductions, and reaches 5,848 tokens per second per chip at ~44% MFU.

Qwen3-235B-A22B is the largest MOE model in this set: 94 layers, 128 experts with 8 active per token, 235 billion parameters of which 22 billion are active per token. It is the case that exercises the large-model half of the playbook. At this depth compiling the whole unrolled step as one program is not an option: XLA plans every buffer of a program at compile time, and a 94-layer graph laid out flat asks for more HBM than the chip has before a step can run, while its compile time grows with every layer. Getting it to work at all meant enabling one of the two Graph Trainer paths for large models. Scan over layers is the XLA-native answer, the same construct JAX and MaxText rely on: the 94 identical layers become one compiled loop body, so compile time and program size stop growing with depth. The graph splitter is the more flexible alternative: it keeps the layers unrolled but cuts the captured step into per-layer executables, which works no matter what kind of layers you have (and does not require layers to be the same). We run both for this model and they land within a few percent of each other. Either way the activations do not fit in HBM at sequence length 4096 with 16 sequences per device, so both paths offload layer activations to host memory and prefetch them back for the backward pass.

From there the playbook applied, one change at a time, each measured on the same 64-chip slice. We raised the scoped VMEM budget so the compiler could keep more kernel intermediates on chip, which also removed an exposed update at the tail of the optimizer step. We enabled a set of libtpu scheduling flags that let XLA's latency-hiding scheduler overlap the FSDP all-gathers and reduce-scatters with the surrounding compute and offload the reduce-scatters to the SparseCores, so most of the collective time is hidden behind compute, leaving only a small exposed reduce-scatter tail in the backward pass. We switched attention to the tokamax splash kernel, and replaced the expert matmuls with specialized paired grouped-matmul kernels backed by a K-tiled weight-gradient kernel, with hierarchical reduce-scatter and the SparseCore latency-hiding scheduler to hide the expert-gradient collectives. A fused route-score backward removed a large fp32 copy from the MoE backward pass. The last round of fixes came from profiling at 1,024 chips: keeping the blocked activation layout under scan offload, raising the backward layer-scheduler threshold so the expert reduce-scatters overlap with compute again, and pipelining small reduce-scatters across the scan loop's back-edge. The recipe runs at ~32% MFU on 256 v7x chips with fp32 gradient reductions kept throughout.

We also used this model to test how far TorchTPU scales, from 64 chips up to 8,192-chip v7x pod scale, in both single-slice and multi-slice configurations. A TPU slice is a set of chips connected directly over the inter-chip interconnect (ICI); multislice lets one training job span several slices connected over the data-center network (DCN), which is how a job grows past the largest single slice. Within a slice we run FSDP over ICI, sharding parameters, gradients and optimizer state across the slice's chips; across slices the model is replicated and the gradients are all-reduced over DCN once per step, so the only traffic on the slower network is a single gradient reduction, which overlaps with the backward pass. That is hybrid sharded data parallelism (HSDP), and it is what the 2,048- to 8,192-chip runs use, with 1,024-chip slices as the sharding group. The framework behaved as expected throughout: per-chip throughput dropped by 8% going from 64 to 8,192 chips (4,568 to 4,208 tokens per second per chip), near-linear scaling across 128x more chips. One caveat: for a model of this size, data parallelism beyond about 1K chips is a scaling experiment rather than a training recipe. This is a weak-scaling run: the batch per device stays fixed as chips are added, so the global batch grows with the chip count and at 8k chips is larger than one would use to train this model. What the run shows is that the framework, the compiler and the collectives hold up at that scale, not that this is the configuration to train with.

The three case studies above are deep dives: one model at a time, with a parity test and a profile behind every change. A team arriving on TPU usually has a different problem. They have a model they already train, often a Hugging Face one, and quite likely it won’t be the model that TorchTitan ships for TPU. They may well want every last percent out of it eventually, but first they need an easy way to get it running at all. That is the problem the Transformers modeling backend solves: it builds the model from the Transformers library by its Hub id and runs it through TorchTitan's trainer and parallelism instead of its own. On TPU the backend has a TorchTPU lane with a single configuration that applies the first rungs of the playbook optimizations to any supported checkpoint, with no change to the model code: SimpleFSDP in place of FSDP2, per-block torch.compile, the splash attention kernel registered as an attention implementation with the transformers attention interface so the model's own attention calls land on it, the fused Pallas cross entropy fed the final hidden states and the head weight instead of materialized logits, and, for mixture-of-experts checkpoints, the TPU grouped-matmul experts substituted for the portable implementation. Most of the dense architectures the backend supports train this way unmodified; mixture-of-experts checkpoints run through the same path but without expert parallelism yet. None of this reaches the hand-tuned numbers above, but it is a working, reasonably efficient first run for a model that has no TPU recipe yet, and a way to find out whether it deserves one.

Sooner or later a model deserves more than a generic path: it is the one you will train for months, it has an architecture the backend cannot express, or the out-of-the-box number is not the one you need. Porting a model to a new accelerator used to be a specialist project measured in weeks, with the hard-won lessons living in one engineer's head. On TorchTitan TPU it is a repeatable procedure. The repository ships a model-onboarding skill for coding agents that captures how the case studies above were done and walks an engineer, or the agent itself, through the same ladder, with the tests, the acceptance bars and the traps built in.

The roadmap runs along five lines: production readiness, precision, hardware, parallelism and models. On production readiness, the recipes gain the operational tooling that long runs on Google Cloud expect: integration with the ML Goodput Measurement library, so a run reports how much of its chip time turns into preserved training progress and where the rest goes, and with Multi-Tier Checkpointing on GKE, which keeps checkpoints in local memory and in-cluster replicas with Cloud Storage as the durable tier, so large jobs save often and resume quickly. On precision, the float8 recipe that today ships for Llama 3 70B, built on torchao's Float8Linear and v7x's native fp8 matmuls, extends to the rest of the model set. On hardware, Google has announced its eighth-generation TPUs, TPU 8t for pre-training and TPU 8i for inference and reinforcement learning, and TorchTitan will support them as they become available on Google Cloud, with the same recipes carried forward. On parallelism, expert parallelism for the MoE kernels is the open item named earlier, and long-context training is next after it, now that million-token context windows are the norm for frontier models. On models, the targets are the next size up from Qwen3-235B: Qwen3.8-Max, a 2.4-trillion-parameter mixture of experts, and Kimi K3, 2.8 trillion parameters with Kimi Delta Attention in place of standard attention on most layers, which will exercise the kernel plug-in path as much as the scaling one.

TorchTitan on TPU is no longer an experiment. The same codebase and plain-PyTorch model definitions train Llama 3 405B at about 57% MFU on up to 1,024 chips, and run Qwen3 235B, a mixture-of-experts model, on up to 1,024 chips in a single slice. In a weak-scaling test with a fixed per-chip batch, Qwen3 235B kept 92% of its per-chip throughput from 64 to 8,192 chips. That shows the framework, compiler and collectives hold up at pod scale, even though a model of this size would not be trained at that scale. The pieces that make this possible are not buried in one recipe. SimpleFSDP, the Graph Trainer with scan and the graph splitter, host offload, the Pallas kernel library and the optimization playbook are building blocks, each behind a flag and each documented with the number it is worth. For models that TorchTitan does not ship, the Transformers modeling backend runs 500+ Hugging Face models through the same trainer with the first optimizations already applied, and for teams that want more than that, the onboarding skill turns porting a model into a repeatable procedure that an engineer or an agent can follow.

That leaves two ways to use it. The first is to adopt TorchTitan as the training framework: pick a recipe, launch it on a slice, and build on a configuration whose performance is already known. The second is to keep the training code you have and take what you need: the kernels are standard PyTorch custom ops, SimpleFSDP and the Graph Trainer are modules you can import, the compiler flags and the playbook apply to any TorchTPU workload, and every recipe spells out what it enables and why. Either way, the fastest way to find out what this does for your model is to run it.

#10476. One UI 9.5 might bring Pixel 11’s ‘Rambler’ feature to Samsung Keyboard before Google’s

The Pixel 11 launched with one AI tool most people got on board with, called “Rambler.” It’s a transcription tool that overhauls and cleans up voice-to-text messages. Too bad this Rambler clone will only be available on Samsung Keyboard.

The Rambler-like keyboard feature was found in an internal test build of Samsung’s One UI 9.5, thanks to SamMobile. The settings include a feature named “Refine Voice Input,” and the description removes any doubt that it might be something other than a Rambler clone.

Clean up filler words, hesitations, and more. Text appears after you finish speaking.

Much like the Pixel 11’s Rambler tool, the final product is only available after you’re done dictating. The audio is then processed and turned into something coherent, devoid of the onomatopoeic words we often say when gathering our thoughts in a voice-to-text message. Unlike Rambler, though, it sounds like Samsung’s tool will automatically process your message instead of requiring an end action, like tapping the checkmark.

The biggest downside to this Rambler variant is that it falls under Samsung Keyboard’s feature set, not Google Keyboard. Even though One UI 9.5 is largely based on Android 17, Samsung still offers its own keyboard as the default experience.

This isn’t the first Google Keyboard feature Samsung Keyboard has emulated. Magic Cue was a massive addition to Samsung Galaxy devices, in the form of Now Nudge. It works wonderfully if you happen to like using Samsung Keyboard.

Google still hasn’t made Rambler available for any other Pixel devices besides the Pixel 11, and it’s the same story for Google Keyboard builds on Galaxy devices. Workarounds exist, but they aren’t official and shouldn’t be treated as such.

Nvidia's Board of Directors is preparing to oust co-founder and chief executive Jensen Huang following revelations of two extramarital affairs, including one with a woman suspected of working for Chinese intelligence and another with an Nvidia executive, reports The Wall Street Journal. Although no final decision has been made yet, the company's directors could meet as early as November 11 to discuss Huang's future. Whoever succeeds Huang will be the company's second CEO in 33 years. Nvidia's stock dropped 8% in after-hours trading following the reports. Nvidia's board of directors launched an independent investigation into the matter on November 8.

The report from the WSJ comes hours after the New York Times revealed that Huang allegedly had an extramarital relationship with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of health care, who reports directly to him. According to five people who worked at Nvidia, the relationship was known internally, while Huang and Powell were reportedly seen together at a San Jose hotel during Nvidia's GTC conference in March. The allegations follow an earlier report that Huang had a relationship with Alice Huang, a Chinese businesswoman whom U.S. intelligence believes to be a Chinese intelligence asset.

A similar controversy cost Intel chief executive Brian Krzanich his position in June 2018, when an internal investigation confirmed that he had maintained a consensual relationship with an employee, which violated the company's non-fraternization policy applicable to all managers. It is noteworthy that Nvidia's code of conduct explicitly identifies personal relationships as potential conflicts of interest and instructs employees to consult the company's legal department when such conflicts arise. Whether Huang violated Nvidia's internal policies remains unclear, as the code of conduct does not explicitly prohibit all relationships between managers and subordinates.

Huang's potential departure would mark the first CEO transition in Nvidia's 33-year history, and therefore his succession has become an immediate concern for Nvidia, which does not have anyone of Huang's caliber in the leadership team. Although chief financial officer Colette Kress could potentially serve as interim chief executive if Huang is ousted while the BoD searches for a permanent replacement, it remains to be seen how Wall Street would react to this assignment.

Nvidia co-founder Chris Malachowsky, who used to be the company's senior vice president of engineering and operations and remains a member of the company's board and an Nvidia Fellow, could also play a role in the succession process, given his extensive knowledge of Nvidia's technology, corporate culture, and history. However, has not served in an executive role at Nvidia for at least a decade, so the chances that he will succeed Huang are low.

Back on Sunday, directors expressed confidence in Jensen Huang and his leadership team, a choice of words that did not exactly guarantee his continued tenure.

#10469. Nintendo Switch 2 emulation on Android already has The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake up and running

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake now runs surprisingly well on phones after just a week of additional emulator development.

Legend of Zelda fans all over the world have been marveling at the Ocarina of Time remake’s visuals and enhanced gameplay systems on the Nintendo Switch 2, but the Android emulator scene has been busy marveling over something just a little bit different. One Redditor got the latest Zelda game running on Banana Native, a free emulator that’s available right now on GitHub.

Ocarina of Time remake performance has now reached a major milestone into the 30 FPS territory with frame gen, which means the game is actually running at around 15 FPS, but then scaled and interpolated using AI frame generation to appear like 30 FPS. Banana Native isn’t a traditional emulator, as it doesn’t emulate hardware. Rather, it uses the game’s own ARM64 code to run on the phone’s CPU. The Switch 2 is powered by a mobile Nvidia chip, after all.

The user is running the Ocarina of Time remake on a Poco F7 Ultra, which sports a two-generation-old Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Elite (Gen 4) processor. The latest phones that are coming out, like the Redmagic 12 Pro, run a 6th-gen Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme chip, so performance could be a lot better on a newer phone. Apps like Banana Native require specific driver support for each phone to run, and as I pointed out in my Redmagic 12 Pro review, platforms like Droid Deck and Game Native don’t yet have proper drivers for the newest chips.

9to5Google’s Damien Wilde recently reviewed Droid Deck, another native-type emulator that lets you legally play Steam games you own on your phone by just signing into your Steam account. It works extremely well on lots of Android phones and helps showcase just how far Android emulation has come this year.

#10467. Google Pixel Tag Initial Review: The gold standard for Find Hub trackers, whatever that’s worth

Google’s Find Hub had a tumultuous launch for its performance, but also for a lack of clear direction in hardware. Over two years later, that’s finally arriving in the Google Pixel Tag which, even just a few days in, is the obvious gold standard in the race to build an AirTag for Android devices.

Google Pixel Tag is now available for purchase at the Google Store and Amazon.

There are plenty of really good Android Find Hub trackers on the market. Pebblebee and Chipolo have been long-standing examples of how to build good dual-network trackers that work on both Android and iOS, while Motorola’s Moto Tag series has delivered two Android-only trackers that deliver better performance and more focused features. But even with the Moto Tag, there’s a clear compromise. Features like the ability to ring your phone are tied to a separate app and, in turn, have some inherent clunkiness.

Pixel Tag is the device that finally brings it all together, and it’s brilliant so far.

I’ve only had the Pixel Tag for a week and, frankly, it’s impossible to properly review a tracker in that time. There are only so many ways to artificially test out how a tracker works, as most cases where you’ll find value in this sort of product can only occur in real life.

So, with that said, let’s focus on the fundamentals.

Google Pixel Tag pairs to your Android phone just like any other Find Hub tracker – though much faster compared to some others I’ve tested – but the differences are obvious towards the end of that process. The final screen shows options for setting ringer volume and an option to set up “Ring this phone.”

Those new features are what make the Pixel Tag so good, and so appealing compared to all of the competition. A quick double-tap on the Tag’s button rings whichever phone you connected it to, assuming it’s in range, and it works pretty reliably. You can also set up “left behind” alerts (now available on other Find Hub trackers too) for when the phone leaves the tracker behind – you can set up exclusion zones such as your home or work address.

One of my favorite examples of the added polish from Google is the ability to adjust the ringer volume on the page where you actually want to ring the tracker. In a crowded but quiet space where you know the tracker is close by, being able to quickly set the volume to low is a great addition. In my book, it’s the reason to buy Pixel Tag over any other Android tracker.

UWB, of course, only makes up-close finding all the better. I found this decently reliable on my Pixel 11 Pro XL, but it is a little bit frustrating that the directional cues vanish within about 10-12 feet. Fine for a backpack or something like that, potentially a problem if your Tag is attached to a set of keys.

On that note, my biggest hardware complaint is that Google took the Apple route of having no built-in mounting mechanism. The super thin and light hardware of Pixel Tag is wonderful, don’t get me wrong, but being forced to buy a separate accessory just to attach it to my bag is frustrating! One of Google’s partners, Bellroy, built one of these accessories and it’s simple enough – a silicone case with a rope. I wish I didn’t need it, but it works, and at least you get two of them in a pack for $25.

The hardware itself is pretty good across the board, really, with the battery door also being slightly easier to open compared to the Moto Tag. Inside is a standard CR2032 battery that Google says will last “over one year.” I’d personally still prefer a rechargeable battery with USB-C or wireless charging, but this is a good enough solution given the amount of time the battery lasts – hopefully Google’s claim lives up.

Rounding out some of the key aspects here, it’s worth saying that the built-in speaker is more than loud enough at its highest setting, but the value for me so far has really come in that the low setting is actually low enough to not be a nuisance. I do find it a little strange that there’s no visual indication of where the Tag’s button is, but the packaging and app both do a good enough job of letting you know.

I really just wish it would have a rechargeable battery, preferably via USB-C. It would have been the only tracker on the market to support UWB and be rechargeable.

I’m looking forward to seeing how Pixel Tag performs as the travel season spins back up, so stay tuned for a later long-term review of Google’s tracker in the coming months. Initial impressions, though, are solid. Sound off in the comments below if you have any questions around Pixel Tag, I’ll answer everything I can!

Google Pixel Tag is now available at the Google Store and Amazon.

#10466. How to Build an Accessible Customer Reviews Section with HTML and CSS

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Customer reviews are often displayed on business websites, but presenting them effectively requires more than adding a few star icons and customer quotes. Developers need to consider semantic HTML, responsive layouts, accessibility, and how review content fits into the overall user experience.

A well-built review section makes customer feedback easy to read without distracting visitors from the rest of the page. It should also work across different screen sizes and remain understandable to people using assistive technologies.

Let's look at how to build one using standard HTML and CSS.

The first step is to structure review content properly. Instead of placing everything inside generic

elements, use meaningful HTML elements to describe the content.

For example, a review can use

as its container and
for the customer's actual feedback.

This structure separates the review from its attribution and gives the section a clear heading. The rating also includes an accessible label so screen-reader users can understand what the stars represent.

For real websites, developers should display genuine customer feedback and avoid presenting invented testimonials as actual customer experiences.

A review section might look attractive on a desktop but become difficult to read on smaller screens. CSS Grid provides a straightforward way to create a flexible layout without relying on JavaScript.

The auto-fit and minmax() combination allows cards to adjust to the available space. Instead of defining a fixed number of columns for every device, the browser fits as many cards as the container can comfortably accommodate.

Developers working with more complex interfaces can explore SitePoint's guide to responsive web design for additional layout techniques.

A responsive layout should remain readable when viewed on a phone. Avoid fixed card widths, excessive padding, and text sizes that force users to zoom.

You can also adjust spacing at smaller viewport widths:

Test the section at several viewport sizes rather than relying on one desktop preview. Check that long customer names wrap correctly, cards do not overflow, and text remains comfortable to read.

Some review components use animated sliders, automatic scrolling, or hover effects. These features can create problems for visitors who prefer reduced motion or navigate with a keyboard.

A simple approach is to avoid unnecessary animation. If transitions are included, respect the visitor's system preference:

The MDN guide to using media queries for accessibility explains how developers can adapt interfaces to user preferences.

Also make sure interactive controls have visible keyboard focus styles. A carousel should provide usable previous and next controls, clear labels, and a way to pause automatic movement if it is used.

On a production website, reviews may come from a database, a content management system, or an external review service. Keeping the content separate from the presentation makes the component easier to maintain.

Businesses can also use an online reputation management platform to monitor customer feedback and understand how their brand is perceived across online channels.

For example, a developer could store each review with fields such as:

The frontend can then render the same component for every review. This avoids manually creating separate HTML for each customer and makes future design changes easier.

Developers should also validate incoming data, handle missing fields, and avoid displaying private customer information without permission.

Before publishing, check the component in different browsers and screen sizes. Test keyboard navigation, screen-reader labels, text wrapping, and color contrast. If reviews are loaded dynamically, check how the section behaves when there are no reviews or when the data request fails.

A useful review component does not need complicated animations or a large JavaScript library. Semantic HTML, flexible CSS Grid layouts, accessible labels, and reliable content handling provide a strong foundation.

Customer feedback can only be useful on a website when visitors can understand and navigate it easily. By treating reviews as a proper interface component rather than decorative content, developers can create sections that are maintainable, accessible, and consistent with the rest of the website.

#10465. Hobbyist turns $45 USB-C hub into a working Nintendo Switch 2 dock by patching its firmware

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The hub now tells the console it’s Nintendo’s own dock.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A hobbyist going by mkopec turned an approximately $45 Cable Matters USB-C hub into a working Nintendo Switch 2 dock, using only about 540 bytes of code to mod the firmware. That mod lets the hub masquerade as an official dock, as mkopec detailed in a blog post. The edited firmware replies to Nintendo’s vendor messages and returns the same identity as the official dock, and makes TV mode work, which was not the case with the hub’s stock firmware. A separate fix with minor firmware rewriting also corrects washed-out HDR over HDMI, a nice bonus.

The modder, who writes that “Nintendo went in a proprietary direction (as they so often do),” decided to modify the hub’s firmware to make a working travel dock, as Nintendo’s official dock can be bulky. Unlocking the additional functionality did not come with a noticeable cost, as the hub “still works normally with my PC and my phone.” Grabbing an off-the-shelf USB-C hub and turning it into something more is quite the trick.

The stock hub has no problem charging the console, but it shows no picture, even though it negotiates both power and DisplayPort Alt Mode. Capturing the official Switch 2 dock’s traffic with a USB Power Delivery (USB-PD) sniffer, the modder found that it advertises two Standard or Vendor IDs: one for DisplayPort and one for Nintendo’s own mode.

The console enters Nintendo’s mode first, then DisplayPort, and, once DisplayPort is configured, it sends Nintendo’s vendor messages. This is the exchange of proprietary messages for negotiation, although the company told us last year that it “does not have any intention to hinder or invalidate legal third-party dock compatibility.”

The modification only required adding about 540 bytes to a firmware of just 32 KiB. The Cable Matters 7-in-1 triple display hub, like most, is simply an amalgamation of separate, specialized chips, in this case: a VL103 as the USB-PD controller, a VL817 USB hub chip for the USB ports, an RTD2142 DisplayPort Multi-Stream Transport chip for the DisplayPort-to-HDMI function, and XT25F16B flash memory chips. The VL chips are from VIA Labs, the RTD2142 is from Realtek, and the stock hub reports VIA’s vendor ID, which is an identity that needed to be changed for this mod.

The modder first used the WITRN K2 inline USB-PD sniffer on the official dock. Then, it was a matter of dumping the VL103’s firmware with the vli plugin of fwupd, the Linux firmware update tool, before flashing the VL103 with the changes over USB and through the VL817.

Changes were made incrementally, with seven versions in all, starting with answers to Nintendo’s discovery, mode entry, and echo commands, then an update to power delivery to offer 15 V at 2.8 A like the dock, changing the reported vendor ID to Nintendo’s, masquerading entirely as the official dock and getting TV mode working, and finally a full sequence match that removed a pause of about 3 seconds.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The process appears to contradict claims two accessory makers made to The Verge that the dock check uses encryption with a dedicated chip, as, in this case, copied identity data and fixed replies were sufficient.

Getting TV mode to work would have already been an accomplishment worth celebrating, but the modder took it further: the picture over HDMI was washed out with HDR turned on, and it was simply a matter of narrowing down the cause. The end theory was a mistake in how the hub’s AVI InfoFrame signaled BT.709, the standard dynamic range color space, instead of HDR’s wider BT.2020.

The theory was confirmed by a 108-byte patch to the RTD2142’s firmware, flashed with flashrom over I2C carried on the DisplayPort AUX channel through the same USB-C cable, which marks the signal as BT.2020 whenever HDR is in use. One third-party dock’s firmware contains a debug string, “switch2 color space patch,” which suggests this is a known issue. HDR through the hub’s own DisplayPort port is still washed out.

Separately, the hub’s firmware has no variable refresh rate (VRR) support, but because the patched hub identifies as the official dock, the console now offers VRR. However, turning VRR on froze the DisplayPort picture, and HDMI remained at 60 Hz. In a separate Linux test, with the hub patched to advertise Adaptive-Sync and an EDID override in place, the screen went black as soon as the frame rate varied, so the feature was left off.

The modder’s post shares no code, patch files, or repository, although the method is well-explained. The USB ports and Ethernet weren't tested rigorously, and the post does not say which Switch 2 system version was used. The modder also doesn’t understand many of Nintendo’s commands, so the hub’s version replies are copies of the official dock’s, and unrecognized commands still get the hub’s stock reply.

That’s important because Nintendo’s system update 21.0.0 left some third-party docks charging but with no video output. While vendors provided updated firmware as a fix, the modder’s ad hoc solution has no vendor behind it, so would-be modders could end up on their own.

#10463. Pixel Tag tidbits: Surprise NFC, Find Hub setup, & Bellroy keychain cases

The Made by Google 2026 lineup is finally complete with tomorrow’s retail launch of the Pixel Tag. You can find our review here, while there are some additional details about the device since the August announcement.

The Pixel Tag is the first Find Hub tracker with NFC. Google did not mention this connectivity specification in August, with Near Field Communication letting you tap the tracker to the back of your phone to immediately open device settings. The NFC area on the Pixel Tag is below the circular battery door, which you hold down and twist counterclockwise to open.

On Android 17 QPR2, tapping immediately opens the details page. On QPR1, you first get a system notification that you need to click. NFC joins Bluetooth 6.0 with Channel Sounding and Ultra Wideband (UWB).

The setup process adds a new screen after the initial pairing and terms of use. It lets you configure: Notify when left behind, Ring this phone, Edit name, Edit category, and Adjust volume for this tag.

The Pixel Tag has a physical button on the rear side below the battery cover. Clicking twice plays a loud sound on your phone if both devices are nearby. Once found, you can tap the button once to silence, or turn it off from your phone. This feature can be disabled from the Pixel Tag’s device details page on a tracker-by-tracker basis.

Google does not have a first-party case for the Pixel Tag. Instead, it’s working with Made for Google partner Bellroy.

There are two models available from the Google Store, with the first made of silicone. The Bellroy Lite Case Duo costs $24.99 and comes in a pack of two: Mist and Shadow colorways. It wraps everything but the face of the Pixel Tag (and its speaker cutout), while giving you a hole in the corner and color-matching cord.

There’s also the Bellroy Leather Tag Case in Artichoke, Azalea, Black, Pewter, and Red Ochre for $29.99.

#10461. Save serious money with RedPocket’s Black Friday plans that start at $5 a month

You’re not going to believe this, but there’s one part of your life that’s not actually getting more expensive. If you switch to RedPocket Mobile during its Black Friday sale, you can ditch your costly carrier and get a plan that starts at $5 per month. It’s not too good to be true: RedPocket has been around for 20 years, and unlike most MVNOs, it’s independently owned and operated. That gives it the ability to offer you real network choice when you sign up for a new plan (we’ll explain more later), and keeps your costs low. Really low.

But you’re reading this on 9to5Google, so how does it impact you as an Android user? That’s a great question.

RedPocket is offering $150 rebates on Android devices like the Pixel 11, Galaxy S26, Galaxy Z Flip 7, Moto G Play, and more. (You can also get $100 rebates for iPhones if you’re into that sort of thing.)

These offers run from November 9 through the 30th at redpocket.com, so don’t wait too long to save big.

It’s OK if you haven’t heard of RedPocket Mobile, because it has been quietly working for 20 years to improve the way people use their phones. Founded in 2006, the company works with all three major US carriers — AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon — and because it doesn’t own any of its towers, it can pass along significant savings to its customers. Here’s what the company says on its website:

RedPocket was built for price-conscious consumers who want real value and freedom from rigid, high-cost contracts. As a no-contract carrier, we’ve stayed focused on making reliable, transparent, and flexible phone service for everyone. Our plans are designed to fit real lifestyles, whether you’re a heavy data user, a frequent traveler, or just looking to cut your monthly bill.

Most MVNOs are owned by the Big Three, which means you don’t really have a choice of network when you sign up. But RedPocket works with all three major carriers and lets you choose which one works best for your location and usage patterns.

RedPocket offers a tool called CoverageGenius that helps you decide which network is right for you. While you can’t switch between networks once your line is set up, it’s a great way to figure out where to start.

I teased that $5/month cost at the beginning of the article, but here’s the real breakdown: you can get a full year of service for $60 if you’re a light data user. RedPocket’s Annual Essentials and Annual Plus plans get you unlimited calling and texting in the US, along with calling and data roaming in dozens of countries around the world. The main difference is how much data you get: 3GB for Essentials and 20GB for Plus.

Annual Premium ups the ante to unlimited data, plus calling and data roaming in even more countries.

Across all three plans, you’re saving between 33% and 50% off the regular price when you sign up between November 9th and 30th.

One thing to note about all of these plans: the annual prices are paid upfront, not billed monthly. Promotional pricing applies to the first year, and after that, annual plans renew at $110 for Essentials, $220 for Plus, and $330 for Premium.

RedPocket understands that it may be hard to commit to a full year of service even if it’s super cheap. That’s why it offers monthly plans, too, that can serve as a trial for any of the networks. It costs just $10 for the first month to try any of the three plans, with Monthly Plus rising to $20/month after that and Premium $30/month thereafter (Essentials stays at $10/month).

If you need a new phone to go along with your new plan, RedPocket is a great place to get it.

#10458. Google Fi brings back network switching with ‘secondary’ partner, expands W+ beyond Pixel

Google Fi Wireless is rolling out a big update today with the return of network switching thanks to a “secondary” partner.

Google Fi is now bringing back a second partner as part of its new “super network.” Most users will be on the “Primary network,” but Google Fi will automatically switch users to the “Secondary network” if it meaningfully improves performance with so-called “smart thresholds” for doing so. At launch, there is no manual switching.

Google is not naming the second network today, but did share that Ultra Wideband is supported. Officially, you are switching between “two of America’s fastest 5G networks.”

Network switching will first be supported on Pixel 9 and newer devices that have a Google Fi eSIM (and no other SIM active). It makes use of Android’s Multiple Enabled Profiles (MEP) eSIM technology that allows for two carrier profiles on a single chip.

It might be important to note that T-Mobile actually bought out Sprint, and added their towers to their network so its safe to say that Google Fi still works with the same coverage as before the merger.

The Google Fi app gains a “Your super network” card in the Home tab that shows whether you’re connected to the Primary or Secondary network. “See activity” lets you view switching history and when it last occurred. Google will also note if you’re in range of W+ networks.

Google today is also expanding its Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ network beyond Pixel phones to select Samsung Galaxy (S23, Fold/Flip 5, and newer) and modern Motorola phones. These premium Wi-Fi networks are available in millions of airports, malls, stores, and restaurants. The connection happens automatically, with the Google Fi Virtual Carrier Network encrypting data.

The final update today sees the companion app gain “Ask Fi.” This AI-powered chat is meant to quickly provide answers and guided troubleshooting for common issues. You can request human support with seamless handoff that keeps the chat context.

These Google Fi updates start rolling out today, with network switching seeing gradual availability. This launch is accompanied by several deals:
