#10457. Powering the Agent Development Lifecycle with any framework, from ADK to LangChain and beyond

End to end walkthrough of the new Agents CLI Extension System, with a worked LangChain example and a custom deploy policy

When we launched Agents CLI at Google Cloud Next '26, we built it around one strong opinion: going from an idea to a production agent on Google Cloud should take a single unified command surface. For teams building with Agent Development Kit (ADK), that opinionated path has worked. You run agents-cli create, write the agent logic, and ship to Cloud Run with observability and evals wired up, in about thirty minutes.

The agent framework landscape has grown more diverse in the months since. In addition to ADK, developers today pick between LangChain and LangGraph, specialized newcomers like Deep Agents, or in-house frameworks tailored to a specific business domain. Different teams prefer different frameworks, and the right choice for a greenfield prototype is rarely the right choice for a production migration. Enterprise admins may also have governance policies they want to enforce across the ADLC for all of their development teams, such as: a compliance scan, an SSO gate, a Model Risk Management hop.

Can a team benefit from the deployment, evaluation, and observability work in Agents CLI without being forced to adopt ADK, or without being forced to accept the default behavior of every command? Starting with version 1.5.0, the answer is yes. Agents CLI now ships with an Extension System that lets developers override or add commands without forking the tool. Version 1.8.0 ships the first-party LangChain extension, which replaces the framework-coupled commands with their LangChain equivalents and leaves the rest of the lifecycle untouched.

We will walk through two journeys below. First, to show you the ADLC with LangChain agents, we'll show you how you can scaffold, run, deploy, and evaluate a LangChain agent through the Agents CLI lifecycle using the first-party extension. Then we build our own small extension that wraps the deploy command with a Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) scan, demonstrating the pattern enterprise platform teams can use to enforce their own policies across every project. Both journeys use the same mechanism.

Most agent development toolchains available today share a limitation: they are opinionated about one specific framework, and the surrounding capabilities (deployment targets, CI/CD pipelines, evaluation harnesses, observability wiring) assume the developer has adopted that framework. When your framework matches the tool's assumption, the opinionated path works. When it does not, you're left with three bad options.

The first is to fork the CLI, maintaining a divergent copy and re-syncing it on every upstream release. You pay a permanent maintenance tax, and every upstream improvement has to be hand-merged into the fork. The second is to wrap the CLI behind custom scripts, building a parallel command surface that invokes the underlying tool. The problem is that developers (and their coding assistants) forget about the wrapper and call the underlying tool directly, bypassing the intended abstraction. The third is to walk away and build everything from scratch, which can consume several engineer-months of work that the team had not planned for, and which rarely produces tooling as polished as the upstream project.

The problem shows up in two scenarios. One is enterprise customers adopting Agents CLI: in regulated industries, a central platform team often requires that every deploy invocation be wrapped with a compliance check, that login route through corporate single sign-on, or that evaluations flow through a Model Risk Management pipeline. Each of these requirements, implemented today, means either forking the CLI or building a wrapper layer. The other is teams that use ADK most of the time but need to run a non-ADK framework on a specific project. Within the Agents CLI command surface, six commands assume the ADK runtime: playground, run, and four eval subcommands. The other eighteen or so (scaffold, infra, deploy, publish, login, and friends) are framework-agnostic. Without extensions, choosing a non-ADK framework for a single project meant losing access to all twenty-four.

The Extension System handles both with one mechanism.

An Agents CLI extension is a directory in a Git repository that contains a single file called agents-cli-extension.yaml. The file declares which built-in commands the extension overrides and which new commands it adds. From the official documentation:

Extensions take two shapes, described in the Using Extensions page. A command override replaces the behavior of a built-in like playground, deploy, or eval generate. The overridden command receives the user's arguments verbatim, so the user experience stays consistent with the native command. A new command exposes a workflow the CLI does not currently provide as agents-cli . Both shapes can coexist in the same extension, and most non-trivial extensions use a combination of the two.

Extensions are distributed through Git repositories rather than through a dedicated package registry. The design draws from the extension model used by the gemini-cli project. There is no central index to publish to, no package format to maintain, and no ownership roster to apply for. Any directory inside any Git repository can serve as an extension, and Git handles the versioning, pinning, and distribution. For enterprise platform teams, this means creating an internal platform-extensions repository and pointing their developers at it, with no external registry to coordinate with.

An extension can be referenced in several ways, depending on where it lives and how the developer wants to use it:

The # syntax lets a single Git repository hold multiple extensions, with the fragment identifier selecting one of them. Enterprise platform repositories often bundle together a family of related extensions (one for deploy, one for login, one for scaffold create) under a single repo with shared ownership and release cadence.

Extensions install at two scopes. The default is the project scope, where the extension and its lockfile entry are committed to the project's own repository. Teammates and CI pipelines pick it up on checkout. The alternative is the user scope, enabled via the --global flag, where the extension is stored in the user's home configuration directory and applies to every project on the machine. The user scope is the one you reach for when an extension needs to be available before any project exists, such as a framework bootstrap extension that configures agents-cli create itself.

When both scopes define the same command, the project scope wins, and agents-cli info surfaces the source of each active command. Scoping is deliberately explicit: the Extension System will not silently merge or override extensions, and any conflict surfaces to the developer at install time or in the extension list output.

The Extension System follows the discipline of package managers like uv or npm: it resolves a Git reference to an exact commit SHA at install time, records the resolved SHA in a lockfile, and uses the lockfile to re-materialize the exact reviewed code on every fresh checkout. The agents-cli install command restores the vendored working copy from the pinned SHA without advancing the pin, which guarantees that CI and local development run bit-for-bit identical code. To advance a pin to the latest commit on the tracked reference, the developer runs agents-cli extension update, which re-resolves the reference and re-prompts for third-party trust. The complete lifecycle is in the Using Extensions → Lifecycle section.

The Extension System is marked experimental in v1.8.0. The manifest schema and the command surface may still change in breaking ways before the system is finalized, so teams depending on it should pin their Agents CLI version.

The LangChain first-party extension is a project template: one command scaffolds a working LangChain agent wired into the Agents CLI lifecycle. The resulting project has zero google-adk dependency and deploys to Cloud Run through the same code path an ADK project uses. The template's own description reads:

In modern LangChain (version 1.0 and later), the state management layer is provided by LangGraph, so the three frameworks share a common graph representation. The template serves all three communities with the same code.

A Google Cloud project with billing enabled is also required. If you do not have one yet, it can be created at console.cloud.google.com.

If Agents CLI is not already installed on the development machine, it can be installed in a single command:

Finally, we authenticate with Google Cloud and enable the APIs the agent will depend on at runtime:

Scaffolding and extension installation are the same operation. Rather than installing an extension first and then scaffolding a project, we point agents-cli create directly at the extension's template, pinned to a specific release tag:

The output confirms the scaffold succeeded:

The scaffolded project already contains an agents-cli-extension.yaml at its root, which is auto-loaded at project scope the next time any command is invoked. There is nothing to install manually, because the project itself is the extension's installation. A developer scaffolding a LangChain project does not need to know the Extension System exists. The command looks and feels like a native agents-cli create invocation that happens to produce a LangChain project instead of an ADK one.

The structure looks similar to an ADK project: same app/, same tests/, same Terraform under deployment/, same manifest at the root. The two differences are the agents-cli-extension.yaml at the top (which carries the LangChain overrides) and the langgraph.json file (which configures the LangGraph dev server used by the local playground).

The main agent definition lives in app/agent.py:

The agent is created with the langchain.agents.create_agent helper, which compiles into a LangGraph CompiledStateGraph. The type annotation on root_agent makes this explicit. Modern LangChain uses LangGraph as its state management layer, which is why the template is positioned as supporting LangChain agents, LangGraph graphs, and Deep Agents through the same lifecycle: all three compile to the same underlying graph.

The scaffolded project contains no dependency on the Agent Development Kit. We can verify this directly:

The grep returns no matches, and the exit code of 1 confirms the absence of ADK references. The google-* packages that do appear in pyproject.toml are the ones any Google Cloud Python project would include: google-cloud-aiplatform[evaluation], google-cloud-logging, langchain-google-genai, and the OpenTelemetry Google Cloud exporters.

Behind the scenes, Gemini-3.8-flash on Vertex AI has invoked the compiled graph in-process, called get_weather("San Francisco"), received the simulated foggy response, and reasoned the final sentence back to the user. The warning line tells us which extension commands are active, with one entry per overridden command. The warning is deliberate: an override quietly changes native behavior, so the loader always announces which commands are being intercepted and by whom.

For a local inspector, agents-cli playground boots LangGraph Studio on port 8080, replacing the ADK web UI that would otherwise appear.

To confirm which commands the extension has modified, we can inspect the help output:

The [↑ langchain] suffix is the Extension System's way of signalling that a command has been overridden, together with the name of the extension responsible. Both human developers and coding assistants reading the terminal can tell at a glance which commands behave differently in this project.

For a complete inventory, agents-cli info lists every active extension together with the exact command vector it executes:

Six overrides total, falling into three categories: four framework-coupled replacements (playground, run, eval.generate, and publish.gemini-enterprise), two explicit refusals (eval.dataset and eval.optimize need an ADK agent object to introspect, so the LangChain project refuses them cleanly rather than producing invalid results), and the publish.gemini-enterprise override additionally refuses only on the Agent Runtime deployment target while delegating to the native command on Cloud Run and GKE. The First-party frameworks page has the official breakdown.

The deploy command is not overridden by the LangChain extension. Deploying a LangChain project to Cloud Run uses exactly the same code path as deploying an ADK project:

The underlying mechanism shells out to gcloud run deploy --source . against the project's Dockerfile, which includes:

That CMD is identical to the one in an ADK project's Dockerfile. The LangChain template serves A2A (Agent-to-Agent) protocol traffic over FastAPI, exposing the application under app.fast_api_app:app. The deployment path does not need to know which framework is powering the agent, because uvicorn and gcloud run deploy operate on the FastAPI contract.

The same holds for infra, scaffold enhance, scaffold upgrade, install, info, setup, lint, and login. All unchanged in a LangChain project. A year of Agents CLI work on these commands lands in LangChain projects for free, with no divergence to maintain between the ADK and LangChain code paths.

Evaluations in a LangChain project follow the familiar pattern from the ADK walkthrough, with one difference: the eval generate step is overridden to drive the LangChain graph, while the subsequent evaluation steps use the native commands:

The overridden eval generate command produces output in the standard EvaluationDataset format, which the native eval grade, eval compare, and eval analyze commands know how to consume. The evaluation service and the grading rubrics are framework-agnostic.

If a developer invokes one of the refused eval subcommands, the extension responds with a clear explanation rather than failing silently:

A clear refusal is more useful than a silent failure, especially when a coding assistant is driving the terminal and would otherwise retry a broken command multiple times before giving up.

The LangChain template is the first-party demonstration of the Extension System, but the same mechanism is open to any team that wants to customize the Agents CLI command surface. Enterprise platform teams can author and distribute extensions internally, encoding their policies as code and shipping them through the Git infrastructure they already use for everything else. Every developer on the team then follows the same checks by default.

To illustrate the pattern, we will build a small extension called sbom-deploy that wraps the deploy command with a Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) scan, refusing to deploy if any critical vulnerabilities are found. The authoritative reference for the manifest schema is the Authoring Extensions page.

We begin by creating a directory for our extension and adding the manifest file at its root:

The manifest contains five top-level keys, and no others are allowed. The schema uses strict validation, so a typo like reqires: fails install with a did-you-mean suggestion rather than silently dropping the override.

The schema field identifies the manifest schema version. The name field is a single path component (slashes and parent-directory references are rejected) and becomes the directory under extensions/ where the vendored copy is stored. The requires block declares which versions of Agents CLI the extension supports, together with a policy (warn or error) that controls behavior on a version mismatch. The commands block is where the actual overrides and additions live.

The Rules that matter section of the authoring docs enumerates additional constraints worth knowing about. Two in particular: you can override a specific subcommand (such as eval.generate) but not a top-level command group (such as eval as a whole), and run: is a command vector, not a shell command, so any chaining of commands has to happen inside a wrapper script rather than inside the manifest.

Because run: is a command vector executed without a shell, chaining multiple operations means writing a wrapper script that sequences them explicitly:

AGENTS_CLI_DISABLE_OVERRIDES=1 is what makes this work. Agents CLI sets it in the environment of any script invoked through an override. When our wrapper calls agents-cli deploy recursively, the call lands in the native deploy command rather than looping back to our own override. No special syntax, no recursion.

We can verify this directly. For demonstration, we swap the SBOM scan for a few echo statements that confirm the environment and arguments:

The --project foo flag passed through to the wrapper verbatim, and the environment variable was set by the loader before our script began executing. The wrapper has everything it needs to delegate to the native command.

With the manifest and wrapper in place, we can install the extension. During development, point at the local path:

Once the extension has been pushed to a Git repository, the same syntax works with a remote reference:

After installation, we verify the extension is active by listing the installed extensions and inspecting the help output:

The [↑ sbom-deploy] tag next to each overridden command is the Extension System's user-facing signal that the behavior has been modified.

Behind the scenes, extension add resolved our Git reference to an exact commit SHA and recorded the resolution in a lockfile at the project root:

Both the lockfile and the vendored working copy under extensions/ should be committed. On a fresh checkout, agents-cli install re-materializes the exact reviewed code from the pinned SHA, the way uv sync or npm install restore pinned dependencies. The install command never advances the pin, so an extension cannot change its behavior between two deployments of the same commit.

To advance the pin to the latest commit on the tracked reference (branch, tag, or SHA), the developer runs agents-cli extension update sbom-deploy. To switch to a different tag or branch, re-run agents-cli extension add with a new --ref. The complete lifecycle is detailed in the Using Extensions → Lifecycle section.

Agents CLI began life as an opinionated path to shipping ADK agents on Google Cloud. With the Extension System in place, those opinions are now yours to override, replace, or augment.

Then explore the official documentation: the Extensions overview, Using extensions, Authoring extensions, and First-party extensions. The full source code of Agents CLI, including the first-party templates, is at github.com/google/agents-cli.

#10453. Build a Hardware Diagnostic Log with Vanilla JavaScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A device disconnects halfway through a test. The application reports a timeout, the firmware restarts, and the next attempt succeeds. Where should you start?

For developers working with connected devices, browser interfaces, or hardware prototypes, an application error is often only the final symptom. Replacing code without recording the conditions around a failure can leave you chasing the wrong layer.

This tutorial builds a small browser-based diagnostic log. It records measurements, preserves the test conditions, and exports evidence for another developer to review. You need basic HTML and JavaScript knowledge; no framework or connected instrument is required.

1. Define the question before collecting data Consider a hypothetical development board that disconnects when a peripheral starts. Several explanations remain possible: a firmware exception, a transport problem, or a power supply issue. Start with a testable question: “Does the measured supply voltage change when this peripheral starts?” That question tells you what to record and what to keep constant.

Use a known-good board of the same revision where available. Match the firmware, cable, supply, peripheral, and workload. A comparison loses value when several conditions change together.

Record the observation separately from the explanation:

The hypothesis remains provisional. A reading taken after the device restarts does not establish what happened during the failure.

2. Choose evidence that answers the question Application logs, firmware traces, and bench measurements answer different questions. A timeout establishes that an operation did not finish in time; it does not identify a failed component.

For a view of specialist equipment used in phone-board troubleshooting, DIYFIXTOOL’s overview of Chip-Level Diagnostic Tools discusses thermal imaging, BGA X-ray inspection, and inductance testing. Treat it as a vendor-authored introduction to equipment categories; use instrument documentation and board-specific references when interpreting measurements.

The log below deliberately leaves interpretation to the operator. Enter a numeric value, its unit, and the conditions under which it was measured. Do not turn an isolated reading into an automatic “faulty chip” verdict.

For physical measurements, follow the board and instrument documentation and work within your training. This tutorial covers the software record rather than a procedure for probing or repairing a powered device. 3. Build a small, accessible form

Save the following as index.html and serve it through your usual local development server. For example, if Python is installed, run python -m http. server 8000 in its directory, then open http://localhost:8000. 4. Preserve data without hiding failures

The application stores an array of records as JSON. On reload, it parses that JSON and checks the expected field types before rendering it. Parsing alone does not validate the structure of stored data.

SitePoint’s guide to looping through JSON responses provides useful background on working with objects and arrays after parsing JSON. Each table cell uses textContent, so a note containing HTML is displayed as text rather than inserted as markup. The MDN documentation for textContent explains this behavior.

Storage operations sit inside try...catch blocks because browser persistence can fail. The interface keeps new measurements in memory and offers an export when saving fails. It does not claim those measurements will survive a reload. For another practical vanilla JavaScript project, SitePoint’s **Markdown previewer tutorial **explores browser UI construction and persistence.

As **MDN’s localStorage documentation **explains, storage belongs to the document’s origin. Opening the application on a different port may therefore show a different log. This prototype is a single-browser tool, so export important sessions and avoid recording customer identifiers or private device contents.

5. Compare measurements under matching conditions Suppose two hypothetical readings are 3.29 V and 3.08 V. Their difference is −0.21 V. That arithmetic is straightforward; its meaning depends on the rail specification, measurement uncertainty, workload, and timing.

Do not add a universal voltage threshold to the application. A device-specific limit should come from its specification or an established test procedure.

If the failure is brief, a manually entered steady-state measurement may miss it entirely. Record that limitation. The next investigation may require a time-correlated capture rather than another static reading.

When sharing a session, include the firmware revision, board revision, instrument settings, and reproduction steps. A colleague should be able to repeat the comparison without guessing how the reading was obtained. 6. Verify the workflow before relying on it Try adding a decimal reading and a negative reading, then reload the page. Confirm that both values and their units remain intact. Enter a note containing test and check that it appears literally in the table.

Export the JSON and inspect the record count. Then simulate blocked storage and confirm that the interface reports the failure while allowing an export of the current session.

For a larger application, add editable entries, instrument metadata, and explicit baseline records. A shared service would also need access controls, server-side validation, and a policy for retention. These additions should follow the needs of the diagnostic workflow.

Turn a symptom into a reproducible investigation

The finished prototype captures a useful minimum: what was measured, where, when, and under which conditions. Its value comes from preserving evidence that another person can inspect.

When an application error crosses into firmware or hardware, that record helps you choose the next experiment. Repeat the failure, change one variable, and document what the new evidence supports before deciding what to fix.

The AI data center boom is bringing hope to struggling cannabis farmers as they face increased competition, costs, and taxes amid reduced demand for marijuana. According to Arizona Public Media, the cannabis industry is in decline, with sales peaking in 2024 and oversupply driving prices down. And now, several growers are considering selling their cultivation facilities or converting them into “mini data center nodes.” These facilities already have many of the ingredients needed for AI data centers, such as the increasingly hard-to-get supply of electricity and water infrastructure.

"For some reason, there's a lot more cannabis grown in Arizona than Arizonans use. And so, a lot of folks are getting out of that business, like myself, at least on the cultivation side. But these facilities can potentially be repurposed to be mini data center nodes," Moe Asnani, the former owner of two Tucson D2 Dispensaries, told the public broadcaster. He also added, "The whole idea is if you create these smaller nodes where people control their own data, where they're not at the mercy of other people's updates and things like that — that's kind of where the idea came in, because I believe in decentralization."

Cannabis farming is both power- and water-intensive. Forbes looked at a 32,850-square-foot cannabis facility in Kalamazoo, Michigan, which is currently wired for 1.5 megawatts, which can be expanded to 2.5 megawatts in under a year and 15 megawatts in the long term. It's also provisioned for fiber internet and equipped with a state-of-the-art HVAC system for climate control, which should make it a tempting property for anyone wanting to put up a small data center — something that even OpenAI and Anthropic are interested in as massive hyperscale sites face opposition from the community.

This concept is dubbed "adaptive reuse," with Tucson City Council member Paul Cunningham saying that it’s a more palatable alternative to massive, purpose-built sites sitting on the outskirts of town. "Data centers are dirty words, and everybody knows that. And no one wants to build a brand-new data center anywhere," the politician said. "But if we have buildings that were using that amount of water and that amount of energy already in their previous function and [it] can be done without being invasive, I'm OK with exploring that option."

There is massive demand for data centers, but it’s not a guaranteed payoff for struggling cash-strapped growers looking to unload their unprofitable properties. Niki Krear, vice president of acquisitions of real estate investment trust New Lake Capital Partners, said that there’s a sweet spot between connectivity, costs, and regulation that data center operators look for in a site.

"It’s a combination of finding affordable power, dense fiber networks, and favorable tax policies," Krear told MJBizDaily. Even though many cultivation facilities already boast higher-than-average power capacity for lighting and climate control, data centers often demand twice to thrice more, with upgrades possibly taking up to five years.

Data center operators looking for smaller sites may also have to contend with NIMBY — not in my backyard — fights from neighbors. Additionally, there may be zoning restrictions, moratoriums, and regulatory hurdles, and the lack of economies of scale could end up costing operators more.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

This is particularly important as AI chips are expected to increase in power and efficiency in the next five years. As these new AI GPUs become available, data centers will need to sell their existing equipment and buy new hardware to remain competitive. Because suppliers usually offer favorable pricing for larger orders, upgrading these smaller sites will likely end up being more expensive on a per-unit basis. That said, growing opposition toward large new projects could make these former cannabis farms suitable alternatives for operators desperate to get more compute online quickly, provided they check all (or most of) the boxes they need.

#10447. Wyze’s new door lock has speedy fingerprint recognition and 12-month battery life

Wyze is today launching its new “Lever Lock,” a smart lock for your front door that has a year of battery life and fast fingerprint recognition, all for an affordable price tag.

Available starting today, the Wyze Level Lock is not your typical deadbolt smart lock. Rather, this is a smart lock designed for your standard single-bore latch, meaning it’s available for doors that other smart locks aren’t compatible with. You can still have a deadbolt, but you’ll need enough space for both (per Wyze’s guidelines below).

The Lever Lock has a PIN entry and a fingerprint sensor, the latter of which Wyze says recognizes your fingerprint in about half a second. It uses local storage for biometric data and works to improve that data with each successful unlock.

The PIN has a clever feature (though it’s presented in somewhat poor taste) that lets you input random digits before or after your actual code to prevent onlookers from being able to see your code. There’s also a physical key backup as well as a USB-C input on the outside in the case that the battery dies – Wyze says it lasts about 12 months on a charge using eight AA batteries.

The Wyze Lever Lock is available today for $99.98 and shipping immediately.

#10443. Techie uses voltage glitching hack to turn $2,500 Nvidia 8GB cryptomining GPU into a full-fat $20,000 A100 with 80GB of VRAM

The most dangerous thing in the world is a bored engineer with a screwdriver. If you see a soldering iron, run for the hills.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Enterprising techies looking to build their own local AI cluster have far more interesting options than buying DGX Sparks or Apple Mac Studios. Used data center GPUs are everywhere, and a server rack's garbage is an enthusiast's treasure. This option has become even more popular with CMPunlocker, a soft-mod for Nvidia CMP-170HX cards that unlocks abilities well beyond stock. Now, a techie has taken it a step further, blasting through the remaining limitations with voltage glitching and creating a card faster than the actual $20,000 Nvidia A100 GPU with 80GB of VRAM... but from an $2,500 8GB CMP-170HX mining card.

The volt-mod was done by Kate (GitHub link here), who got her starting point from a paper that described how one could exploit GA100 cards' microcode. She turned her attention to the normally protected onboard fuse controller. Although it's rarely discussed, a "fuse box" has been integral in most any CPU and GPU for a while — though it's normally used once at the factory to break physical connections in chips to parts that have tested poorly, thus disabling them so they can be sold as a lower-end model.

If you're wondering how people are getting 80-40GB of VRAM out of 8-10GB, the explanation is simple: the GA100 silicon actually includes all the corresponding HBM2e VRAM dies within; but they get disabled at the factory like the shader modules. The smaller version is more desirable, as it's based on the variant of GA100 with 80GB of HBM2e within, while the 10GB cards spawn from the 40GB GA100.

The Nvidia cards in question have quite an elaborate eFuse controller (16,384 in a 512 x 32 array) — which can be read to obtain the card's current configuration. Most every part of the card has related fuses: VRAM sections, individual shader modules, PCIe 4.0 and NVLink, and even video codec hardware. However, as Kate succinctly put it: Nvidia's design is insecure, as popping fuses can grant access to more features, not fewer.

Under normal conditions, these fuses can't be popped, or "written to," by the user — but a geek armed with a soldering iron isn't a normal user. Kate had GPT-6 Astra design a simple circuit board based on an $8 STM32G4 microcontroller to tap into the power signals on the card's PCB.

The voltage regulators (VRMs) on the card "speak" PWMVID, an Nvidia standard, and Kate's board intercepted the PWM signals sent into the VRM controller, forcing it to produce voltage glitches on the line feeding the GPU, where the fuse controller lies.

Remember when we said the fuse controller was read-only? With the voltage glitches, many fuse-status reads in a tight loop, and she managed to set the controller to a state in which its security bit flags allowed writes through. From that point, it was "easy" to reconfigure her card. She was careful to force-disable any shader modules previously marked as defective, because these are physical fuses — there's no "undo" button.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

She applied the mod to her cards and, after testing with multiple workloads, found that nine had the full 80 GB of VRAM working, and one card "only" had 64 GB usable. Most interestingly, unlocking all the shader modules (SM) nets her FP16 FMA performance 20% beyond a regular A100 card. She thinks this is due to the chips often having 120 or more usable SMs — over the 108 on the spec sheet. The GA100 die starts with 128 SMs to account for defects, which is normal in chip manufacturing.

The CMPUnlocker soft mod is required for enabling the "new" features on the drivers, but it doesn't unlock the card's full potential: the VRAM upper limit is 40-64GB (depending on the variant), many shader modules remain locked, and PCIe bandwidth only goes to 2.0. Additionally, there's no ECC, NVLink, or multi-instance GPU support, all of which are critical in a server setup and are also used for product segmentation.

Kate's volt-mod does away with all of these limits, and her best test card is happily working at PCIe 4.0 speed with the full feature set. She didn't test the video encoder bits, but it's unlikely anybody using these cards cares much for that.

As for the cost, a CMP-170HX 8GB is currently hovering around $3,000, and the 10GB variant costs around $2,500. A full-fat Nvidia A100 80GB in a PCIe configuration can reach $20,000 or more. The SXM server-module versions are cheaper, but they require infrastructure around them. Note that these prices will likely vary significantly, especially as people become increasingly aware of the mods.

Kate says she's publishing her research now because another person independently found the same issue. She has informed Nvidia of the vulnerability, though there's not much the company can do for existing cards already on the market.

Pixel Watch Drop: Create My Widget, Phone-to-Watch Unlock, and Wear OS 7.1

#10438. Streaming comes to API Gateway: real-time and AI-ready APIs, without changing your spec

For most of the last decade, an API call looked like a transaction. A client sent a request, waited, and got a complete response back in one piece. That model is exactly what most API management tooling — including API Gateway — was built to handle, and it still describes a huge amount of production traffic.

But that no longer describes all traffic types. A model generating a response emits tokens as it produces them, not in one batch at the end. An agent working through a multi-step task reports progress as it goes. A dashboard, a collaborative editor, a live inference endpoint — these all need a connection that stays open and carries data in both directions.

Until now, developers building those experiences in API Gateway had to make an uncomfortable choice: put API Gateway in front of their APIs and give up streaming, or route streaming endpoints around the gateway and give up the authentication, API key enforcement, and observability that made the gateway essential in the first place. Teams ended up with a split estate — governed APIs on one path, ungoverned streaming traffic on another.

We're announcing that streaming support in API Gateway is available in Public Preview. Your streaming endpoints can now sit behind the same gateway, with the same policies, as everything else you serve.

For AI applications, streaming also strengthens API Gateway's connection to Gemini platform. A gateway that uses model routing now streams responses from Gemini, Claude and OpenAI gpt-oss models in model garden in Gemini platform as they're generated, and streaming backends you host anywhere else work the same way. Alongside Apigee and Agent Gateway in Gemini platform, API Gateway is the lightweight entry point to Google Cloud's AI gateway capabilities.

API Gateway now supports the full range of streaming patterns modern APIs rely on:

Streams can stay open for up to one hour. As with any long-lived connection, clients should implement reconnection logic so that a session survives the end of a stream — a well-behaved WebSocket or SSE client should be doing this anyway.

This is the part we are most pleased with: there is nothing new to learn.

Streaming is not a different product, a different resource type, or a different way of describing your API. There are no new extensions to add, no streaming-specific annotations, no separate spec for streaming operations. The same document that describes your existing API describes your streaming API.

Everything you already rely on continues to work exactly as before:

During Public Preview, you opt-in when creating the gateway:

That single flag is the entire difference. At GA, it is planned that streaming will simply be how API Gateway behaves, with no opt-in required and, as during Preview, no changes to your API config.

Alongside streaming, gateways get a deterministic default hostname:

Previously the default gateway.dev hostname included a generated hash, which meant you could not know your gateway's URL until after you had created it. That is a small thing that causes a lot of friction: you can't write the endpoint into a client config, a Terraform module, a CI pipeline, a firewall allowlist, or a partner integration doc until the resource exists — and if you tear the environment down and rebuild it, the URL changes underneath you.

Now the hostname is a pure function of values you already chose. You can generate it, template it, document it, and commit it to source control before the gateway exists, and you get the same URL every time you rebuild the same gateway in the same project and region. For anyone managing environments as code, or promoting a configuration from dev to staging to prod, this removes an entire category of bootstrapping problem.

Streaming isn't a niche feature for AI applications — it's the expected interaction pattern.

Users expect to see a model's response appear as it's generated, not after a thirty-second pause. Agents need to report intermediate steps. Tool-calling protocols and remote MCP servers depend on long-lived, incrementally-delivered connections. An API management layer that can't stream severely limits the user experience of AI applications.

With streaming, API Gateway becomes a viable front door for that agentic traffic — and the value of a front door is exactly the same as it's always been, only more urgent. AI endpoints are expensive, they consume quota, they need per-consumer authentication and rate limiting, and they need to be observable. These are the governance problems that an AI gateway is intended to solve. The alternative — every team standing up its own proxy in front of its own model endpoint — is how organizations end up unable to answer basic questions about who is calling what.

Model routing in API Gateway lets you expose a single OpenAI-compatible endpoint and dispatch requests to Gemini, Claude, or OpenAI gpt-oss models based on the model field in the request payload. Model routing plus streaming means clients get a familiar, standards-based streaming interface, while you retain centralized control over which models are reachable, by whom, and under what policy.

Streaming, model routing and MCP support are all features within API Gateway that provide a lightweight on-ramp to AI gateway capabilities within Google Cloud. These AI gateway capabilities allow teams to monitor, secure and control agentic traffic of tools, models and agents in their ecosystem, and they're delivered as a composable suite so you can choose the right gateway for each use case. For more expansive AI policies and full lifecycle management, use Apigee. To manage and secure agent connectivity, use Agent Gateway in Gemini platform. You can also combine these gateways: API Gateway or Apigee can sit in front of your agents and APIs, or behind Agent Gateway so your agents can reach models, tools, and MCP servers. For example, you can route your agent's egress through Agent Gateway for strict security governance, and then pass the request to API Gateway to handle model routing.

A few things to plan around while the feature is in Preview:

If you're already using API Gateway, the fastest way to see this working is to point a new streaming-enabled gateway at an existing backend that emits SSE and watch the events arrive incrementally. No spec changes needed.

A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

#10436. Google Workspace vs. Microsoft 365 vs. Proton Workspace: Which Is Best for Developers and Freelancers?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Think about what's sitting in your inbox and Drive right now. Client contracts and invoices. Project specs under NDA. Staging credentials a client emailed you. That API key or password you meant to delete.

For most developers and freelancers, all of it lives in Google Workspace or Microsoft 365. Both are excellent tools, and both encrypt your data in transit and at rest. But in their standard setup, the provider holds the encryption keys, which means your provider (and the integrated apps and AI features connected to it) can access what's inside.

Proton Workspace takes a different approach: the same everyday tools, with encryption designed so that not even Proton can read your data.

Here's how the three compare, what each does best, and which makes the most sense for how you work.

Google and Microsoft are built around ecosystem integration. Proton is built around keeping your data yours, without sacrificing productivity tools.

Google Workspace is the go-to suite for startups, agencies and freelancers, and for good reason. Gmail, Drive, Docs, Sheets, Calendar and Meet work together seamlessly, and several people can edit the same file in real time without emailing versions back and forth.

Google encrypts data at rest and in transit, and offers client-side encryption on eligible editions. But that extra layer requires higher-tier plans and configuration, so in the default experience Google holds the keys. A password manager and VPN aren't part of the suite.

Best for: Teams whose top priorities are real-time collaboration and third-party integrations.

If your work runs on Word, Excel and Outlook, Microsoft 365 needs little introduction. Business Standard includes full desktop apps, and Excel remains the benchmark for heavy spreadsheet work.

That breadth comes with complexity. Licensing, permissions and device management can eat into time a small team or solo developer would rather spend shipping. And like Google, Microsoft's everyday productivity experience isn't built around end-to-end encryption, while Copilot and advanced security features add cost.

Best for: Businesses built on desktop Office apps and established Microsoft workflows.

Everything is managed from one admin console, with one bill.

This is the core difference. Proton uses zero-access encryption: your data is encrypted before it reaches Proton's servers, and Proton never holds the keys. Proton can't read your data. Not won't. Can't.

The honest trade-offs: Proton Docs and Sheets cover core editing and collaboration, not every advanced Excel feature. There's no presentation app yet, and the third-party integration ecosystem is smaller than Google's or Microsoft's.

US prices for comparable plans with annual billing, excluding taxes:

The headline prices are close. The real cost isn't:

Proton Workspace covers business email, 1 TB of encrypted storage, docs, sheets, video calls, a password manager and a VPN. That's one bill instead of three.

Need more? Workspace Premium and adds 3 TB of storage per user, 20 custom domains, meetings for up to 250 participants, Lumo private AI and email data retention policies for only $7 more per month. Both plans include a 14-day free trial, and annual plans are currently up to 20% off.

Choose Google Workspace if your team lives in real-time collaboration and relies heavily on Google's integration ecosystem.

Choose Microsoft 365 if your work depends on desktop Excel, PowerPoint and established Microsoft workflows.

Less than you might expect. Proton's Easy Switch tool imports email, contacts and calendars from Google Workspace and other providers, and you keep your custom domain, so clients won't notice a thing. Smaller teams can migrate on their own, and Proton's business team supports larger moves.

Moving from Microsoft 365? Check the migration options for each data type rather than assuming every workflow transfers automatically.

Is Proton Workspace a good alternative to Google Workspace for developers?

Can Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 read my email and files?

By default, yes. Google Workspace and Microsoft 365 hold the encryption keys, and their default terms give them access to your data.

No. Zero-access encryption means Proton never holds the keys to your stored data. Emails between Proton users are end-to-end encrypted automatically, and you can send password-protected encrypted emails to anyone.

Yes. Proton's apps are open source and independently audited.

Can I use Proton Mail with my existing email client?

Yes. Proton Mail Bridge connects Outlook, Apple Mail and Thunderbird.

Does Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 use my data to train AI?

It can. If your plan allows Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 to hold the encryption keys, they can access your data and use it to train their integrated AI models.

No. Proton can't read your data, so it can't use it for AI training.

Google Workspace and Microsoft 365 are mature, capable platforms, and if your work depends on advanced Excel or a deep web of Google integrations, there's a good case for using them.

But if you're a freelancer or a developer or running a small team, Proton Workspace is the stronger choice. You get the same core tools for less money, a password manager and VPN you'd otherwise pay for separately, and encryption that keeps client data, code and credentials readable only by you.

You don't have to take that on faith, either. The code is open source, and the trial is free.

#10434. Google reworks TPU silicon into specialized systems for agents and security

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Google has yet to share specs, prices or ship dates for either new system.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Google's eighth-generation TPU family has now expanded further. Back in April, the company announced TPU 8t for training AI models and TPU 8i for general-purpose inference workloads. Google has now introduced TPU 8tx, built on the same silicon as the previously announced 8t, for confidential, hardware-isolated inference aimed at regulated enterprises and governments, and TPU 8i Max, which combines two 8i chips on one package for long-context agentic work.

TPU 8tx swaps the Arm-based Axion hosts of the 8t and 8i for a 5th Gen AMD EPYC CPU, adopting a Boardfly network, and the 8i Max doubles the compute, SRAM, and HBM bandwidth of the original 8i. The original split, the TPU 8t and 8i, made natural sense. The 8t targets large-scale training, while the 8i leans toward low-latency inference and reasoning. TPUs have become a fast-growing business, with the company claiming nearly 100 customers, including Meta’s recent move to TPUs. The more modular approach is flexible and can deliver specialized systems “in a matter of months rather than years,” helping that popularity along.

Secure, confidential inference; small-scale training

For the new parts, the TPU 8i Max isn't innovative on the surface, as it’s just two TPU 8i chips combined into a single package. The result is twice the theoretical compute, but more importantly, it fits twice the silicon within the “existing rack footprint,” and Google claims more compute per square foot and per watt.

Doubling both SRAM and HBM bandwidth helps keep things scaling, which would put the 8i Max at about 20.2 FP4 PFLOPS, 768 MB SRAM, and about 17.2 TB/s HBM bandwidth. This compares to Nvidia’s Rubin R200 GPU at a rated 50 FP4 PFLOPS for inference and 22 TB/s of HBM4 per socket, or AMD’s MI455X with 40 FP4 PFLOPS. The compute gap is closing.

The memory bandwidth doubling is also potentially one way around moving to HBM4. The memory wall, as Google calls it, or the bandwidth bottleneck, is a real concern “when dozens of AI agents work together across an entire software codebase.” Larger context windows — with larger models targeting 1 million tokens — mean larger KV caches, partly held in SRAM, and each new token has to read that cache back from memory, which is where the extra HBM bandwidth helps. It’s a different route from Nvidia’s LPX: more bandwidth in one package, rather than a second chip type for decoding and a storage tier for context.

Google also provides useful software for agents to get the most out of its hardware. TPU Sync is an open-source “model-state layer for TPU clusters” that moves KV caches off the chip’s HBM, “automatically tiering massive KV caches off the chip in milliseconds” down to host DRAM and, experimentally, shared storage. Google says this can cut KV transfer latency by “more than 90%,” with time-to-first-token gains that vary by workload. The company has also announced the Agent Compute Engine, a Google Cloud service that pauses idle agent sessions and moves their working memory aside until they’re needed again, another memory-saving effort.

Google credits its vLLM-TorchTPU stack with “state-of-the-art price-performance marks” on InferenceX and AgentX, which test realistic agentic coding and long-context work. However, the only published third-party numbers so far are for the last-generation Ironwood TPU. In SemiAnalysis’s InferenceX tests in September, running Qwen3.5 397B in FP8, Ironwood delivered 50.4% more tokens per dollar than Nvidia’s B200 and 96.0% more than the B300 at 20 tokens per second per user. One issue with that, however, is that Ironwood lacks native FP4, although newer November results show its FP8 beating Nvidia’s FP4 GB300 NVL72 on the same model, at 1.67 times the performance per dollar. There is also no eighth-gen result yet to compare how the new generation differs.

In contrast to the 8i Max, which is designed for long-context work, the new TPU 8tx, “built on the same silicon as TPU 8t,” is “optimized for secure and confidential inference” as well as small-scale training. The security is built into the hardware with isolation to prevent snooping on sensitive data, including model weights. Normally, data has to be unlocked in memory during processing, which is the step this isolation is meant to cover.

This chip is intended for areas where security and confidentiality are high priorities, which means regulated enterprises and governments, particularly those who are using frontier or proprietary models. In that case, the operators may want to “fine-tune models using their private data, distill large models into smaller ones, and train their agents,” without having to move sensitive data between clusters. Agents and security meet here for “secure agent execution.” Back in April, the Pentagon and Google reportedly held discussions for TPU usage in classified settings, although the 8tx chip is not specifically named.

Google’s Private AI Compute, its “hardened TPU platform” with protections “comparable to a typical TEE,” per its September technical brief, already runs confidential serving on TPUs. The 8tx appears to be that capability turned into a product for customers. Alternatives include Nvidia’s NVL72, with one security domain across 72 Rubin GPUs, 36 Vera CPUs, and the interconnects, and Intel’s TDX, which is adding trusted I/O to cover data moving to accelerators.

The TPU 8tx hosts are based on 5th Gen AMD EPYC boards, instead of the Axion hosts on the 8t, which replaced x86 in April. There's one Axion for every two TPUs on the 8i, compared with one Xeon per four on Ironwood. Google gives no reason for the change, but its Confidential VM configuration list has both AMD and Intel series chips, with no Arm-based ones listed. Private AI Compute uses “an AMD-based hardware Trusted Execution Environment” for its CPU workloads, suggesting Google swapped something it already runs on its confidential stack.

The 8tx also adopts the 8i’s Boardfly network over the 8t’s torus, which Google claims can deliver “up to 2x lower latency compared to TPU 8t” with almost no performance penalty, although it’s not clear what measure Google is using. The alternative torus network suits training, since chips mostly swap data with neighbors in a predictable manner, but inference on large mixture-of-experts and reasoning models can send smaller messages between distant chips, with every hop adding delay. With Boardfly, it’s at most seven hops against 16 at 1,024 chips.

What is lacking is a full specifications table, the amount of HBM capacity, pod sizes, an availability date, pricing, and customers for either system. Details on attestation, that is, the ability for the customer to verify confidentiality, are also missing for 8tx. The 8tx and 8i Max are currently listed, along with the 8i and 8t, as “coming soon” on Google’s Cloud TPU page.

Delivering two new TPU systems nearly seven months after April’s pair shows Google wants to turn out specialized systems much more quickly than has been the case in the past. Now, it just needs to prove them, through benchmarks and an availability date when the hardware is actually in customers’ hands.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

#10427. I’ve waited a long time for the Boox Picco, and I’m not disappointed

At around $100, the Boox Picco offers one of the most portable e-reader experiences you can get. You add MagSafe-like capability with a refreshingly simple UI, and you have a delightful way to read, literally wherever you are.

There is an argument to be made for every single shape and size e-readers come in, and they come in so many different sizes it’s hard to keep track. I love the idea of having a 10-inch display that I can skim, but even if it’s perfect for sitting at home and reading anything from novels to comics, I don’t think it provides a very good travel experience

But even smaller devices like the Boox Palma make you choose between bringing a second device or sticking with your phone. Most often, the phone wins out – especially when you have a flexible approach with a foldable like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 I’ve been using daily.

Unlike the other phone-sized e-readers from Boox, the Picco stands at just 4.15 inches tall. The screen measures at just under that – 3.97 inches. It’s essentially the size of a MagSafe or Qi2 wallet – just small enough to fit on the back of your phone with the included magnetic case.

It’s thin, too. A 5.5mm side profile means it slips anywhere a minimal wallet would without issue. That goes for pockets just as well as crossbody bags; perfect for travel.

And that’s where I think I find so much more joy in using this over what would otherwise be considered a small e-reader, like the Palma. I don’t have a lot of excuses not to carry it around when I’m on the go. I’ll throw it in my Bellroy bag when I’m carrying what I’d considered my minimal EDC. It slides into any internal pocket, and it weighs next to nothing.

The back of the Boox Picco isn’t magnetic, but the included case is. If you want the thinnest possible footprint, forego the case. But adding it lets you do one of the coolest – and slightly copium-inducing – things with your phone: attach the e-ink device directly to your OLED-laden phone.

It does not work well with an unconventional device like my Galaxy Z Fold 8, but if you have nearly any other device like the Pixel 11, you should get a rather perfect fit. The Picco squares up to the back of any slab phone with Qi2 magnets, whether internal or via a case.

But, why? Why slap another device and add weight/thickness to your phone when it’s perfectly capable of displaying e-books?

Because it’s cool! That’s the driving reason. You can make the argument that it doubles your phone’s usability or that it gives you an easy place to store the tiny e-reader. The truth is that it does add extra thickness to your phone, and using your phone with the Picco piggybacking along can be uncomfortable, since your fingers now rest on another display and not a case or phone backing.

You can get used to it, and, as unnecessary as it may seem, it’s a fun way to carry around a much more eye-friendly screen.

The Picco diverts from other Boox devices in another way. It doesn’t run Android, which means you have no access to third-party apps like Google Play Books, Amazon Kindle, or any other store/library. Instead, the Picco uses a companion app to transfer EPUBs from your phone to the tiny e-reader.

I initially opposed this idea when I got a sneak peek during IFA in Berlin this year. But after using it for a few weeks, I’ll admit I’ve come around. But that all comes down to how you consume your media.

I try to source EPUBs no matter what device I use. On Android-powered e-readers, that means downloading Google Play Books and reading from there; I have my EPUBs uploaded to my own Google Play Books library, so they’re available universally.

On the Picco, my approach hasn’t changed much. I take those same EPUBs I have in storage and transfer them from the companion app.

I can understand how the lack of third-party libraries would make the Boox Picco moot. Still, I’ve been a proponent of buying digital books I can store in my own drives, so I can read them wherever, whether that’s the Google Play Books library or from a device like the Picco.

All of that is a roundabout way of saying the Picco, though it doesn’t let you use apps to read, makes it easy to get digital copies loaded and ready to flip through.

It uses a handshake connection that requires you to open the companion app on your phone, hit connect, and do the same on the Picco. Once connected, transferring EPUB files is as easy as selecting them from your phone’s physical or cloud storage and queueing them up. The process takes seconds, and the only issue I’ve had was solved by a force stop and trying again.

The homescreen is simply a carousel of your library, with a delightful little progress bar under each title as they’re sorted. You can access and manage the bookshelf from that page, as well as the minimal “apps” and settings. I appreciate the focus app and reading stats, but the countdown and to-do options require using the companion app. I don’t see the point of those.

It doesn’t even have a control panel. Swiping down from the top surfaces screen brightness and color controls. Even though this little guy is just over $100, it still has a front light controlled by a physical switch on the side. Compare that to e-readers at much higher price points without that game-changing feature.

The buttons opposite don’t control volume. Instead, they’re there for page turning.

It’s just simple and well-designed. Features are there to make reading a better experience, down to granular text controls for your digital books. It does exactly what it needs to do – nothing more, nothing less.

As far as battery goes, it meets my expectations. It’s not massive, so the battery is small in comparison to a smartphone. It still holds its own easily, with an easy week of use if you’re reading for an hour or two a day. Beyond that, it takes little time to charge and does great on idle, which is perfect for such a passive device.

The Picco will hit home for you if you want an ultra-portable option for your e-books. The same goes for those who don’t mind the smaller screen and lack of Android or third-party app support.

I get genuine joy every time I pick it up to read where I left off, and I think that’s because it’s so simple and nothing about it feels bloated, even the companion app, which is also straight to the point.

I think it’s been a while since a device has offered that experience for me. Adding the form factor, optional magnetic attachment for your phone, and price tag just gets me to that conclusion sooner.

The Boox Picco is the most adorable e-reader I’ve used, and it’s $109 on Amazon.

The news was made available on an official Nintendo support page. That means you won’t be able to use your Google or Apple accounts to log in to your Nintendo account as of January 26. After January 26:

Instead of using 3rd-party account linking, Nintendo is recommending that users register a passkey for an exclusive, secure login experience through the Nintendo Switch app. If you forget to do this before January 26, you can always head to the Nintendo account page and run through the “forgot password” instructions. Either way, if you use the Nintendo Switch app or have a Nintendo account of any kind, you should change how you log in immediately.

#10416. Prompt Management in TypeScript: LangSmith vs Git vs 3 Open-Source Registries

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every prompt registry we tested can change a prompt in a running TypeScript app, roll it back, and give production and staging different versions. LangSmith's prompt hub, Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore all passed those three tests. The real differences show up elsewhere: which template syntax each one accepts, how many npm packages it pulls in, how long a change takes to reach the app, and what happens when the registry is down.

We tested all of this with one small app and one prompt, which has a condition and a loop. Git files served as the baseline. Every number below comes from a recorded run.

Most teams start with prompts in code: a template string, or a .txt file next to the code that uses it. That works until a product manager wants to change one sentence, and the change has to wait for a full release.

A prompt registry moves the text out of the deploy. The app fetches the prompt by name and by a label such as production. You change which version the label points to, and the app picks up the new text without a deploy.

LangSmith's prompt hub is the best-known registry, but it is not the only one. Several of the LangSmith alternatives also manage prompts, and three of them are open source and self-hostable: Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore. We covered how those tools handle tracing in Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent. This article covers prompts only.

Every tool got the same prompt, support-reply. Its template takes three variables: a customer name, a plan, and a list of help articles. It has one condition: a priority line appears only for premium customers. It also has one loop: one line per help article. In Jinja2 syntax, the template looks like this:

One help article contains the text Settings > Export. We included it on purpose, to catch any tool that HTML-escapes > into >.

Every tool then ran the same sequence from a TypeScript script:

Each tool used its own documented TypeScript SDK. The four registries passed all six steps. The rest of this article covers how each one got there, and what it cost.

Keeping prompts in the repository costs almost nothing. The template sits in prompts/support-reply.njk, and nunjucks, the JavaScript port of Jinja2, renders it:

The autoescape: false line matters. Our first version called nunjucks.renderString() directly, and the test failed. The default environment HTML-escapes its output, so Settings > Export reached the model as Settings > Export. Escaping is correct for web pages and wrong for prompts.

Git gives you full Jinja2, code review on every prompt change, and history in the same place as the code. What it can't do is change a prompt in a running app. Every edit, and every rollback, waits for a deploy.

LangSmith stores a prompt as a serialized LangChain object (ChatPromptTemplate), not as plain text. LangSmith's own documentation says you must use the LangChain npm package to pull prompts in TypeScript (Manage prompts programmatically). So a plain TypeScript app that uses the openai package still needs langchain and @langchain/core:

Those three packages installed 23 packages and 70 MB. Most of the size is @langchain/* (27 MB) and js-tiktoken (22 MB).

Templates. In TypeScript, LangChain accepts f-string and mustache templates. Mustache sections ({{#docs}}…{{/docs}}) handled our loop. Mustache has no ==, though, so the premium check became an is_premium boolean computed in code. We also tried templateFormat: "jinja2", which LangChain's Python package supports. In TypeScript it threw DEFAULT_PARSER_MAPPING[templateFormat] is not a function.

Labels and rollback. production and staging are reserved tags, and the documentation moves them in the UI. The langsmith JavaScript client has no public method that moves a tag. Our script called the same REST route the SDK uses internally (POST /api/v1/repos/-//tags). It worked, but it is not a documented API.

Caching. The SDK caches prompts for 300 seconds by default. In our test, a running app saw the new version 300 seconds after we moved the tag.

Langfuse's TypeScript client is one package, @langfuse/client. It installed 5 packages and 8 MB:

Creating a version, moving a label and reading by label are all public SDK calls: prompt.create, prompt.update and prompt.get (Get started with prompt management).

Templates. The documentation shows only {{variable}} substitution. But compile() calls the mustache library, so sections worked for our loop. The type signature accepts only string values, so passing a list needed a cast. The Langfuse UI colours the section tags red, and it lists title and body as the prompt's variables, not docs or is_premium. We would not depend on behaviour the documentation doesn't promise. When we sent the Jinja2 version instead, Langfuse returned the {% if %} and {% for %} tags unchanged, with no error. The model would have received them as text.

Caching. The cache time is 60 seconds by default (Caching). Our app saw the change after 62 seconds. Langfuse is also the only client with a fallback option: a prompt string the SDK uses when it cannot reach the server on a cold start.

Phoenix's TypeScript client fetches a prompt by name and tag, and toSDK() converts it into the message format the openai package expects. Phoenix templates only replace variables, so the condition and the loop have to run in your code before rendering:

That is 16 lines, against 6 to 8 for the other registries. All the extra lines are template logic that now lives in the app.

Templates. Phoenix offers MUSTACHE, F_STRING and NONE formats (Prompts reference). Even in MUSTACHE mode, though, rendering is a plain replacement of {{name}}. When we sent mustache sections, they came back unchanged, with no error.

Errors. getPrompt() catches every error, logs it and returns null. You can see this in the client source, src/utils/getPromptBySelector.ts. A missing prompt and an unreachable server look the same to your code, which is why the snippet checks for null.

Size and caching. The client installed 57 packages and 90 MB. Most of that is OpenTelemetry, which comes in through its @arizeai/phoenix-otel dependency. The client has no prompt cache, so every render is a network call. That is also why a label change showed up immediately: 0 seconds in our test.

AcruxCore stores Jinja2 templates and renders them on the server with nunjucks. The SDK sends the variables and gets back finished messages:

The template from the test section works as it is, condition and loop included, with no is_premium flag computed in code. A label is called an alias here, and promoteAlias() moves it (Manage prompts via the SDK). The SDK installed 3 packages and 12 MB. Most of the 12 MB is one OpenTelemetry package, @opentelemetry/semantic-conventions.

Templates. Jinja2 only. When we committed the mustache version, AcruxCore rejected it at commit time with Template parse error: unexpected token: #. That is a clear error, but it also means that moving mustache prompts from LangSmith or Langfuse requires rewriting them.

We sent the same two templates, a Jinja2 one and a mustache one, to each tool's own renderer:

The rows that matter most are the ones where the tags were left in the output, because those failures produce no error. The model receives raw template syntax, and the only symptom is a strange answer. Whatever tool you pick, test one prompt with logic in it before you move the rest.

Putting logic in the template is not automatically better. It lets someone change the condition without a deploy. It also lets them break the condition without a deploy. Keeping logic in code, as Phoenix forces you to do, puts every branch under code review and in your tests.

A registry is a new runtime dependency, so we tested an outage. The app reached each registry through a local proxy, and we switched the proxy off. A separate process then started while the registry was still unreachable.

LangSmith, Langfuse and AcruxCore keep serving the last good version while the registry is down, even after the cache time has passed. The risk is a cold start: a new container or a serverless function that boots during the outage. Langfuse's fallback option is the built-in answer to that. With the other tools, keep a copy of each prompt in your code as a fallback, which brings back part of the Git setup.

Keep prompts in Git if engineers make every prompt change and your deploys are quick. Set autoescape: false, and you get Jinja2, reviews and history for free.

Use LangSmith's prompt hub if you already use LangChain, or already trace with LangSmith. If your app doesn't use LangChain, budget for the extra dependency and for the tag moves that only the UI supports.

Use Langfuse if you want a small client, a local compile(), and a fallback for cold starts. Keep to {{variable}} substitution unless you are comfortable relying on mustache behaviour that the documentation doesn't promise.

Use Phoenix if you already use it for tracing, or if you prefer all template logic in code. Remember the null check, and that every render is a network call.

Use AcruxCore if you want real conditions and loops in the template and rendering on the server. Expect one network call per new set of variables, and a personal API key for writes.

Before you choose, run one of your own prompts with real logic in it through the tool's SDK, not through its playground. Two of the four registries we tested failed on template syntax without any error, and an SDK run is where you would see that.

According to Bloomberg, it’s unclear when the suspected Chinese spy arrived in the capital or why she’s in the area, but she was last spotted leaving Singapore for Shanghai in 2025 after she was detained and questioned during the city-state’s investigation into the $2-billion smuggling case involving Megaspeed.

The Nvidia CEO is now under intense scrutiny after the revelation of his indiscretion with the Megaspeed executive, with Congress threatening to issue a subpoena if he does not respond to their invitation to “a joint, closed-door briefing” with the heads of the House committees on China, Foreign Affairs, and Intelligence. The FBI is also looking into the relationship between the head of the largest AI hardware company in the world and a person suspected of helping sanctioned Chinese companies secure hundreds of thousands of banned H100 and H200 chips. Overnight, a second bombshell report claims that Jensen Huang was also involved in an extramarital affair with Nvidia's VP of health care, Kimberly Powell.

Although the Chinese embassy in Washington has denied any links to Alice Huang, calling it nothing but “groundless smears and slanders” against Beijing, there is speculation that she’s under the control of the Ministry of State Security (MSS), which is the primary agency that handles China’s foreign intelligence and counterintelligence operations. The U.S. intelligence community suggests that she may be passing on information to the MSS, but it remains unclear if she was specifically tasked by Chinese intelligence to target Jensen Huang or if they were only leveraging her relationship with the Nvidia chief to get insights into the U.S. AI industry.

A deeper investigation into Alice Huang, known by her Chinese name as Huang Le, revealed that she supposedly worked in media before jumping into banking and finance. The Wall Street Journal reports that it’s unclear when she met her husband, Kang Wei, but the couple ran a private equity and investment management firm called Shanghai Hexi in 2016. This allowed the couple to invest in companies across industries, including gaming, pharmaceuticals, and, eventually, data centers and cloud computing.

The couple is known for riding trends and isn’t a stranger to scandals. Their firm, Shanghai Hexi, invested in gaming companies in the 2010s, especially when mobile gaming started to boom across the world, while Chinese regulators fined Kang and the company in 2018 for disclosure failures and other violations while it was in the midst of a takeover battle for a corporation. Kang’s moves in the gaming industry are what allowed him to meet Jensen Huang, which eventually led to a close relationship between the couple and the Nvidia chief. Sources told the WSJ that Kang apparently accepted Alice and Jensen’s extra-marital relationship, even sometimes jokingly telling people that she was Jensen’s “personal assistant.”

It seems that the couple is used to leveraging their relationships to gain business advantage, and with investments in AI data centers expected to hit $32 trillion by 2050, Alice Huang’s “close” relationship with Jensen Huang put Shanghai Hexi in an excellent position to take advantage of the trend through Megaspeed and make big money. And with U.S. sanctions making it harder for competitors to acquire Nvidia chips, it appears that their access has turned into a gold mine for the company. But whether Alice Huang acted solely out of business interest with the supposed procurement of Nvidia chips or was leveraged by Chinese authorities to import these chips, the fact remains that $7.5 billion worth of banned chips reportedly made their way into China despite Washington’s efforts.
