#10410. Architecting Governed, Self-Evolving AI Agents on Google Cloud

As enterprise cloud platforms scale to serve millions of concurrent users, tightly coupled microservices introduce cascading failure modes that no static runbook or threshold alert can anticipate. During high-velocity events like a Black Friday flash sale, Site Reliability Engineers (SREs) face an unforgiving reality: diagnose noisy distributed telemetry in seconds, or suffer cascading outages costing millions of dollars per minute.

While generative AI copilots have accelerated code authoring and log summarization, AI agents struggle to reliably remediate live production outages. An on-call engineer battling connection pool starvation does not need an AI agent offering troubleshooting tips or unverified pull requests that might not fix the root cause; they need a governed, self-evolving AI system that can:

In this Google Cloud research reference architecture, codenamed Project Chrysalis, we demonstrate how three foundational research breakthroughs combine into a unified, production-grade architecture that can run on Gemini platform and the Agent Development Kit (ADK). Across simulated Black Friday cache-stampede outages, this governed architecture cut time-to-first-mitigation from 15 to 45 minutes down to 1.8 seconds, reduced HTTP 5xx error rates from 72.7% to 80.0% down to 0.0%, dropped P99 latency from 519.8 ms to 18.2 ms, and evaluated 400 counterfactual traffic policies in BigQuery in ~0.5 ms.

Crucially, this self-evolving architecture does not operate as an unconstrained "black box". To ensure the agent remains secure, reliable, aligned, and contained against rogue behavior, every autonomous action is cryptographically bounded by Google Cloud's defense-in-depth security stack: Model Armor to sanitize incoming telemetry against prompt injections and data leaks; Google Cloud Key Management Service (Cloud KMS) and Binary Authorization middleware to cryptographically sign synthesized patches and block unverified code from mounting; Open Policy Agent (OPA) Policy Controller to enforce strict SLA guardrails that prevent self-inflicted outages; and Dual-Custody Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) governance to require explicit SRE sign-off before any permanent changes are made to the agent hierarchy.

To see why static playbooks and advisory copilots fail under pressure, consider a classic distributed failure cascade:

Conventional AI agents are frozen at deployment with fixed system prompts and pre-registered tools. When a novel infrastructure bottleneck strikes, a self-evolving AI architecture extends those baseline capabilities by synthesizing situation-specific mitigation tools at runtime, scoring the telemetry impact of every intervention, and codifying verified guardrails for future incidents.

For true autonomous resilience, the agent itself must evolve based on empirical evidence of what worked and what failed:

Self-evolving cloud agents are an active area of applied research focused on resolving the SRE Trilemma (balancing exploration safety, structural adaptability, and multi-step credit assignment) by unifying three research pillars on Google Cloud:

1.Serverless Counterfactual Simulation (Dream-RSI):Challenge: Testing unvetted rate-limiting parameters in live production risks throttling legitimate revenue or re-triggering outages.Mechanism: Production request traces are exported to Google BigQuery, where the engine replays hundreds of candidate token-bucket configurations in sub-millisecond vectorized memory sweeps to identify Pareto-optimal policies offline.

2. Tri-Level Co-Evolution (APEX):Challenge: Single-layer prompt tuning provides fragile band-aids, while unconstrained code self-modification risks system instability.Mechanism: Agent evolution is separated into three governed timescales:

3. Step-Level Shapley Attribution (AgentEvolver):Challenge: During chaotic incidents, multiple interventions occur in sequence; coarse outcome rewards cannot isolate which step healed the service and which caused collateral damage.Mechanism: AgentEvolver computes marginal Shapley credit across each execution span using pre- and post-intervention telemetry deltas (P99 latency, HTTP 5xx rate, and database pool saturation), isolating beneficial patches from erroneous ones.

We implemented this architecture as an interactive application on Gemini platform. The diagram above illustrates the closed-loop workflow across the Production Target Service, Agent Development Kit (ADK) Multi-Agent Plane, Self-Evolution Engine, and Defense-in-Depth Governance Pipeline.

Each phase of the interactive demonstration showcases a distinct stage of the agent's self-evolution:

To deploy self-evolving agents safely in production, our architecture enforces an unbroken four-gate defense-in-depth chain of custody. Every synthesized patch, distilled memory, and topology update must clear four sequential verification boundaries:

Across automated evaluations of Black Friday cache-stampede workloads, the self-evolving architecture delivered decisive operational gains over both manual triage and static advisory copilots:

#10405. Noctua brings space-grade carbon nanotube thermal pad to AMD AM5 and AM4 processors

Austrian cooling outfit Noctua is officially rolling its new NT-CP1 AM5/4 Carbice carbon nanotube thermal pad for AMD AM4 and AM5 processors into the retail market. After announcing its partnership with advanced nanotechnology company Carbice back in June 2026, the premium thermal pad can now be purchased for $24.95 in the US and €24.95 in the European region.

Conventional thermal pastes and most thermal pads gradually lose performance over time due to pump-out, delamination, cracking, and other forms of degradation. However, Noctua says that its new NT-CP1 thermal pad takes a different approach. It makes use of Carbice's vertically-aligned carbon nanotube technology, originally developed for high thermal reliability in aerospace systems. The carbon nanotubes gradually conform to microscopic imperfections of a CPU’s IHS over hundreds to thousands of thermal cycles.

This allows heat transfer performance to improve during the initial activation period rather than gradually deteriorating. The thermal pad can also maintain stable, maintenance-free performance for years once the initial activation phase is complete. Thus, making it a suitable option for users who want consistent thermal performance without having to periodically replace degraded thermal paste.

"Carbice pads offer a unique combination of ultimate performance stability and convenient installation. This makes the NT-CP1 the perfect choice for everyone who’s looking for a thermal pad that’s super easy to apply and never needs to be replaced," said Noctua CEO Roland Mossig in a press note.

Unlike conventional carbon or graphite-based thermal pads, which can be brittle, slippery, and electrically conductive, the NT-CP1 features an aluminum backbone sandwiched between layers of carbon nanotubes. The surface is also protected by a nanoscale polymer coating, which Noctua says makes the pad more robust, easier to handle, and less likely to move during installation. The company recommends a simple peel, press, and peel installation process, while the slightly tacky coating helps keep the pad in place and offers electrical insulation as long as the polymer layer remains intact.

With an ultra-thin 90µm profile and precut 33x33mm size, the thermal pad fits both AM5 and AM4 socket processors. Noctua also highlights its manufacturing process, noting that the pad is produced in the US using recycled aluminum and waste carbon gas to grow the carbon nanotubes. The company claims that its manufacturing process is more energy-efficient compared to conventional graphite and graphene-based pads, while the aluminum structure allows the product to be recycled through standard aluminum recycling channels.

#10403. Technical Aspects of AI-Generated Video Assets for Web Design

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated videos are becoming an important part of modern web design. Designers can now create animated backgrounds, product demonstrations, hero section videos, and visual storytelling elements without relying entirely on traditional video production. However, generating a visually appealing video is only one part of the process. To use AI-generated videos effectively on a website, developers and designers must also understand video formats, visual quality, file optimization, and website performance.

These technical factors directly influence how videos appear across devices, how quickly pages load, and how smoothly visitors interact with a website.

Video format selection is one of the first technical decisions when preparing AI-generated assets for the web. Different formats offer different levels of compression, compatibility, and visual quality.

For most websites, MP4 with H.264 remains a dependable default. WebM or AV1 can be offered as additional formats where appropriate.

Developers should also consider browser compatibility before selecting a format. Providing multiple sources allows browsers to choose a supported option.

AI-generated videos can sometimes contain visual inconsistencies, unnatural movements, distorted objects, or changes in lighting between frames. These issues become more noticeable when videos are displayed in large website sections.

Resolution is an important factor in maintaining visual quality. Common resolutions include:

However, higher resolution does not automatically mean better website performance or user experience. A 4K video may look impressive, but loading it unnecessarily can increase bandwidth consumption.

Frame rate also affects the viewing experience. Many website videos work well at 24 or 30 frames per second. Higher frame rates may be useful for fast-moving scenes, but they also increase the amount of visual data that needs to be processed.

Designers should review AI-generated footage before publishing it. Checking transitions, motion consistency, sharpness, and color accuracy helps prevent distracting visual problems.

Large video files can significantly increase website loading times. This is especially important for websites with video backgrounds, animated landing pages, and multiple visual sections.

Video optimization involves reducing file size without making the footage noticeably worse.

Some practical optimization techniques include:

Compression: Video compression reduces unnecessary data while preserving important visual details. Developers can use encoding tools such as FFmpeg to adjust bitrate, resolution, and codec settings.

Bitrate control: Bitrate determines how much data is used to represent each second of video. A higher bitrate generally preserves more detail but increases file size. Selecting an appropriate bitrate depends on the video's resolution, movement, and intended display size.

Trimming unnecessary footage: Website videos rarely need to be several minutes long. Removing unused sections helps reduce file size and keeps visual content focused.

Removing unnecessary audio: Background videos that do not require sound should be exported without an audio track. This reduces unnecessary data and supports a cleaner user experience.

Responsive video delivery: Websites should serve appropriately sized assets for different screen sizes. A smaller video may be sufficient for mobile users, while desktop visitors can receive a higher-resolution version.

These techniques help developers balance visual quality with efficient content delivery.

Even a well-optimized video can affect website performance if it is implemented incorrectly. Developers should consider how videos are loaded, displayed, and processed by browsers.

For example, automatically loading several large videos when a page opens can consume significant bandwidth. Instead, developers can use lazy loading strategies for videos that appear further down the page.

For background videos, the HTML video element offers several useful attributes:

Developers should also avoid autoplaying large videos unnecessarily. A static image can often provide a better experience on mobile devices or slower connections.

Performance testing tools such as Google Lighthouse and Chrome DevTools can help identify whether video assets are affecting loading speed, bandwidth usage, or rendering performance.

Technical optimization should not come at the expense of accessibility. Background videos should not distract users or make website content difficult to understand.

Developers should respect the prefers-reduced-motion media query, which allows users who prefer reduced animation to receive a less motion-intensive experience.

A suitable static background image can be displayed as an alternative.

Mobile compatibility is equally important. Smaller screens, limited data plans, and varying network conditions can make heavy video assets less practical. Testing videos on different devices and connection speeds helps identify potential problems before publication.

AI-generated video assets offer exciting possibilities for modern web design, but their successful implementation requires more than creative output. Choosing suitable video formats, maintaining consistent visual quality, compressing files, and considering website performance are all essential parts of the development process.

By combining thoughtful video production with responsive delivery, accessibility considerations, and performance testing, designers and developers can create engaging websites without compromising loading speed or usability. As AI video technology continues to evolve, understanding these technical fundamentals will remain valuable for building efficient and visually appealing web experiences.
